                                            LONG TIME DERIVATION OF THE BOLTZMANN EQUATION FROM HARD
                                                                 SPHERE DYNAMICS


                                                                               YU DENG, ZAHER HANI, AND XIAO MA


                                                Abstract. We provide a rigorous derivation of Boltzmann’s kinetic equation from the hard-sphere system




arXiv:2408.07818v2 [math.AP] 14 Nov 2024
                                                for rarefied gas, which is valid for arbitrarily long time as long as the solution to the Boltzmann equation
                                                exists. This extends Lanford’s landmark theorem (1975) which justifies such derivation for sufficiently short
                                                time, and is a crucial step towards resolving Hilbert’s sixth problem.
                                                    The general strategy follows the paradigm introduced by the first two authors for the long-time derivation
                                                of the wave kinetic equation in wave turbulence theory. This is based on propagating a long-time cumulant
                                                ansatz, which keeps memory of the full collision history of the relevant particles. The heart of the matter
                                                is proving the smallness of these cumulants in L1 , which can be reduced to combinatorial properties for the
                                                associated Feynman diagrams, called collision history (CH) molecules. The latter is then proved by devising
                                                an elaborate cutting algorithm, which is a major novelty of this work.

                                              1. Introduction
   Dating back to the year 1872, Boltzmann’s kinetic theory is one of the most profound and revolutionary
scientific ideas ever introduced. At the heart of this theory lies Boltzmann’s kinetic equation which gives an
effective macroscopic description of the statistics of a large system of interacting microscopic particles. A
significant feature of this theory, which caused some controversy at the time of its inception, is the fact that
the macroscopic description is given by a time-irreversible equation (i.e. with an entropy functional that
increases forward in time), which is derived from a time-reversible microscopic system satisfying Newton’s
laws of motion. This is known as the emergence of the arrow of time.
   Unsurprisingly, this profound physical theory naturally prompted the deep mathematical question of
rigorously justifying the derivation of Boltzmann’s equation starting from Newton’s laws. This question was
            LONG TIME DERIVATION OF THE BOLTZMANN EQUATION FROM HARD SPHERE DYNAMICS                                        3


asked by Hilbert, in his address at the International Congress of Mathematicians in 1900, as the sixth problem 1
Hilbert proposed the question of giving a mathematically rigorous derivation for the macroscopic equations
of fluids, such as the Euler and Navier-Stokes equations, from the Newtonian laws governing microscopic
particle interactions, with the derivation of Boltzmann’s kinetic equation as a crucial intermediate step.
In other words, this problem requires justifying two limits: (i) the kinetic limit where we pass from the
(microscopic) Newtonian dynamics of an N -particle system to Boltzmann’s kinetic equation for the one-
particle density function in an appropriate limit N → ∞, and (ii) the hydrodynamic limit where we pass
from Boltzmann’s kinetic equation to the (macroscopic) equations of fluid motion in the limit where the
collision rate goes to infinity.
   Despite being the subject of extensive study in the last century, this problem remained open for more
than 120 years since it was first announced. While the second hydrodynamic limit is much better understood
(see discussions in Section 1.4.1 below), the main difficulty in the resolution of Hilbert’s sixth problem is the
justification of the first kinetic limit, which is the subject of this manuscript. In this direction, the state of
art can be briefly summarized as follows (see Section 1.2 for a more extensive review). A landmark result of
Lanford [41] in 1975 provides the rigorous derivation of the Boltzmann equation for sufficiently short time.
This result has since been completed, improved and further developed by different groups of authors (see for
example Illner-Pulvirenti [35], Gallagher-Saint Raymond-Texier [27] and more recent works [51, 8, 9, 11, 7]
concerning fluctuations). However, all these works are still restricted to perturbative regimes, either under
short-time or near-vacuum conditions or at the exact Maxwellian equilibrium.
    The main result of this paper fills this gap, and thus completes the justification of the first limit needed
for the resolution of Hilbert’s sixth problem. More precisely, we provide a rigorous derivation of Boltzmann’s
equation from hard sphere dynamics, for arbitrarily long time that covers the full lifespan of the solution
to the Boltzmann equation with any given initial data, which can also be arbitrarily large. In particular,
if a solution to the Boltzmann equation exists globally, then our derivation is valid for all finite times. We
plan to connect this to the second hydrodynamic limit in a follow-up work, thus giving a full resolution to
Hilbert’s sixth problem (cf. Section 1.4.1).
1.1. Set up and the main result. To state our result precisely, we start by describing the microscopic
dynamics of the interacting particles, the kinetic formalism involving s-particle density functions, and the
Boltzmann equation which gives the effective dynamics of those functions. The microscopic system here is
given by the standard hard sphere particle model with elastic collisions. We shall discuss some variants, like
the system with short range potentials, in Section 1.4.2.
1.1.1. The hard sphere dynamics. Consider N hard-sphere particles with diameter ε whose centers of mass
and velocities are denoted by xj and vj for 1 ≤ j ≤ N . Their dynamics are defined as follows.
Definition 1.1 (The hard-sphere dynamics [1]). We define below the dynamics for the N -particle hard
sphere system with diameter ε in dimension d ≥ 2. For distinction, this will be referred to as the Original
or O-dynamics when necessary.
    (1) State vectors zj and zN . We define zj = (xj , vj ) and zN = (zj )1≤j≤N , where xj and vj are the
        center of mass and velocity of particle j; this zj and zN are called the state vector of the j-th
        particle and the collection of all N particles, respectively.
    (2) The domain DN . We define the non-overlapping domain DN as

    We list some known properties of the hard sphere dynamics, which hold up to Lebesgue zero sets:

Proposition 1.2. Up to some closed Lebesgue zero subset of DN , the time evolution defined by the hard
sphere system (Definition 1.1 (3)) exists and is unique, and satisfies that
     (1) No two collisions happen at the same time t;
     (2) The total number of collisions has an absolute upper bound that depends only on d and N ;
     (3) The flow maps HN (t) defined in Definition 1.1 (4) are measure preserving diffeomorphisms from DN
         to itself, and satisfy the flow or semi-group property HN (t + s) = HN (t)HN (s) for t, s ≥ 0.

Proof. See Appendix A.                                                                                            □

   From now on we will often ignore Lebesgue zero sets like those in Proposition 1.2 (as they do not contribute
to Lebesgue integrals); for example we will not distinguish the set DN from its full measure subset in
Proposition 1.2 where properties (1)–(3) hold. Same for the modified dynamics in Sections 3.1–3.2.

particle correlation function f1 (t, z) = f1 (t, x, v) should solve the Boltzmann equation in the kinetic limit
ε → 0, which is defined as follows.

Definition 1.4 (The hard-sphere Boltzmann equation). We define the Cauchy problem for the Boltzmann
equation for hard-sphere collisions, with initial data n(0, z) = n0 (z) where z = (x, v) ∈ R2d , as follows:

Remark 1.5. We make a few comments on the scope of Theorem 1.
      (1) Assumptions on the Boltzmann solution: the Maxwellian decay of n(t, z) in v is standard for solutions
          to the Boltzmann equation, and in principle can be replaced by bounds in any space in which the
          Boltzmann equation is locally well-posed by fixed point arguments. On the other hand, the required
          decay in x in (1.15) for the initial data n0 is basically at the level of L1x , which is the minimal
          requirement to define and normalize the ensemble in (1.7)–(1.8).
      (2) The time interval [0, tfin ]: if the solution n(t, z) to the Boltzmann equation (1.13) develops singularity
          at some finite time tsing , then the tfin in Theorem 1 can be chosen as anything strictly less than tsing .
          If the solution exists and is uniformly bounded for all time t > 0, then the tfin in Theorem 1 can be
          chosen as a function of ε that diverges as ε → 0; the current proof would lead to tfin ∼ log | log ε|.
          We do not expect this to be optimal, and it is an interesting question whether this can be improved
          (say to tfin ∼ | log ε|).
          LONG TIME DERIVATION OF THE BOLTZMANN EQUATION FROM HARD SPHERE DYNAMICS                             7


    (3) Parameters and convergence rate: the restriction s ≤ | log ε| and the convergence rate εθ in (1.18)
        are not expected to be optimal, and are chosen primarily for convenience. The method of our proof
        would cover the case s ≤ ε−κ for sufficiently small κ > 0 depending on tfin ; on the other hand, if s is
        as large as ε−1 then (1.18) might not hold even for initial data, see [51]. Next, for s = 1, the optimal
        value of θ should be 1− and can be obtained by refining our proof; as for cumulants, their optimal
        decay rate is linked to the derivation of the fluctuation equations about the Boltzmann limit, which
        we will discuss in Section 1.4.3 below. Finally, the value of constant Kn−1 := E(N ) · εd−1 (inverse of
        the Knudsen number) occurring in the Boltzmann-Grad scaling law (1.12) can be replaced by any
        constant independent of ε (by replacing the ε−(d−1)N in (1.7)–(1.8) with (Kn−1 ε−(d−1) )N ), or (a
        suitable power of) log | log ε| as in point (2).
    (4) The grand canonical ensemble (Definition 1.3): we choose to work with the grand canonical ensemble,
        as opposed to the microcanonical ensemble where the particle number N is fixed, only to simplify
        some calculations with cumulants. This simplification was observed in several recent works on this
        subject involving cumulants [51, 8, 9, 7]. It is also possible to work with fixed number of particles
        N ≈ ε−(d−1) , and Theorem 1 remains true, but the proof would be significantly longer due to the
        extra manipulations needed to reduce the expressions of cumulants.
    (5) The torus Td case: We believe that the proof in this paper can be adapted, with minor modifications
        on a mere technical level, to cover the Td case where the x domain is a periodic box. In fact, the
        only nontrivial changes would occur in Section 13, where some different arguments are needed to
        bound the term fserr . We plan to address this in an upcoming work.

1.2. Background and history. We briefly discuss the background and history of the problem of rigorously
deriving the Boltzmann equation, and the closely related problem of deriving its wave counterpart, i.e. the
wave kinetic equation in wave turbulence theory.

1.2.1. Particle systems and the Boltzmann equation. The study of large particle systems (like in Definition
1.1 or in (1.33) with short range potential) has a long history, coinciding with the evolution of the theory
of statistical physics. The kinetic theory, which is lying at the heart of nonequilibrium statistical physics,
started with the following genius observation of Boltzmann [12, 13]: assuming the independence of states
of different particles, the full statistics of these large particle systems can be described by a single closed
effective equation, namely the kinetic equation, in the limit of infinite degrees of freedom.
    The first instance of what is now known as the Boltzmann equation was written down in the 1870s, and
since then, the mathematical justification of this limiting process has been a major challenge. The main
difficulty comes from justifying the assumption of (asymptotic) independence of different particles, which
is known as the Stosszahlansatz or molecular chaos. The formulation of this problem as known today was
first put forward by Grad, and the works of Cercignani and Grad in the 1950s introduced the important
idea of the BBGKY hierarchy to this subject. In the mean time, a simplified model was also introduced and
studied by Kac ([39], see also subsequent developments [48, 44, 45, 46]). Motivated by these contributions,
Lanford [41] (see also King [40]) was able to set a first milestone in this subject, by proving the molecular
chaos assumption and deriving the Boltzmann equation for sufficiently small time.
    Since [41], there have been a large number of results devoted to the derivation of the Boltzmann equation.
We shall not attempt to be exhaustive in recounting the vast literature here, but will only list some of the
most important ones below; see the surveys [54, 6, 10] and references therein for the other contributions.
     • Illner-Pulvirenti [35] (see also [36]), which obtained a long-time result but for the near-vacuum case
       (i.e. the collision rate E(N ) · εd−1 ≪ 1 in (1.12));
     • Gallagher-Saint Raymond-Texier [27], which completed and refined the result of [41], and obtained
       explicit convergence rates;
     • Pulvirenti-Simonella [51], which obtained explicit decay rates for cumulants associated with the
       hard sphere system, with the exponent proportional to the number of involved particles, again in
       the short-time regime;
8                                      YU DENG, ZAHER HANI, AND XIAO MA


     • Bodineau-Gallagher-Saint Raymond [3, 4, 5], which studied the related problems of deriving Brow-
       nian motion, Ornstein-Uhlenbeck processes, and the Stokes-Fourier system from the hard-sphere
       dynamics, by passing through a linearized Boltzmann equation;
     • Bodineau-Gallagher-Saint Raymond-Simonella [8, 9, 11, 7], which investigated the further question
       of deriving the equation for fluctuations, either for short time, or for longer times but at the exact
       Maxwellian equilibrium.
   Finally, apart from the question of deriving the kinetic equations, there has also been a huge literature
concerning the solution theory of these equations (Boltzmann, Landau etc.). In general strong solutions
exist in local and perturbative settings, but only weak solutions are known globally (see however [31, 37] on
recent global existence results in the homogeneous setting). We will not elaborate on this, but refer to the
reviews in [57, 58] and references therein.

1.2.2. Wave turbulence and the wave kinetic equation. One of the most important developments in the
physics of thje twentieth century was the emergence of quantum physics as a theory to describe subatomic
particle dynamics. A central idea in this new theory was the particle-wave duality, which contributed to
an exponential rise in the physical and mathematical interest in nonlinear wave systems. This led to the
recasting of many classical particle theories in the context of interacting wave systems, including the theory
of nonequilibrium statistical physics for waves, which sometimes goes by the name of wave turbulence or
wave kinetic theory.
   Same as the particle case, the wave kinetic theory aims at describing the statistics of a wave system using
a closed equation for the lowest order correlation; usually this is the second moment of the wave field, as
opposed to the one-particle density function in the kinetic theory for particles. The role of the Boltzmann
equation is then played by the wave kinetic equation (WKE). For instance, in the case when the wave system
is modeled by the cubic nonlinear Schrödinger equation, the wave kinetic equation takes the form
                                  ˆ ˆ
                                          (v − v1 ) · ω + · n′ n′1 (n + n1 ) − nn1 (n′ + n′1 ) dωdv1
                                                                                             
                (∂t + v · ∇x )n =                                                                        (1.19)
                                  R3   S2

in dimension d = 3 (including also the homogeneous variant where the v · ∇x term in (1.19) is absent),
where we use the same notation as in (1.14). The wave kinetic equations, similar to (1.19) but for different
wave systems, were first introduced in the work of Peierls [49] in 1929 for anharmonic crystals. This was
shortly followed by works of Nordheim [38] and Uehling and Uhlenbeck [56] in the context of quantum
gases. Since then, the theory has attracted a great deal of attention from mathematicians and physicists,
and has developed into a rich and broad subject. In particular, these contributions included the influential
works of Hasselmann [32, 33] in 1962–1963, which applied the wave kinetic theory to ocean waves and led
to the modern methods of ocean surface forecasting, as well as the work of Zakharov [59] in 1965, which
provided a systematic approach to the theory and how it can be used to understand turbulent phenomena for
waves. In [59], Zakharov also introduced the notion of Kolmogorov-Zakharov spectra, along the same lines
as Kolmogorov’s theory of hydrodynamic turbulence. It is for this reason that the theory is also commonly
known as wave turbulence theory. We refer the reader to [47] for a textbook treatment.
   Mathematically, the study of the derivation of wave kinetic equations like (1.19) started with the work
of Spohn [55]. For linear models, this program was completed in a series of papers by Erdös-Yau [26]
and Erdös-Salmhofer-Yau [25]. For the full nonlinear problem, an important early milestone was due to
Lukkarinen-Spohn [42] and subsequent partial results were obtained in [14, 20, 17, 18]. We refer the reader
to the introduction of [23] for a more exhaustive historical recount. The full derivation of (1.19) in the
short-time regime was obtained by the first two authors [21, 22, 23], which provides the wave-counterpart of
Lanford’s theorem.
   Recently, in [24], the first two authors have extended the derivation of (1.19) to arbitrarily long times
that cover the full lifespan of solutions to (1.19). We remark that the result of [24] is parallel to Theorem
1, not only conceptually but also on a very concrete level. In fact, while the proof in the current paper is
self-contained, many of the key ideas in this proof are obtained by adapting the ideas from the proof in [24]
            LONG TIME DERIVATION OF THE BOLTZMANN EQUATION FROM HARD SPHERE DYNAMICS                                            9


to the particle setting. In Section 2.5 we will make a detailed comparison between the proofs in [24] and in
the current paper, including both similarities and differences.

1.3. High level overview of the proof. In this subsection, we present a mostly philosophical description
of the proof, its strategy, and its most important ideas. A more elaborate and concrete discussion of these
ideas can be found in Section 2. The underlying philosophy of the proof is the same as that in [24], which
we explain below in the context of deriving the Boltzmann equation from the hard sphere particle dynamics.

1.3.1. Time layering and cumulants. The well-known fundamental difficulty in extending Lanford’s theorem
to longer times, which is the same difficulty in the long-time derivation of the wave kinetic equation, is the
divergence of the time series expansions of the solutions. In fact, in both settings, the proof of the short-
time result is based on matching two different time series expansions: expansion I, given by the Duhamel
expansion of the dynamics of the microscopic system (or the BBGKY hierarchy satisfied by the correlation
functions fs ), and expansion II, given by the Duhamel expansion of the kinetic equation. The bulk of the
proof3 goes into identifying the leading terms in expansion I, matching them order-by-order with the terms
in expansion II, and proving that all the remaining non-leading terms vanish in the limit. Of course, the
validity of the above scheme requires that the expansion II for the kinetic equation converges in the first
place; this accounts for the restriction to sufficiently small times, which is given by the radius of convergence
of this expansion.
   To go beyond this threshold and reach an arbitrarily large time t ≤ tfin , a natural idea is to subdivide the
long time interval [0, t] into L subintervals (called time layers) [(ℓ − 1)τ, ℓτ ] with 1 ≤ ℓ ≤ L and τ sufficiently
small, such that on each subinterval, the solution to the kinetic equation is given by a convergent series
expansion (i.e. expansion II) with base time (ℓ − 1)τ . This is the first key step in our proof, namely the
time layering, where we aim at controlling the behavior of the microscopic system on [(ℓ−1)τ, ℓτ ] using the
data at time (ℓ − 1)τ . An obvious difficulty arises with this approach, namely that the data at (ℓ − 1)τ will
no longer have exact independence between different particles. Note that such independence (or equivalently
the complete factorization of the correlation functions fs ) is granted4 at time 0, and plays a fundamental
role in controlling the expansion I of the microscopic system in the short-time proof.
   Next, to characterize the lack of independence, we shall resort to the cumulant expansion of the s-point
correlation functions which was introduced in [51] in the Boltzmann context, namely
                                               X  Y                   
                                 fs (t, zs ) =             f A (t, zj ) · EH (t, zH ),                        (1.20)
                                                 H⊆[s]    j∈[s]\H

where [s] = {1, · · · , s} and zH = (zj )j∈H . Here in (1.20), the tensor product represents the factorized part
of fs (t, zs ), where f A (t, z) is a single variable function for z ∈ R2d , which is an approximate solution to
the Boltzmann equation (1.13) up to a small error. We refer to the quantities EH as cumulants; note that
exact independence or factorization corresponds to EH ≡ 0 for all H ̸= ∅ (we understand that E∅ := 1).
Similar cumulant expansions play a fundamental role in the wave setting in [24] (which actually involves the
full cumulant expansion where the sum in (1.20) is taken over all partitions of [s]), as well as in the particle
setting in several recent works [51, 7, 8, 10].
   Recall that the ultimate goal of Theorem 1 is to prove (1.18), thus it clearly suffices to show that the
L1 norm of the cumulants EH (t) = EH (Lτ ) vanishes in the limit for H ̸= ∅. As such, Theorem 1 can be
reduced to the following statement, see Proposition 5.3:
  (♡1 ) For each 1 ≤ ℓ ≤ L, we have the cumulant expansion (1.20) at t = ℓτ , with some constant α > 0
        such that
                                   ∥EH (ℓτ )∥L1 ≤ εα|H| , ∀H ̸= ∅.                               (1.21)
   3In the wave case, there is an extra level of complication coming from the fact that the series does not converge absolutely,

but only conditionally (see Section 2.5).
   4Strictly speaking, the presence of 1
                                          DN (zN ) in (1.7) prevents the exact factorization at time t = 0 and requires an initial
cumulant expansion to deal with, but we shall ignore this minor point in this introduction.
10                                          YU DENG, ZAHER HANI, AND XIAO MA


1.3.2. The cumulant ansatz. To study (♡1 ), we start with an illuminating observation made in [51], where
it is proved that: if the cumulants at time (ℓ − 1)τ are bounded in L∞ , say

                                                  ∥EH ((ℓ − 1)τ )∥L∞ ≤ εβ|H|                                               (1.22)

for some β > 0, then the cumulants at time ℓτ are bounded in L1 , i.e. (1.21) holds for some α > 0.
   This looks like an inductive scheme if one could replace the L∞ norm in (1.22) by L1 . However, a
careful analysis shows such a scheme can never work as it would contradict the time irreversibility of the
Boltzmann equation. In fact, the hard-sphere dynamics is time reversible, and reversing the time direction
just corresponds to flipping the signs of the velocity (vj ↔ −vj ), which does not change either L1 or L∞
norm in (1.21) or (1.22). Therefore, if this inductive scheme were to work forward in time starting from time
(ℓ − 1)τ where ℓ ≥ 2, then it would also work backward in time. This would imply that the asymptotics at
time (ℓ−2)τ would be given by the sign-flipped forward evolution, which is impossible as the real asymptotics
is given by the backward evolution.
   The above discussion suggests that we should keep as much as possible of the structural information of
the cumulants EH (ℓτ ), in order to propagate it to the next time layer. To see how this creates the arrow
of time, assume for example ℓ = s = 2 and H = {1, 2}. Then (a component of) the cumulant EH (τ, z1 , z2 )
behaves like the indicator function 1Ω of the set

                Ω = {(z1 , z2 ) : the particles with state vectors z1 and z2 at time τ collide in [0, τ ]}.                (1.23)

If we keep this structural information, this will guarantee that the contribution of EH to the one-point
density f1 (2τ, z1 ) vanishes in the limit, as we have |Ω ∩ Ω1 | ≪ |Ω1 | for the set

           Ω1 = {(z1 , z2 ) : the particles with state vectors z1 and z2 at time τ collide in [τ, 2τ ]}.                   (1.24)

However, if we perform the change of variables vj ↔ −vj for the set Ω, we will get another set Ω which does
not satisfy |Ω ∩ Ω1 | ≪ |Ω1 | (in fact it is easy to see that Ω = Ω1 ). In this way, we see the creation of the
distinction between going forward and backward in time.
   In reality, we will propagate exact expressions of these cumulants EH (ℓτ ) up to small errors; these
expressions are given by summations over layered interaction diagrams. This is the second key step
in the proof, which we explain below.
   Recall that the correlation functions fs = fs (ℓτ, zs ) represent the joint density of s particles at time ℓτ .
We know they can be expressed in terms of (i) the collision history of these particles in [(ℓ − 1)τ, ℓτ ] (layer
ℓ), i.e. the set of collisions involving any of them, or any other particle colliding with any of them, and so
on; (ii) the correlation function fs+ ((ℓ − 1)τ, zs+ ) for all the particles involved in (i) (including the s target
particles and the additional particles colliding with them etc.), at time (ℓ − 1)τ . Then, in view of (1.20), we
can obtain a similar expression for EH (ℓτ ). Schematically, we have5
                        X                                                                           
         EH (ℓτ, zs ) =       integral expressions involving Dℓ , f A ((ℓ − 1)τ ) and EH ((ℓ − 1)τ )          (1.25)
                            Dℓ

up to negligible error terms, where Dℓ represents the collision history in layer ℓ. By iterating (1.25) we get
the expression
                                                        X
                                         EH (ℓτ, zs ) =     IN (D)                                      (1.26)
                                                                    D

up to negligible error terms. Here D = (Dℓ , · · · , D1 ) is the combination of collision histories Dq for all layers
q ≤ ℓ, which we refer to as the layered interaction diagram, and IN (D) is an explicit integral expression
formed by composing the expressions (1.25) for all different layers. In particular IN (D) involves both the

     5In reality, this expansion also relies on an inclusion-exclusion principle (cf. Lemma 4.27) similar in spirit to that used in

earlier works like [51, 8, 7]. This then brings a new interaction structure into the cumulant expansion, namely the overlaps,
which eventually correspond to the loss term in the Boltzmann collision kernel.
             LONG TIME DERIVATION OF THE BOLTZMANN EQUATION FROM HARD SPHERE DYNAMICS                                              11


layered interaction diagram D (which contains Dq for all q ≤ ℓ) and f A ((q − 1)τ ) for all layers q ≤ ℓ. By
further taking the L1 norm, we get that
                                                    X
                                     ∥EH (ℓτ )∥L1 =   ∥IN (D)∥L1 ,                                     (1.27)
                                                                   D

where the L1 norm is taken by further integrating the expression IN (D) in (1.26) in the zs variables.
   The expressions (1.26)–(1.27) are established in Sections 4 and 6, see Propositions 4.17, 4.21, and (6.16)–
(6.15) in Section 7; they are also discussed in more detail in Section 2.1. Here, we make two comments
concerning our strategy, and in particular the cumulant ansatz (1.26):
    (1) This ansatz retains all the structural information of EH (ℓτ ) by memorizing the full collision history
        that contributes to this cumulant (see the precise formulas in Propositions 4.17 and 4.21).
    (2) In (1.25) and (1.26) we are performing a partial expansion in the following sense: while we apply
        the iterative expansion of EH ((ℓ − 1)τ ) in (1.25) to obtain (1.26), we do not do the same thing for
        the one-particle function f A ((ℓ − 1)τ ), and do not expand it any further. As a result, the definition
        of the cumulant EH (ℓτ ) involves all previous layers q ≤ ℓ, whereas that of f A (ℓτ ) only involves the
        immediately previous layer at time (ℓ − 1)τ . The reason why we do not expand f A (ℓτ ) all the way
        to t = 0 is because it is well approximated by the solution of the Boltzmann equation, which we
        have a priori control. This partial expansion is crucial in controlling the combinatorial factor given
        by the number of choices for the layered interaction diagram D, see Section 1.3.3 below. The same
        idea is also used in the wave setting in [24].
   At this time, with the formulas (1.26)–(1.27), the proof of (♡1 ) then reduces to (i) controlling the number
of choices for D in (1.26) and (1.27), and (ii) bounding each individual term ∥IN (D)∥L1 in (1.27). This in
fact requires an extra step of truncation, which is discussed in Section 1.3.3 below.
1.3.3. Truncated dynamics, and two key estimates on recollisions. In order to bound ∥EH ∥L1 given by the
sum (1.27), it is crucial that the following two quantities are under control, within each layer [(ℓ − 1)τ, ℓτ ]:
       • The size of each cluster, where a cluster is a set containing particles that are connected/related by
         collisions in a single layer;
       • The number of recollisions in each cluster, where a recollision in a cluster is a collision that is in
         excess of the collisions necessary to bring the particles in this cluster together (in particular, the
         number of recollisions equals ρ = m − n + 1 where m is the number of collisions and n is the number
         of particles).
   Here, the restriction on the size of clusters is needed to bound the number of choices for D (if the size
gets too big, say larger than ε−1 , then this combinatorial factor can mess up with asymptotic independence,
see [51]), and the restriction on the number of recollisions is needed to avoid the exceptional scenario where
a relatively small number of particles become O(ε) close to each other and produce too many recollisions
within O(ε) time period. See Section 2.2 for more explanations.
   To enforce the above restrictions, we need to introduce the notion of truncated dynamics (see Definition
3.7) within each time layer, which is the third key step in the proof. More precisely, in the truncated
dynamics, at each time where a collision would occur, we check whether or not this collision would cause a
violation to the following two conditions:
    (1) The number of particles in each cluster is at most Λ := | log ε|O(1) , and
    (2) The number of recollisions in each cluster is at most Γ = O(1).
If this collision would cause a violation, then we “turn off” this collision and allow the particles to cross each
other; otherwise we allow this collision just as in the original hard-sphere dynamics.
   With the truncation in Definition 3.7 in effect, it is clear that the layered interaction diagram D occurring
in (1.26) must satisfy both conditions (1)–(2) stated above in each layer 6, which puts a significant restriction
   6Note that the number of recollisions can be arbitrarily large if we put together two different time layers; for example,

assume a set of m particles is connected by a tree of collisions (i.e. no recollision) in time layer [0, τ ], and is also connected by
12                                          YU DENG, ZAHER HANI, AND XIAO MA


on the possible combinatorial structure of D. By combining this with the partial expansion described in
Section 1.3.2 above, we can obtain a crucial upper bound for the number of choices of D in (1.26) as follows.
Let the total number of particles involved in D be X ≤ | log ε|O(1) , and the total number of recollisions in
D (accounting for all layers together) be ρ, then
                                    (the number of choices for D) ≤ C X · | log ε|C·ρ ,                                      (1.28)
which is proved in Proposition 6.12. As such, the proof of (1.21) in (♡1 ) then reduces to the following
estimate in Proposition 7.11 (which is stated in the equivalent form IN (M), explained in Section 1.3.4
below):
     (♡2 ) For any layered interaction diagram D that consists of L layers, if the number of recollisions in each
           cluster in each layer is at most Γ, then we have
                                                ∥IN (D)∥L1 ≤ τ X · εα(L,Γ)·ρ ,                                               (1.29)
          where α(L, Γ) is a positive constant depending on L and Γ, X and ρ are the total number of particles
          and total number of recollisions in D, as in (1.28).
             Note that the quantity ∥IN (D)∥L1 on the left hand side of (1.29) equals the integral of the
          probability density IN (D), which represents the probability that all the collisions recorded in D
          indeed happen.
    Of course, by introducing the truncated dynamics, we also create an error term corresponding to the case
where the original dynamics (or in fact the extended version of it, see Definition 3.5) does not coincide with
the truncated dynamics. Here we need to prove that the L1 norm of the associated error density (Proposition
5.4), i.e. the probability that this scenario happens, vanishes in the limit. For this, we consider the first time
layer where this inconsistency happens, and the first time in this time layer that this happens. By ignoring
all collisions after this time and arguing as in the truncated case before this time layer, we obtain a layered
interaction diagram D, which satisfies (essentially) the same assumptions as in the truncated case, but also
contains at least one cluster in the last layer that has more than Γ recollisions within this single layer. If Γ
is large enough depending on d, this cluster alone will provide enough powers of ε and lead to a vanishing
upper bound on the desired probability. This is summarized in the following estimate:
     (♡3 ) Let Γ be large enough depending on d (essentially Γ > (2d) ↑↑ (2d) where ↑↑ is the tetration). For
           any layered interaction diagram D, which contains only one layer and one cluster that has at least
           Γ recollisions, we have
                                              ∥IN (D)∥L1 ≤ εα                                          (1.30)
          for some positive constant α > d − 1.
             We note that, in comparison to (1.29), the required gain in this part of the argument is α = O(1),
          which is the reason why Γ can be chosen to be O(1). The reason that we need α > d − 1 will be
          explained later in Section 2.4.

1.3.4. CH molecules, and the algorithm. Our goal now is to prove (♡2 ) and (♡3 ), i.e. the bounds (1.29) and
(1.30). The next and fourth key step here is to reduce the estimate of the quantity ∥IN (D)∥L1 to a pure
combinatorial property for the layered interaction diagram D.
   To achieve this, we first introduce the notion of the collision history (CH) molecule M, see Definition
7.1. This is a combinatorial object in the form of a directed graph, which can be viewed as a topological
reduction of the actual trajectories of the particles under collisions, see Figure 2. Note that this M is in one-
to-one correspondence with the layered interaction diagram D, but is more suitable for our combinatorial
analysis.
   Next, we can recast the integral ∥IN (D)∥L1 as an equivalent integral expression IN (M) (see Definition 7.3
4), associated with the corresponding CH molecule M. This IN (M) involves integration over all intermediate

another tree of collisions in time layer [τ, 2τ ]. Then in the whole time interval [0, 2τ ], the number of recollisions among these m
particles is m − 1. In fact, this is a most typical case that we need to deal with in the proof of (♡2 ), see Section 2.3.2.
            LONG TIME DERIVATION OF THE BOLTZMANN EQUATION FROM HARD SPHERE DYNAMICS                                            13


positions and velocities (for each edge of M), and all collision times (for each atom of M), against the Dirac
δ functions that represent the relations between the incoming and outgoing velocities at each collision of M.
The reduction is done in Section 7, see Proposition 7.8, where we prove that ∥IN (D)∥L1 ≤ IN (M). The
main advantage of IN (M) is that it is more flexible and easily localized, due to the freedom to exchange the
order of integration; see Section 2.2 for more detailed discussions.
   To get optimal bounds for IN (M), we need to find the correct order of variables in which we integrate. For
this we introduce the key notion of cutting operations, see Definition 8.6, which provides a systematic way
of deciding the order of integration (i.e. applying the Fubini theorem). More precisely, each cutting divides
the molecule M into two separate molecules M1 and M2 ; by first integrating in those variables associated
with the molecule M2 (with those variables associated with the molecule M1 regarded as fixed) and then
integrating in those variables associated with the molecule M1 , we get that7
                                               IN (M) ≤ IN (M1 ) · IN (M2 ).                                               (1.31)
   The goal of the cutting algorithm is to cut M into a number of special molecules called elementary
molecules Mj (see Definition 8.9), for which the quantity IN (Mj ) can be directly calculated and estimated,
so that applying (1.31) then yields an upper bound for IN (M). In fact, the two most important types
of elementary molecules are the {3}-molecules which represent collisions (i.e. a particle X colliding with
a given particle Y ), and the {33}-molecules which represent recollisions (i.e. a particle X colliding with a
given particle Y , and then colliding again with another given particle Z). It is known that {3}-atoms are
normal in the sense that integrating it does not gain any power, while {33}-molecules are good in the sense
that integrating it gains a power εα for some constant α > 0 (there are also {4}-molecules which are usually
viewed as bad ; we will not discuss them in detail here, but see Section 2.3). The discussion of elementary
molecules, as well as associated weight and volume bounds, are proved in Section 9; see also Section 2.3 for
more detailed discussions.
   With the above discussions, it is now clear that the main ingredient in the proof of (♡2 ) and (♡3 ) is to
construct a cutting algorithm that produces sufficiently many {33}-components. This is the final key step
and one major novelty of the proof. Roughly speaking, the cutting algorithm can be split into two big steps
(A) a step of reducing the multi-layer case to the two-layer case by performing a layer refining algorithm
and a layer selection algorithm, see Section 10, and (B) a step of treating the two-layer case by exhibiting
a dichotomy between two main scenarios, and applying several basic sub-cutting-programs, see Section 11).
For more detailed discussion of the algorithm, see Section 2.3.
1.4. Future horizons. Here we list some future directions which will become within reach with the proof
of Theorem 1.
1.4.1. The hydrodynamic limit. After deriving the Boltzmann equation from particle dynamics in the kinetic
limit, the next and final step of solving Hilbert’s sixth problem would be to take the hydrodynamic limit and
derive the fluid equations (Euler and Navier-Stokes) from the Boltzmann equation. Formally, this requires
considering densities that are local Maxwellians, i.e. those functions that are Maxwellian in v but with mass,
center and variance depending on (t, x):
                                                                    |v − u(t, x)|2
                                                                                  
                                               ρ(t, x)
                               n(t, x, v) =                 exp   −                  .                  (1.32)
                                            (2πT (t, x))d/2            2T (t, x)
By considering perturbation expansions (i.e. Hilbert and Chapman-Enskog expansions) around (1.32), we
can derive the compressible Euler equation for (ρ, u, T ) and the incompressible Euler and Navier-Stokes
equations for subsequent terms, in a suitable scaling limit.
  Compared to the derivation of the Boltzmann equation, this step of going from Boltzmann to fluid
equations is much better understood, see for example [19, 30, 53, 28]. We believe that, by combining the
    7In reality, for technical reasons, we will not apply (1.31) in this form; instead, we will (equivalently) rewrite IN (M) as an

iterated integral and estimate each step of integration separately, see Proposition 8.10 and the iterated integral formulation
(9.80) in the proof of Proposition 7.11.
14                                   YU DENG, ZAHER HANI, AND XIAO MA


arguments in the proof of Theorem 1 and those in the above-listed references, one can finally execute both
steps of Hilbert’s program in tandem, and arrive at the final resolution of Hilbert’s sixth problem, where the
fluid equations are rigorously derived from the colliding particle systems in a suitable limit. We leave this
analysis to an upcoming work.

1.4.2. The short-range potential case. With the methods in the current paper, a natural problem that should
be within reach, is the short range potential case studied in [27], namely
                                                                          
                                  dxi         dvi      1X          xi − xj

                                   dt          dt      ε              ε

where Φ is a suitable potential function. We believe that Theorem 1 should remain true in this case, but the
proof would need to involve (among other things) extra technical ingredients to deal with the possibility of
simultaneous interactions between more than two particles via (1.33).

1.4.3. Fluctuations and large deviations. The rigorous study of this direction was initiated in the recent
works of Bodineau-Gallagher-Saint Raymond-Simonella [8, 9, 11]. Here one considers the random variable

                                      2. An overview of the proof
    In this section, we give a more detailed sketch of the proof of Theorem 1, and introduce at the end of the
section some important notations used in the rest of the paper. As explained in Section 1.3, we will divide
[0, tfin ] into smaller intervals
                                                         L
                                                         [
                                            [0, tfin ] =   [(ℓ − 1)τ, ℓτ ]                               (2.1)
                                                    ℓ=1
with τ small enough so that, on each subinterval, the solution n(t, z) to the Boltzmann equation is given by
a convergent series expansion in time with base point (ℓ − 1)τ .
           LONG TIME DERIVATION OF THE BOLTZMANN EQUATION FROM HARD SPHERE DYNAMICS                                15


2.1. The cumulant ansatz. We start by explaining the cumulant expansion (1.20) and the cumulant ansatz
(1.26) in Section 1.3.2. It is worth mentioning that different variants of the cumulant expansion (1.20) have
appeared in previous works like [51, 8]. In the discussion below, we will make two assumptions:
       • We omit the cumulants caused by the non-overlapping factor 1DN in the initial density, and assume
         that W0,N in (1.7) is completely factorized; in reality such cumulants can be easily treated, see
         Proposition 5.1.
       • We assume the truncation introduced in Section 1.3.3 is in effect, so the requirements (1)–(2) in
         Section 1.3.3 are satisfied. The necessity of the truncation is further explained in Section 2.2, and
         the estimate for the error term created by this truncation, namely (1.30) in (♡3 ), is explained in
         Section 2.4.
                                                                                          Qs
   Begin with the first time layer [0, τ ]. At time t = 0, we have fs (0, zs ) = j=1 n0 (zj ) by complete
factorization; now we want to calculate fs (τ, zs ), which is the density function for the s target particles
at time τ (we call them root particles). For this, we need to specify the collision history D1 in this layer.
This D1 contains two ingredients: (i) the clusters consisting of the particles that are connected to each root
particle either directly via collisions or indirectly via collisions with intermediate particles, and (ii) the exact
set of collisions between particles in these clusters and the time partial ordering between these collisions. We
shall call ingredient (i) a cluster forest and denote it by F1 (Definitions 4.11, 4.19), and call ingredient (ii)
an interaction diagram and denote it by D1 (Definition 6.1).
   To proceed, first fix a cluster forest F1 in layer [0, τ ]. This also determines the set of all particles in these
clusters, which we denote by p(F1 ) (see Definition 4.13). Note that p(F1 ) contains the set of root particles at
time τ , which we denote by r(F1 ). As |r(F1 )| = s we may identify r(F1 ) with [s] := {1, · · · , s} if necessary;
similarly we can assume |p(F1 )| = s+ ≥ s and identify p(F1 ) with [s+ ] = {1, · · · , s+ } if necessary. Using
complete factorization at initial time t = 0, we then have (schematically) that
                                          Xˆ Y
                                                       f A (0, zej ) · 1(F1 )
                                                                              Y

                                         F1        j∈p(F1 )            j∈p(F1 )\r(F1 )

Here in (2.2), the integral is taken over zj for all non-root particles j in layer 1, the 1(F1 ) is the indicator
function that the clusters are exactly given by F1 , and f A (0, ·) = n0 (·) is the initial one-particle density.
Moreover the variables zep(F1 ) := (e zj : j ∈ p(F1 )) represent the states of the particles in p(F1 ) at time 0,
which is related to the time τ variables zp(F1 ) by an explicit coordinate transform that depends only on the
set and order of collisions in each cluster of F1 .
   Next, we extract the part of fs (τ, zs ) in (2.2) that is factorized. In fact, in view of the factorization
structure of the factors in (2.2) and the coordinate transform between zp(F1 ) and zep(F1 ) , we know that for
each particle j ∈ r(F1 ) = [s], the expression (2.2) contains a factor that depends only on zj , if and only if the
particle j is not connected to any other root particle in layer 1 (in the sense of being in the same cluster). For
such j, the corresponding factor that depends only on zj is given by an infinite series of integrals involving
only f A (0); by similar arguments as in the short time proof, we know this exactly matches the Duhamel
iterations of the Boltzmann equation up to lower order terms, and gives an approximate solution to the
Boltzmann equation at time τ , which we denote by f A (τ ).
   In this way, we obtain the cumulant expansion (1.20) at time τ , with f A defined above as an approximate
solution to Boltzmann, and the cumulant EH given by

Here in (2.6), the F is the layered cluster forest (Definition 4.19), p(F ) is the set of all particles in F
