is the indicator function associated with the layered cluster forest F defined as the 1(Fℓ ) above. Moreover,
in the summation in (2.6) we require that for each q ≤ ℓ and each particle in the set Kq := r(Fq ), it
must be connected to another particle in Kq either in layer q, or via layers before q (through previous time
cumulants). Here Kq = r(Fq ) ⊆ p(Fq+1 ) is the set of root particles in the expansion of the cumulant at time
qτ (this expansion involves layer q and those before q). For the exact formula, see Proposition 4.21.
   All the above calculations are done in Section 4, see Propositions 4.17 and 4.21. Finally, we can introduce
the layered interaction diagram D := (Dℓ , · · · , D1 ) (Definition 6.1) into the expansion (2.6); note that each
Dq fixes the set and time order of the collisions that happen in the clusters of Fq . Then the summation in
F in (2.6) is turned into a summation in (F and) D of a quantity that depends only on F and D, which
we denote by IN (D). This is done in Section 7, see (6.16)–(6.15) (where we include F as an argument of
IN ; also the argument H ′ comes from the time 0 cumulant, which is omitted here). This then completes
the proof of (1.26) and (1.27).
     We make two remarks concerning this part of the proof:
      (1) This partial expansion method (explained in Section 1.3.2), where we only expand cumulants and
          refrain from further expanding any one-particle density, is key to obtaining the control for the
          expansion of EH (t), particularly the number of choices for D in (1.27). We will further discuss
          this point in Section 2.2. Note similar partial expansions are also performed in the wave turbulence
          setting [24].
      (2) In the above cumulant expansion, to extract the factorized part of fs (ℓτ ) in (2.4), we need the
          indicator functions 1(Fℓ ) and 1(F ) in (2.3)–(2.6) to have suitable factorization structure; this requires
          to use an inclusion-exclusion principle, see Lemma 4.27. This will introduce a new type of interaction
          between the particles, namely that of overlaps, which happens when we allow two particles in different
          clusters to pass through each other without changing their trajectories. These overlaps contribute
          to the loss term in Boltzmann’s equation, and also play some auxiliary role in the combinatorial
           LONG TIME DERIVATION OF THE BOLTZMANN EQUATION FROM HARD SPHERE DYNAMICS                              17


        analysis of molecules. We will not spend too much time discussing them in this section, but see
        Remark 2.1 in Section 2.3.

2.2. CH Molecule representation. Now, with (1.26) and (1.27) established, it suffices to prove the bound
(1.28) on the number of choices for the layered interaction diagram D, and the bounds (1.29) and (1.30) in
(♡2 ) and (♡3 ) for ∥IN (D)∥L1 .
    We start with (1.28). The key observation here is the following lower bound on the number ρ of recollisions.
Given a layered interaction diagram D, for each q ≤ ℓ, let sq = |r(Fq )| = |Kq | be the size of the cumulant
(i.e. number of root particles) at time qτ of the expansion. Then, by our assumption on F in Section 2.1
above, each of these sq particles must be connected to another one via layer q and those before q; moreover,
if |H| ≪ sq (which can be assumed or the proof will be easy), then almost all of these sq particles must also
be connected to each other via layers q + 1 and after q + 1. As such, these particles alone will contribute
                                                                                             P
≥ sq /2 recollisions (when putting all layers together), leading to the lower bound ρ ≳         q sq , i.e. each
cumulant expansion generates recollisions that are proportional to the size of this cumulant; see Lemma 6.11.
Note that this lower bound relies crucially on our partial expansion, in which we only further expand the
cumulants into previous time layers.
    In fact, the set of all recollisions in D can be divided into two parts: those that happen across different
                        P
layers, which is ρ1 ∼ q sq as in Lemma 6.11, and those (say ρ2 ) that happen within each single layer. Let
                                                        P
the total number of particles in p(F ) be X and q sq := U, then we can bound the number of choices for
                                                                                          P
D as follows. At each layer q, assume the total number of particles is Xq (note that q Xq ≤ X + U) and
the total number of recollisions within this layer is ∆q . Then, since we are expanding a cumulant of size sq ,
the total number of choices for Dq is bounded by C Xq · (Xq )C(sq +∆q ) (note when sq = ∆q = O(1) then the
bound is exponential in Xq by similar arguments as in the short time theory, while locating the cumulant
                                             C(s +∆ )
particles and recollisions then leads to Xq q q choices). Then, under the assumption that Xq ≤ | log ε|O(1)
(see truncation condition (1) in Section 1.3.3), we get the upper bound
                                      Y
                                           C Xq · (Xq )C(sq +∆q ) ≤ C X · | log ε|Cρ
                                                                 
                                    q
           P                                P               P
using that q Xq ≤ X + U and that U = q sq ≲ ρ and q ∆q ≤ ρ2 ≤ ρ. This completes the proof of (1.28)
(for the actual proof, see Proposition 6.12), and in the mean time justifies the necessity of condition (1) in
Section 1.3.3.
   We now turn to (♡2 ) and (♡3 ). The proof for the truncation remainder (♡3 ) is similar to (♡2 ) but relies
on one additional ingredient, which will be discussed in Section 2.4; in this and the next subsection we will
focus on (♡2 ). Before discussing the proof, we first demonstrate the necessity of the truncation condition
(2) in Section 1.3.3, and why the coefficient α(L, Γ) in (1.29) has to depend on Γ. In fact, it is known, see
[16], that it is possible for m particles to collide at least 2m/2 times within a time interval O(ε), if at some
time they are within distance O(ε) of each other; by a simple scaling argument, we know that this happens
with probability ≳ cm εm−2 if we only fix the label of one particle, where cm is a constant depending only
on m. As the recollision number is ρ ≥ 2m/2 − m, it is then clear that the coefficient α(L, Γ) in (1.29) must
approach 0 when Γ → ∞. This justifies the necessity of the upper bound Γ = O(1) on the recollision number
within each cluster in each layer, otherwise (♡2 ) would not be true.
   Now we proceed with the proof of (♡2 ). Note that the expression ∥IN (D)∥L1 , see (6.15) and (7.6),
contains the integral over the final (time ℓτ ) states of the particles, while the variables in the input functions
f A ((q − 1)τ ) are states of these particles at previous times. The coordinate transformations connecting
them contain extremely complicated compositions of one-collision scattering maps, which are very hard to
analyze. To remedy this, we would like to include all the intermediate states of these particles, as well as
all the collision times, into the integration. This leads to the structure of layered collision history (CH)
molecules M, as equivalent formulations of layered interaction diagrams D, which we explain below.
   The CH molecule M, see Definitions 7.1–7.2 and Proposition 7.7, is defined as a directed graph associated
with the interaction diagrams D (and encodes the same information), where each collision (and overlap)
18                                    YU DENG, ZAHER HANI, AND XIAO MA


in D is represented by a node called an atom in M. Two atoms are connected if they are two consecutive
collisions of the same particle, by a bond that represents a linear piece in the trajectory of this particle.
This M can also be perceived as a topological reduction of the actual trajectories of the particles by ignoring
lengths and distances, see Figure 2. We also allow edges at an atom that are not connected to other atoms;
these are called ends and represent linear trajectories of particles before the first or after the last collision.




        Figure 2. Converting a trajectory to a CH molecule M. Here the blue nodes on the left
        represent particles and diamond nodes ♦ on the right represent atoms in M. The horizontal
        virtual lines represent the time intervals and layers on the left and right respectively.


   In a CH molecule, each atom has four edges (including ends), which correspond to the incoming and
outgoing states of the two particles colliding or overlapping; see Figure 2, where two edges at the same
atom have the same color if they belong to the trajectory of the same particle. For each particle, we call
this topological reduction of its trajectory a particle line. Trajectories within the time interval [(ℓ − 1)τ, ℓτ ]
are translated to the part of the diagram within layer ℓ. We note that the use of similar collision history
diagrams (without layers) is common in the literature [51, 8].
   Now, for each atom n of the CH molecule M, we can assign a variable tn which represents the time of the
collision (or overlap) n. For each edge e of M, which represents a piece of free transport, we can assign a
state variable ze = (xe , ve ) to it, where ve is the velocity during this free transport, and xe is the position
during this free transport, but transported back to time 0. Then, the integral ∥IN (D)∥L1 in (♡2 ) can be
recast as an expression IN (M), which is an integral over the (ze ) variables for all edges e of M and the (tn )
variables for all atoms n, against the Dirac δ functions that encode all the atoms (collisions and overlaps) in
M. Note that this IN (M) can be interpreted as the probability that all the collisions (and overlaps) in M
indeed happen, and obey the time ordering dictated by M; see (7.4) in Definition 7.3 4 and Proposition 7.8.
   Compared with IN (D), the main advantage of the expression IN (M) is that it is more flexible and easily
localized, which allows us to perform integration in any order of the atoms. In fact, the key to obtaining
good estimates for IN (M) is to design an optimal order of integration, dictated by a cutting algorithm. We
will discuss this next in Section 2.3.

2.3. The cutting algorithm. As discussed in Section 2.2 above, the cutting algorithm provides a book-
keeping way to specify the order of integration in the integral expression IN (M) associated with a CH
molecule M. We start with the notion of cuttings. For simplicity, we will assume our molecule M does not
contain overlaps; see Remark 2.1.

2.3.1. Cutting operations, and elementary molecules. Given any molecule M, a cutting is an operation that
divides M into two disjoint sub-molecules M1 and M2 , where for each bond connecting an atom n1 ∈ M1 to
an atom n2 ∈ M2 , we turn it into a free end at n1 and a fixed end at n2 . We say M1 is cut as free and M2
is cut as fixed ; see Figure 3 and Definition 8.6.
          LONG TIME DERIVATION OF THE BOLTZMANN EQUATION FROM HARD SPHERE DYNAMICS                           19


   In terms of the integral IN (M), cutting M1 as free (and M2 = M\M1 as fixed) simply means that we
integrate in the (ze ) and (tn ) variables associated with M2 first treating the variables associated with M1
as fixed, and then integrate in those variables associated with M1 . Here when a bond e between M1 and
M2 is cut and turned into a free end e1 at M1 and a fixed end e2 at M2 , we always impose the condition
ze = ze1 = ze2 ; see Figure 3. This is merely a diagrammatic representation of Fubini’s theorem.
   With the above reduction given by cutting, it is easy to see that IN (M) ≤ IN (M1 ) · IN (M2 ), where
in IN (M2 ) we are integrating in the free ends and bonds of M2 and then taking supremum over the fixed
ends; see Proposition 8.10, where this fact is stated in a formally different but equivalent way (with the full
iterated integral written down instead of taking supremum).




        Figure 3. Cutting a molecule M into smaller molecules M1 and M2 . This corresponds to
        first integrating IN (M2 ) in the M2 variables, where we treat ze = ze1 = ze2 and zf = zf1 =
        zf2 as fixed; these variables are then integrated together with the other M1 variables when
        integrating IN (M1 ). Note also that M2 is a {33}-molecule.


   In our cutting algorithm, presented in Section 2.3.2 below, we will cut M into elementary molecules Mj ,
each of which contains only one or two atoms, so the integral IN (Mj ) can be calculated explicitly. By the
                                       Q
above discussions we have IN (M) ≤ j IN (Mj ), which leads to an estimate for IN (M).
   More precisely, there are four major types of elementary molecules, denoted by {2}, {3}, {4} and {33},
indicated by the degree (i.e number of bonds and free ends) at each atom, see Figure 4 and Definition 8.9.
Note that {33}-molecules are divided into types {33A} and {33B}, and we also allow {44}-molecules which
play minor roles and will not be discussed here.
   The key observation is that, for each {33}-molecule Mj that occurs in the cutting process (see M2 in
Figure 3), the corresponding integral IN (Mj ) in the variables associated with Mj , exhibits an effective
power gain εα for some positive constant α, at least in typical cases (see Remark 2.1 for more discussions).
For this reason, we define each {33}-molecule to be good, and each {3}- and {2}-molecule to be normal.
In fact, a {3}-component corresponds to a normal collision, a {2}-component corresponds to a recollision,
and a {33}-component (which can be further decomposed to a {3}- and a {2}-component) corresponds to
a recollision that follows a normal collision, which constitutes the basic unit of gain, as already observed in
earlier works (put the correct citation) in different forms. Finally, we define a {4}-component to be bad as it
“wastes” one integral and leads to effective losses. See Propositions 9.1–9.2 for calculations of each IN (Mj )
and proofs for their estimates, Proposition 9.3–9.4 for auxiliary results controlling the weights and infinite
domain integrations, and Definition 9.5 for the precise version of classification of elementary molecules.
   By all the above discussions, it is now clear that, we can get a desirable estimate for IN (M), and thus
complete the proof of (♡2 ), if we can construct a cutting sequence that results in #{33} good (i.e. {33})
components and #{4} bad (i.e. {4}) components, such that

                                      #{33} > 100d2 · #{4} + α(L, Γ) · ρ                                  (2.7)

with the parameter α = α(L, Γ) as in (1.29). The precise form of (2.7) is given in (9.76) in Proposition 9.7.
We will discuss its proof in Section 2.3.2 below.
20                                    YU DENG, ZAHER HANI, AND XIAO MA




        Figure 4. Left to right: elementary molecules of type {2}, {3}, {4} and {33A} and {33B}.


2.3.2. The cutting algorithm. The proof of (2.7) can be divided into two parts: first is a reduction from
general molecules M as in (♡2 ) to a two-layer special case, which is done in Section 10; second is the
treatment of this two-layer scenario, which is done in Section 11.
   Part I: reduction to two-layer model. To start, we will assume that |H| ≪ ρ in (♡2 ) (otherwise the
construction will be easy, see Proposition 10.3). Recall also that the ρ recollisions contain two parts: the
       P
ρ1 ∼ q sq recollisions that happen across different layers, and the ρ2 recollisions that happen within a
single layer, see the discussion on (1.28) in Section 2.2. If ρ2 ≳ ρ, then the construction will also be easy,
because the upper bound of Γ recollisions per cluster forces the existence of at least (LΓ)−1 ρ2 clusters in one
single layer, each of which containing at least one recollision. Each of these recollisional clusters then leads
to at least one {33}-component, while the number of {4}-components in this construction can be bounded
by #{4} ≤ |H| ≪ ρ, see Propositions 10.9–10.10. We also remark that, all the above proof relies on one basic
algorithm which we call UP and its properties, see Definition 10.1 and Propositions 10.2–10.4; the proof of
the case ρ2 ≳ ρ also relies on the layer refinement procedure which is defined below.
   Now let us assume ρ2 ≪ ρ. Then these single-cluster recollisions will not be helpful in the proof; in fact
they cause the molecule M to contain cycles within a single layer, which is inconvenient for our proof. Thus,
we will perform a procedure called layer refinement (see Definition 10.7 and Proposition 10.8), where we
divide each layer of M into O(Γ) many sub-layers, such that each sublayer contains no cycles and thus is a
forest. This step relies on the upper bound of Γ recollisions in condition (2) in Section 1.3.3.
   Next is the key step in part I: recall that the molecule M represents an interaction diagram D which
is based on a layered cluster forest F , and this F satisfies the property in Section 2.1. In the language of
molecule M, this means that each particle line that crosses layer q (i.e. for particles in Kq := r(Fq )) must
be connected to another particle line that also crosses layer q, via layer q and layers before q; see Proposition
8.5. Now choose the highest layer q such that sq ∼ ρ1 ∼ ρ. With q fixed, we choose the highest layer or
refined sub-layer q ′ ≤ q, such that a significant portion of these sq particle lines are connected to each other
via layers between q and q ′ . Then, we may ignore all the layers above q + 1 and below q ′ , and also ignore the
layers in [q ′ + 1, q] (with suitable tree structures in these intermediate layers merged to layer q + 1), which
results in a two-layer molecule M′ with only layers q ′ and q + 1.
   The key here is that, by definition of q ′ , sufficiently many of the particle lines in r(Fq ) are connected to
each other precisely in layer q ′ (after possibly forming harmless tree structures in layers [q ′ + 1, q] which are
merged to layer q + 1). On the other hand, with at most sq+1 ≪ sq exceptions, all of these sq particle lines
are also connected to each other precisely in layer q + 1 (see Propositions 8.5 and 10.8). Therefore, we get
enough many (≳ sq ≳ ρ) recollisions by considering the two layers q ′ and q + 1 in the molecule M′ only,
which is sufficient for the proof in part II below. This process is called layer selection and is presented in
Definitions 10.12 and 10.14, and Propositions 10.13 and 10.15.
   Part II: analysis of two-layer model. Now we have reduced to a molecule M′ := MU D consisting of an
upper layer MU and a lower layer MD , which are both forests; moreover the number of recollisions in M′ ,
which is provided by the UD connections connecting an atom in MU to an atom in MD , is ≳ ρ. Our goal is
to design an algorithm that cuts M′ into elementary molecules and satisfies (2.7).
            LONG TIME DERIVATION OF THE BOLTZMANN EQUATION FROM HARD SPHERE DYNAMICS                                    21


    We start with a basic algorithm denoted by UP (see Definition 10.1 and Propositions 10.2–10.4); this
can be applied to the initial molecule M′ or any intermediate molecules M′′ that is formed by applying any
cutting sequence to M′ . The feature of this algorithm is that it minimizes the number of bad {4}-molecules,
but does not always grant sufficiently many good {33}-molecules. However, in the case when M′′ contains
many degree 3 atoms that are separated by degree 4 atoms, i.e. when M′′ contains no degree 2 atom, and
the subgraph Z ⊆ M′′ of degree 3 atoms has many connected components, it can be shown that a suitable
variation of the algorithm UP (denoted by 3COMPUP) can indeed grant enough {33}-molecules, see
Definition 11.6 and Proposition 11.7.
    Now, let us also make the auxiliary assumption that there is no or few 2-connections which means an atom
in MU (or MD ) that is connected to two atoms in MD (or MU ); the case of sufficiently many 2-connections
can be treated using another variant of UP called 2CONNUP, see Definition 11.1 and Propositions 11.2–
11.3. Then, we may consider the following dichotomy: either #cp (X) ≪ ρ or #cp (X) ≳ ρ, where #cp (X)
is the number of connected components of the subgraph X ⊆ MU , and X is the set of atoms in MU that
are connected to an atom in MD by a bond. In the case #cp (X) ≳ ρ, we can first cut MD as free (and cut
it into elementary components), so each atom in X now becomes degree 3, and then apply the algorithm
3COMPUP to the rest of the molecule MU ; this leads to #{33} ≳ #cp (X) ≳ ρ using Proposition 11.7,
while keeping #{4} under control, which suffices to prove (2.7).
    On the other hand, assume #cp (X) ≪ ρ. In this case, the strategy is to perform a variation of UP in the
upper layer MU , and perform a completely different, greedy algorithm in the lower layer MD . In this greedy
algorithm, we will remove any possible {33}-molecule, and any possible {343}-molecule (i.e. a degree 4 atom
connected to two degree 3 atoms, which can be further divided into a {3}-molecule and a {33}-molecule) in
MD , immediately when they occur, and until no such structures exist.
    The overall algorithm in the case #cp (X) ≪ ρ, called MAINUD (see Definition 11.10 and Proposition
11.11) goes like follows. Each time we cut a lowest atom in MU ; if this causes any {33} or {343} structure to
occur in MD 8, then we keep cutting them until no more such structure exists, and then go back to MU and
cut the next lowest atom, and so on. The advantage of this strategy is that, due to the greedy algorithm
in the MD layer, each UD connection (i.e. each recollision) will actually contribute to a {33}-component,
leading to #{33} ≳ ρ. On the other hand, since we are applying the variation of UP in MU in which we
always cut the lowest atom even when it has degree 4, this may lead to more bad {4}-molecules compared to
the original UP; however this loss #{4} can be bounded precisely by #cp(X) ≪ ρ, by means of the auxiliary
Proposition 11.5. This completes the proof of (2.7).

Remark 2.1. We make two remarks concerning the above discussion of the algorithm:
    (1) We have assumed above that every atom in M represents a collision. In reality we also have overlaps,
        which are represented by O-atoms (Definition 7.1). In principle they are easier to deal with than
        collisions (C-atoms), but many of the notions and procedures in our algorithm need to be adjusted
        to accommodate for the presence of these O-atoms, which leads to formal complications in some
        definitions and proofs. This includes for example the definition of cutting (Definition 8.6), and the
        notions of ov-segments, ov-components etc. (Definition 8.2), which occur in many proofs in Sections
        10 and 11.
    (2) Concerning the estimates for {33}-molecules, in reality, we need to treat certain degenerate cases
        separately. Depending on the {33A} and {33B} sub-types, we have strongly degenerate (Proposition
        10.5) and weakly degenerate cases (Proposition 10.16). Basically, in these degenerate cases we
        cannot gain enough power from the {33} molecules, but the degeneracy conditions put strong enough
        restrictions on the relevant vector ze and time tn variables, which turn the previously normal {3}
        molecules to good ones (see for example (5) in Definition 9.5), and again leads to enough power gain
        εα(L,Γ)ρ .

   8Strictly speaking, before executing this algorithm, we may need to perform a pre-processing step to make sure that M
                                                                                                                         D
does not contain {33} or {343} structures, see Definition 11.8 and Proposition 11.9.
22                                    YU DENG, ZAHER HANI, AND XIAO MA


2.4. The Boltzmann approximation and the truncation error. With the bound (♡2 ) for cumulants
established, it now remains to prove, by induction in ℓ, that the single-variable function f A (ℓτ ) occurring in
(1.20) are indeed approximations to the actual Boltzmann solution n(ℓτ ), in L∞       x,v type spaces (Proposition
                                                        A
5.2). By discussions in Section 2.1, we know that f (ℓτ ) is given by an explicit series expansion of iterated
time integrals that involve only f A ((ℓ − 1)τ ), therefore this part follows from essentially the same arguments
in the short-time theory. We identify those leading terms that do not contain recollision, and those sub-
leading terms that contain recollision. For leading terms we match them term by term with the Duhamel
expansion of n(ℓτ ) in terms of n((ℓ − 1)τ ); note that our cumulant expansion method (see (4.37)) actually
leads to extra terms in addition to those occurring in Boltzmann Duhamel expansions, but these extra terms
turn our to cancel each other, as shown in Proposition 12.6. As for the sub-leading terms, they can be proved
to be vanishing by applying a very simple algorithm (Section 12.4), since we only have one single layer and
the number of recollisions in each cluster is bounded by Γ. All these are contained in Section 12.
   Finally we discuss the proof of (♡3 ) for the truncation error term, which is done in Section 13. First, we
recall that the needed gain, represented by α in (♡3 ), should be in excess of (d − 1). This is because the label
of every particle in the cluster in D is allowed to vary (compared to the standard calculations where the label
of one of the particles is fixed), which leads to an extra loss factor ε−(d−1) multipled by the left hand side of
(1.30) accounting for the choice of the label of this particle. The main difficulty in getting the needed gain,
as already mentioned in Section 2.2, is the possibility that a small group of particles within distance O(ε)
of each other producing many recollisions within time O(ε). To deal with this, we rely on a new ingredient,
namely the absolute upper bound on the number of collisions given the number of particles, which is proved
in Burago-Ferleger-Kononenko [15]. Basically, if Γ is sufficiently large, then these Γ recollisions must involve
sufficiently many particles, with each new particle providing a {33}-molecule in a suitable algorithm.
   The algorithm in Section 13 is based on a two-layer molecule formed by A+ and A where A is (almost) a
tree and A+ is arbitrary; see Proposition 13.4 (which actually involves three layers A+ , A and A− , but in
reality we only exploit two of them). The main algorithm, called MAINTRUP, is similar to the MAINUD
algorithm used in the proof of (♡2 ), where each time we cut a lowest atom in A+ , and keep cutting any {33}
or {343} structure in A that occur. This will then guarantee a lower bound for the number of {33}-molecules,
provided we have a lower bound for the number #+         conn of bonds connecting an atom in A to an atom in
a specific subset X + (A) ⊆ A+ . This last lower bound is then provided by (the inverse function of) the
Burago-Ferleger-Kononenko upper bound in [15], see Proposition 13.2. For more details of the algorithm,
see Definition 13.9 and Proposition 13.10.
2.5. Comparison between wave and particle derivations. As mentioned in Section 1.2, the proof in
this paper is deeply inspired by that in the wave setting by the first two authors [24]. Here we make a brief
comparison between the two proofs, in terms of their similarities and differences. In the case of wave kinetic
theory, the microscopic system in [24] is given by the nonlinear Schrödinger (NLS) equation on a torus TdL
of size L, with a nonlinearity of strength α. The lowest-order nontrivial statistical quantity is the second
moment of the Fourier modes E|b     u(t, k)|2 , where u
                                                      b(t, k) is the Fourier transform of the solution u(t, x) to
the NLS equation, which is to be approximated by the solution of the (homogeneous) wave kinetic equation
(1.19), in the limit L → ∞ and α → 0. This kinetic limit is taken under an appropriate choice of scaling law
between L and α, namely α = L−γ , where γ ∈ (0, 1) in the homogeneous setting, see [24].
   At a conceptual level, the two proofs are very similar. The fundamental difficulty represented by the
divergence of the series expansion on the long time interval [0, tfin ] is the same, and the same goes for the
need to create an arrow of time where, in both cases, the microscopic system is time-reversible but the
limiting equation is irreversible. The idea of performing a partial cumulant expansion, where one refrains
from expanding the lowest order statistic (the f A here, and the second moments in the wave case) but
expands the cumulants all the way to time t = 0, is also the same. This keeps track of all the relevant
collisions or interactions on the long time interval [0, tfin ] that affect the cumulants at time tfin .
   Even at a more technical level, there are uncanny similarities between the two proofs. First, both proofs
start with a truncation of the dynamics in order to control the size of the diagrams appearing in the cumulant
           LONG TIME DERIVATION OF THE BOLTZMANN EQUATION FROM HARD SPHERE DYNAMICS                             23


expansions. Also, in both proofs, the needed cumulant estimate is reduced to a combinatorial problem
that is resolved through an elaborate algorithm. This combinatorial problem, in both cases, involves the
combinatorial structure of molecules, which are formed by atoms that represent collisions or interactions.
While the algorithms are vastly different in the wave case and particle case, there is an important similarity
in the gain mechanism. As shown in Section 2.3, the basic unit of gain here is the special structure of
{33}-molecules; similarly, in the wave case, the basic unit of gain also comes from the {33}-molecules and
the associated five-vector counting problem.
   All this being said, the analogy between the particle and wave kinetic theories has its limitations, and
despite the above similarities, each proof carries its own distinct set of challenges. In the wave case, a large
box limit needs to be performed to go from the discrete Fourier space of TdL to the continuum setting of the
wave kinetic equation (1.19). This links the wave kinetic theory to some deep number theory problems, which
seems to be absent in the particle setting. Also, the Feynman diagram expansion for the NLS equation turns
out to converge only conditionally and not absolutely, and requires highly nontrivial cancellations between
arbitrarily large diagrams, which is another distinctive feature and novelty of [24]. On the other hand, the
cumulant expansion in the particle setting is technically more involved, due to the application of inclusion-
exclusion principle and the resulting notion of overlaps, which does not seem to be have an analog in the
wave case. Also the main algorithm in the particle case seems to be conceptually more complicated than
the corresponding algorithm in the wave case, which may be due to the singular nature of the δ integrals in
IN (M) compared to the counting problems in [24].

used throughout the paper.

         a graph G with its node set F = S(G). We also use the notion |A| or #A to denote the cardinality
         of finite sets A, and use |A| to denote the Lebesgue measure of Borel sets A ⊆ Rp for some p. Define
         as usual X+ = max(X, 0) and X− = − min(X, 0), and tensor product f ⊗A (zA ) = j∈A f (zj ).

3.5. Volume preserving properties. In this section, we prove a volume preserving property for the ex-
tended and truncated dynamics. We remark that, unlike the hard-sphere dynamics HN (t), the modified
             E          Λ,Γ
dynamics HN    (t) and HN   (t) are not injective. However, they are still locally volume preserving in the
following sense:
Lemma 3.13. The domain R2dN (up to Lebesgue zero sets) can be divided into countably many disjoint
                                                                            E          Λ,Γ
open sets, such that for each open set and any t ≥ 0, the mapping HN          (t) and HN   (t) is a volume preserving
                                                                                    E            Λ,Γ
bijection between this open set and its image. For the flow operators SN (t) and SN                  (t), the following
identities are true for any function f = f (z)

                                        R2dN                         R2dN

Proof. For both E- and T-dynamics, by Proposition 3.9 (1), we know that they satisfy the two requirements
of modified dynamics (Definition 3.1 (1)). The first requirement (Definition 3.1 (1a)) implies that the number
of collisions is finite on any fixed [0, t]. By listing all the collisions and time ordering them, we can divide
R2dN into a countable union of disjoint open sets (Oα )α∈N , where on each Oα the E- and T-dynamics contain
                                                                                                              E
only a fixed set of collisions between fixed particles with a fixed time ordering. Clearly the mapping HN       (t)
         Λ,Γ
(and HN (t)) is injective on Oα , and hence is a diffeomorphism onto its image. We shall prove that this
mapping is volume preserving on Oα .
                                                  E                                                 Λ,Γ

same). To prove the volume preserving property it is enough to show that

then the Jacobian of Ht equals to 1, which implies (3.27) for general f by the change of variables formula.)
    This allows us to write Ht , restricted to this small neighborhood, as the composition of Htj −tj−1 for some
fixed {tj }1≤j≤m such that exactly one collision happens on each time interval [tj−1 , tj ]. We can then reduce
to the case where the E-dynamics contains a single collision on [0, t], say between particles 1 and 2, and
denote their coordinates by (z1 , z2 ), with initial configuration zj (0) = zj0 . Since all the other particles are
undergoing linear transport, which is volume preserving and does not interact with the dynamics of particles
1 and 2, we may then neglect the other particles, and assume N = 2.

|x01 − x02 | > ε. Moreover, the collision time s is a smooth function s = Φ(z10 , z20 ) of the initial configuration.

                                            4. Formulas of cumulants
     Recall the decomposition
                                            fs (t, zs ) = fes (t, zs ) + fserr (t, zs )                                              (4.1)
in Section 3. In this section, we derive an upper bound for the fserr (tfin , zs ) (Proposition 4.9) and a cumulant
expansion formula for fes (t, zs ). Such cumulant formula takes the form
                                             X         ⊗([s]\H)
                              fes (t, zs ) =   f A (t)           EH (t, zQ ) + Errs (t),                                             (4.2)
                                                H⊆[s]

where Err is a sufficiently small, negligible error, and EH are the cumulants that measure how far away the
correlation function fes is from being factorized (i.e. how far away the states of different particles are from
being independent). See Proposition 4.21 for more details.

4.1. Partial dynamics and splitting of flow operators. In this section, we introduce the notion of
                                                                              Λ,Γ
partial dynamics zλ (t). Under certain conditions, we show that the flow map HN   (t) splits into product of
                   Λ,Γ      Q k     Λ,Γ                                                     E
partial dynamics HN (t) = j=1 Hλj (t), and similar results are also true for SN (t) and SN    (t).

Definition 4.1 (Partial dynamics). Recall z := zN = (z1 , · · · , zN ) as in Definition 2.2 (4). Let λ be a
subset of [N ] and zλ = (zj )j∈λ . Given initial configuration z(0) = z 0 , let z(t), z E (t) and z Λ,Γ (t) be the O-,
E- and T-dynamics respectively, then we can define the following notions.
           LONG TIME DERIVATION OF THE BOLTZMANN EQUATION FROM HARD SPHERE DYNAMICS                                             31


    (1) The partial dynamics zλ (t), zλE (t) and zλΛ,Γ (t). Consider λ ⊆ [N ] as a set of particles. We define
        the partial dynamics zλ (t) (and similarly zλE (t) and zλΛ,Γ (t), but refer to Definitions 3.5 and 3.7
        instead) as follows: it follows the same rule as the O-dynamics (Definition 1.1 (3)) but only taking
        into account particles in λ, and with initial configuration zλ0 = (zj0 )j∈λ .
    (2) The full dynamics. By setting λ = [N ] in the partial dynamics zλ (t), we retrieve the dynamics
        z(t) = zN (t) of all N particles. We refer to z(t) as the full dynamics. This similarly applies to
        zλE (t) and zλΛ,Γ (t).
    (3) The partial flow maps Hλ (t), HλE (t) and HλΛ,Γ (t). We define the partial flow maps Hλ (t) as
        follows: these are defined similarly to HN (t) in (1.4), such that Hλ (t)(zλ0 ) = zλ (t). We shall also
        extend Hλ (t) to involve zλ0 c which acts as a paremeter, i.e. Hλ (t)z 0 = Hλ (t)(zλ0 , zλ0 c ) = (zλ (t), zλ0 c ).
        Similar definitions also apply to HλE (t) and HλΛ,Γ (t).
    (4) The partial flow operators Sλ (t), SλE (t) and SλΛ,Γ (t). We define the partial flow operators Sλ (t),
        SλE (t) and SλΛ,Γ (t), similar to (3.4), as follows:

              (Sλ (t)f )(zλ ) := f ((Hλ (t))−1 (zλ )),                                                                       (4.3)

Remark 4.2. We remark that in general zλ (t) ̸= (zN (t))λ , where the left hand side is defined by the partial
dynamics, and the right hand side is defined by selecting λ components of the full dynamics (nonetheless see
(♢) in the proof of Lemma 4.5).

  The following definition introduces the notion of cluster interaction.

Definition 4.3 (Clusters and cluster interaction). Recall z := zN = (z1 , · · · , zN ) as in Definition 2.2 (4).
Let λ be a subset of [N ] and zλ = (zj )j∈λ . Given an initial configuration z(0) = z 0 , consider the modified
dynamics z ∗ (t) which equals one of z E (t) or z Λ,Γ (t) in [0, τ ] and the associated partial dynamics, then we
can define the following notions.
    (1) Full of collisions. We say λ is full of collisions in [0, τ ] for initial configuration z 0 , if the interaction
        graph G∗λ (τ ) of the partial dynamics zλ∗ (t) is connected.
    (2) Interaction between clusters. Given two disjoint subsets λ, λ′ ⊆ [N ], we say λ does not interact
        with λ′ , and write λ ̸∼ λ′ , if the interaction graph G∗λ∪λ′ (τ ) of the partial dynamics zλ∪λ ∗
                                                                                                            ′ (t) contains

        no edge between nodes in λ and nodes in λ . If not, we say λ interacts with λ , and write λ ∼ λ′ .
                                                         ′                                         ′

    (3) Several indicator functions. Let z ∗ (t) be either z E (t) or z Λ,Γ (t) in [0, τ ], then we introduce the
        following indicator functions.
         (a) 1∗λ (zλ0 ). This function equals 1 if and only if λ is full of collisions in z ∗ (t).
         (b) 1∗λ̸∼λ′ (zλ∪λ
                        0
                           ′ ) and 1λ∼λ′ (zλ∪λ′ ). These functions equal 1 if and only if λ and λ do not or do
                                    ∗       0                                                             ′

             interact with each other respectively.
         (c) 1∗λ∧(rec≤γ) (zλ0 ) and 1∗λ∧(rec=γ) (zλ0 ). Recall that χ∗λ (τ ) is the recollisional number defined in
             Definition 3.4 (3). Then define 1∗λ∧(rec≤γ) to be 1 if and only if λ is full of collisions and
             χ∗λ (τ ) ≤ γ. Also define 1∗λ∧(rec=γ) to be 1 if and only if there exists t ∈ [0, τ ] such that G∗λ (t) is
             connected and χ∗λ (t) = γ.
32                                       YU DENG, ZAHER HANI, AND XIAO MA

                                          E        E      E              E

          tions defined in (3), when z ∗ (t) is taken to be the truncated dynamics z Λ,Γ (t). Note that we have
          omitted the superscript (Λ, Γ) for simplicity, because these quantities occur a lot in the proof below.

Remark 4.4. For the same initial data z 0 , different dynamics give different clusters and indicator functions.
In general, 1λ ̸= 1E
                   λ.

   The following lemma demonstrates that the full dynamics can be factorized into partial dynamics on each
cluster. This is of crucial importance in the derivation of the cumulant formula.

Lemma 4.5. Consider either the E-dynamics or the (Λ, Γ)-dynamics, with given initial configuration z 0 =
  0
zN  . Then, up to Lebesgue zero sets, the clusters for z 0 in these dynamics at time τ are given by {λ1 , · · · , λk },
if and only if the following conditions hold:
      (1) For 1 ≤ j ≤ k, λj is full of collisions given z 0 .
      (2) For 1 ≤ j < j ′ ≤ k, λj ̸∼ λj ′ (in the sense of E- or T-dynamics) given z 0 .
                                                                                                     Sk
      (3) {λ1 , · · · , λk } is a partition of [N ] in the sense of Definition 2.2 (6) (in particular j=1 λj = [N ]).
If the clusters for z 0 at t = τ in E-dynamics or T-dynamics are given by {λ1 , · · · , λk }, then we have
                                   Y k                               Y k              
                        E     0           E      0         Λ,Γ    0            Λ,Γ           0
                      HN (t)zN =        Hλj (t) zN , or HN (t)zN =          Hλj (t) zN                            (4.7)
                                       j=1                                         j=1

for t ∈ [0, τ ] respectively.

Proof. We consider the case of (Λ, Γ)-dynamics, which is more complicated. The case of E-dynamics follows
from the same and much easier proof. Recall that zλΛ,Γ (t) is the partial dynamics of z ∗ (t), and (z Λ,Γ (t))λ :=
(zjΛ,Γ (t))j∈λ is the collection of components of z Λ,Γ (t). Note that in general, (z Λ,Γ (t))λ ̸= zλΛ,Γ (t).
     Step 1. In this step, we first prove the following simple property of T-dynamics:
     (♢) In the dynamics z Λ,Γ (t), if λ ⊆ [N ] is such that no particle in λ has any collision with any particle
         in λc , then (z Λ,Γ (t))λ = zλΛ,Γ (t).
     To prove (♢), define
                                         t∗ = sup t : (z Λ,Γ (t))λ = zλΛ,Γ (t) ,
                                                 

then it suffices to show that t∗ = τ . If t∗ < τ , by left continuity we have (z Λ,Γ (t))λ = zλΛ,Γ (t) for all t ≤ t∗ .
Using also that no collision happens between particles in λ and λc , we know that the interaction graph
GΛ,Γ                                                 Λ,Γ
  λ (t∗ ) is exactly equal to the subgraph of GN (t∗ ) formed by the nodes in λ, which is also disconnected
                             Λ,Γ
to its complement in GN          (t∗ ). Then, we know that a collision happens at time t∗ between particles i ∈ λ
and j ∈ λ in the full dynamics z Λ,Γ (t), if and only if the same collision happens in the partial dynamics
zλΛ,Γ (t). This is because, due to the collision rule of T-dynamics (Definition 3.7 (3)), whether such collision
happens depends only on the values of (xi (t∗ ), xj (t∗ ), vi (t∗ ), vj (t∗ )) and on the structure of the connected
components of the interaction graph containing i and j, all of which are the same for both the full and the
partial dynamics.
    Using the above statement about possible collisions at time t∗ , we may evolve in time for a small increment
before the next possible collision, to conclude that (z Λ,Γ (t))λ = zλΛ,Γ (t) for t > t∗ sufficiently close to t∗ ,
contradicting the definition of t∗ .
    Step 2. In this step, we show that (1)–(3) hold if the clusters of z Λ,Γ (t) are given by {λ1 , · · · , λk }. In
fact, by definition of clusters and (♢), we know that no collision happens between any particle in λj and any
particle in λj ′ in the full dynamics z Λ,Γ (t). By (♢), this implies the same result for the partial dynamics
zλΛ,Γ
   j ∪λj ′
           (t), which is (2). Similarly we can prove (1), since the interaction graph GΛ,Γλ (t) is just the subgraph
of GΛ,Γ
    N (t) formed by the nodes in λ, and (3) is true by definition of partition.
            LONG TIME DERIVATION OF THE BOLTZMANN EQUATION FROM HARD SPHERE DYNAMICS                                            33


    Step 3. In this step, we show that (1)–(3) implies that clusters of z Λ,Γ (t) are given by {λ1 , · · · , λk }. We
first show that under (1)–(3), there is no collision between any λj and λj ′ in z Λ,Γ (t). Assume the contrary,
there must exist a first collision that happens at time t∗ between two clusters λj1 and λj2 , such that no
collision between any λj and λj ′ happens before time t∗ . Let the next collision time in the z Λ,Γ (t) dynamics
be t∗ +δ, then in the time interval [0, t∗ +δ], there is no collision between particles in λj1 ∪λj2 and (λj1 ∪λj2 )c .
By (♢) we know that (z Λ,Γ (t))λj ∪λj′ = zλΛ,Γj ∪λj

4.3. The cumulant expansion formula. In this subsection, we state the next main results of Section
4, namely Propositions 4.17 and 4.21. These propositions give the cumulant expansion formulas for the
truncated correlation function fes (t) defined in (3.22). We leave the proof to Section 4.4.
4.3.1. Definition of cluster forests. In this subsection, we introduce the definitions and related concepts of
cluster forests. For the purpose of performing multi-layer expansions, we will need to relabel the particles
(which are currently labeled by natural numbers 1, · · · , s in (3.22)) by 3-digit strings, where the first digit
indicates the layer, the second digit indicates the cluster and the third digit indicates the particle. This leads
to the concept of particle label set.
Definition 4.10 (Particle label sets). Let Nk∞ to be the set of length k strings of elements in N+ ∪ {∞},
which is linearly ordered by the usual lexicographic ordering. Given a number L, we introduce the following
     (1) PL[L] and CL[L]. The particle label set PL[L] and cluster label set CL[L] are defined as
         PL[L] = {p : p = (ℓ, j, p) ∈ [L] × N2∞ } and CL[L] = {j : j = (ℓ, j) ∈ [L] × N∞ }.
     (2) PL(ℓ) and CL(ℓ). For ℓ ≤ L, the ℓ-th layer particle/cluster labeling sets PL(ℓ) and CL(ℓ) are
         defined as the set of p ∈ PL[L] or j ∈ CL[L] such that ℓ is the first digit of p or j. In other words,
         PL(ℓ) = {ℓ} × N2∞ and CL(ℓ) = {ℓ} × N∞ respectively.
     (3) The interpretation of labels. Given a string p = (ℓ, j, p) ∈ PL[L] or j = (ℓ, j) ∈ CL[L], the first digit
         ℓ represents the layer, indicating the time interval [(ℓ − 1)τ, ℓτ ] where the particles or clusters are
         located. The second digit j is the label of a cluster within a layer, and the third digit p is the label
         of a particle within a cluster.
     (4) The ordering. All sets defined above are subsets of N3∞ or N2∞ and have a natural ordering structure
         from N3∞ or N2∞ .
           LONG TIME DERIVATION OF THE BOLTZMANN EQUATION FROM HARD SPHERE DYNAMICS                              37


  Now we define the cluster forests.
Definition 4.11 (Single layer cluster forest). Given a positive integer ℓ ∈ [L], we define a single layer
cluster forest (on layer ℓ) to be the structure F = (F, E, R, C, (λr )r∈R , (mj )j∈F ) and an abstract single
layer cluster forest (on layer ℓ) to be the structure consisting only of F0 = (F, E, R, C), which satisfy the
following requirements:
    (1) (F, E, R) is a rooted forest, where F , E and R are the sets of nodes, edges and roots. We assume
        F ⊆ CL(ℓ) and elements of F are called clusters. We require that the root set R consists of |R|
        least elements in F according to the order of CL(ℓ). We call elements of R the root clusters.
    (2) C is a collection of unordered node pairs {j, j ′ } ̸∈ E where j, j ′ ∈ F and j = r ∈ R and j ′ belongs
        to the subtree rooted at some R ∋ r ′ < r, such that the graph with node set F and edge set E ∪ C
        is still a forest (i.e. graph without cycle).
    (3) The sets (λr )r∈R are disjoint nonempty subsets of PL[L], and we require that λr ∩([ℓ]×N×N∞ ) = ∅
        for any r ∈ R. Moreover, for each j ∈ F , mj is a nonnegative integer such that mj > 0 for j ∈ F \R.
   A heuristic explanation of the structure F is as follows. In F0 = (F, E, R, C), elements in R are root
clusters that contain at least one root particle; elements in F (resp. in F (r)) are those clusters that are
connected by overlaps to these root clusters (resp. to the root cluster r), and edges in E represent these
overlaps; edges in C represent extra overlaps between clusters that are connected to different root clusters.
In F, the λr is the set of root particles in a root cluster r, and mj is (the cardinality of) the set of non-root
particles in j.
Remark 4.12. Note that F0 only encodes the clusters and the edges amongst them, whereas F also encodes
the particles in each cluster as described by Definition 4.13 below. The requirement λr ∩ ({j} × [mj ]) = ∅
is useful in (4.27).
Definition 4.13 (More on cluster forests). Given a layer ℓ cluster forest F = (F, E, R, C, (λr )r∈R , (mj )j∈F ),
we define the following associated notions, see Figure 7 for an illustration.
    (1) The forest F and the subtree F (r). Below we will always use the symbol F to denote the rooted
        forest (F, E, R). Given a root r ∈ R, denote the subtree rooted at r by F (r).
    (2) Clusters and particles of a cluster. Elements j ∈ F are called clusters. For a cluster j ∈ F , define
        the set of particles in the cluster j to be
                        (
                         λj ∪ ({j} × [mj ]), if j ∈ R;
               [mj ]+ =                                    mcj := |[mj ]+ | = 1j∈R · |λj | + mj .      (4.27)
                         {j} × [mj ],          if j ̸∈ R,
        Note that {j} × [mj ] ⊆ PL(ℓ) by definition. We call the elements in λj (j ∈ R) the root particles.
        The particles will be denoted by p. Note that for j ∈ R, the first two coordinates of the root particles
        need not (and typically do not) agree with j.
    (3) The notions p(F), r(F), (p\r)(F), |F|p , |F|r and |F|p\r . Define the particle set, root particle set
        and non-root particle set for F to be
                      [                     [                                   [
             p(F) :=      [mj ]+ , r(F) :=      λr , (p\r)(F) := p(F)\r(F) =       ({j} × [mj ]).        (4.28)
                      j∈F                   r∈R                                  j∈F

        Define also |F|p := |p(F)|, |F|r := |r(F)| and |F|p\r := |(p\r)(F)|. When |r(F)| = 1 (and in particular
        |R| = 1), we refer to F as cluster trees, and write T and T instead of F and F etc.
    (4) a(F) and q(F). Define the following subsets of r(F):
                                                [
                                       a(F) :=      λr : r ∈ R, |λr | > 1                                (4.29)
        and                [
                 q(F) :=     λr : r ∈ R, |λr | = 1, F (r) is connected to another tree by C ,                (4.30)
                                                                                                 ′       ′
        where F (r) connected to another tree by C, means that there exists j ∈ F (r) and j ∈ F (r ) such
        that r ̸= r ′ and {j, j ′ } ∈ C.
38                                      YU DENG, ZAHER HANI, AND XIAO MA


     (5) bF (H ′ ). For a subset H ′ ⊆ p(F), define bF (H ′ ) to be the union of all λr such that r ∈ R, |λr | = 1,
         F (r) is not connected to another tree by C, and there exists j ∈ F (r) satisfying [mj ]+ ∩ H ′ ̸= ∅.

Remark 4.14. The notions of a(F), q(F) and bF (H ′ ) in Definition 4.13 (4)–(5) will play important roles in
later proofs. Heuristically, they mean the followings: a(F) is the set of root particles that are connected to
another root particle by collisions; q(F) is the set of root particles that are connected to another root particle
by overlaps; bF (H ′ ) is the set of root particles that are connected to another (root or non-root) particle in
H ′ by collisions or overlaps.




         Figure 7. Illustration of a single-layer cluster forest (Definition 4.13). Here layer ℓ = 1,
         clusters j are labeled (1, j) for 1 ≤ j ≤ 9 with 1 ≤ j ≤ 3 being root clusters. The edges in
         E are indicated by segments, and node pairs in C by red dashed segments. The top blue
         thick line represents the root particle set r(F) formed by λr , and the bottom yellow thick
         line represents the particle set p(F) formed by [mj ]+ , and the purple part represents the
         subset H ′ ⊆ p(F) in Definition 4.13 (5). Note that the element of λ(1,1) does not belong to
         a(F), q(F) or bF (H ′ ) in this picture.

4.3.4. The statement of the multi-layer cumulant formula. We now state the multi-layer cumulant expansion
formula for fes (t), namely Proposition 4.21 below. This follows by iterating Proposition 4.17.

Proof of Proposition 4.21 assuming Proposition 4.17. The formula (4.48) for fe|Q| and the bound (4.51) on
Err2ℓ follow directly from Proposition 4.17. The formula for f A and the formula (4.49) for EH (ℓτ, zH ) follows
by applying the formulas (4.37) and (4.38) in Proposition 4.17 inductively in ℓ. The formula (4.50) for Err1ℓ
follows by inserting (4.49) into (4.39).                                                                      □
4.4. Proof of the main formulas. In this subsection we prove Proposition 4.17. The proof will be divided
into several steps, each occupying a subsection from Section 4.4.1 to Section 4.4.4:
         • In Section 4.4.1 we perform cluster expansions and prove an intermediate result (4.66).
         • In Section 4.4.2 we perform inclusion-exclusion arguments and prove Lemma 4.27.
         • In Section 4.4.3 we eliminate the W factors and prove Lemma 4.37.
         • In Section 4.4.4 we establish cumulant expansions and finish the proof of Proposition 4.17.
    The proof within each subsection will also be divided into several sub-steps. We also remark that, in all
these proofs, we will need to perform suitable relabelings of particles and variables, to match the structures
introduced in Section 4.3. For example, in Sections 4.4.1 we shall derive (4.66), which is expressed in terms
of fes (ℓτ, zs ); then in Section 4.4.3 (Lemma 4.37) we will relabel this expression as feQ (ℓτ, zQ ) to match (4.36)
in Proposition 4.17.

4.4.2. The inclusion-exclusion formula. In (4.66), the indicator function 1≤j<j ′ ≤k 1[mj ]+ ̸∼[mj′ ]+ character-
                                                                                                    Q

izes the interaction between different T-clusters. In this subsection we will expand it using inclusion-exclusion
principle to decouple the interactions. Start with the following definitions.
Definition 4.23 (Cluster forests without layer). A cluster forest without layer F or an abstract cluster
forest without layer F0 are defined to be F = (F, E, R, C, (λr )r∈R , (mj )j∈F ) or F0 = (F, E, R, C), which
satisfy the the same requirements as Definition 4.11 (1)–(3), but replace the vector j by the integer j ∈ [k]
(and r by r) and the set CL(ℓ) by [k] in Definition 4.11 (1). We also replace the PL[L] by N in Definition
4.11 (3), and replace the {j} × [mj ] in (4.27) by {j} × [mj ] in (4.64). Given any single layer cluster forest
F, if we remove the layer digit ℓ in the labelings and also suitably relabel the root particles, then we get a
cluster forest without layer.
Definition 4.24 (Set of cluster forests Fsc ,Λ,k and Fserrc ,Λ,k
                                                                 ). Given Λ, define Fsc ,Λ,k and Fserr
                                                                                                    c ,Λ,k
                                                                                                           to be set
of all abstract cluster forests without layer F0 = (F, E, R, C) satisfying the following.
      (1) The node set F satisfies [sc ] ⊆ F ⊆ [k], and the root set R = [sc ].
      (2) For F ∈ Fsc ,Λ,k , we have |F (r)| < Λ for all r ∈ R.
          For F ∈ Fserrc ,Λ,k
                              , there is a unique r0 such that |F (r)| < Λ for r < r0 , that |F (r0 )| = Λ, and that
          |F (r)| = 1 for r > r0 .
            LONG TIME DERIVATION OF THE BOLTZMANN EQUATION FROM HARD SPHERE DYNAMICS                                    45


Definition 4.25 (Set of cluster forests Fsc ,Λ and Fserr c ,Λ
                                                              ). Define Fsc ,Λ (resp. Fserr
                                                                                         c ,Λ
                                                                                              ) to be the collection
of all abstract cluster forests without layer F0 = (F, E, R, C) that belong to Fsc ,Λ,k (resp. Fserr c ,Λ,k
                                                                                                            ) for some
k, and satisfy that F = [|F |].

Definition 4.26 (Equivalence of cluster forests). We introduce the following definitions.
    (1) Equivalence of cluster forests. Let F = (F, E, R, C, (λr ), (mj )) and F′ = (F ′ , E ′ , R′ , C ′ , (λ′r ), (m′j ))
        be two cluster forests without layer. We say F is equivalent to F′ , denoted by F ∼ F′ , if R = R′ ,
        and there exists a bijection q : F → F ′ such that
         (a) For each r ∈ R we have q(r) = r.
         (b) For each j, j ′ ∈ F we have that {j, j ′ } ∈ E ⇔ {q(j), q(j ′ )} ∈ E ′ and {j, j ′ } ∈ C if and only if
              {q(j), q(j ′ )} ∈ C ′ .
         (c) For each r ∈ R and each j, j ′ ∈ F (r) with j < j ′ we must have q(j) < q(j ′ ).
         (d) For each r ∈ R we have λr = λ′r , and for each j ∈ F we have mj = m′q(j) .
        For F0 = (F, E, R, C) and F′0 = (F ′ , E ′ , R′ , C ′ ), we can define equivalence similarly, with q only
        satisfying (1a)–(1c).
    (2) Sum over equivalent classes. Define [F] to be the equivalence class containing F. Now, if Exp =
        Exp(F) is a function of F that is constant on each equivalence class (so Exp(F) = Exp([F])), then we
        can unambiguously define the sum
                                         X                  X
                                             Exp(F) =             Exp([F]),                                        (4.67)
                                            [F]∈A                [F]∈A

          where A is any set of equivalence classes.

   We now state the main result of this subsection, whose proof will be left to the end of this subsection.

defined in (4.64), and fix sc as above. Then we have the following decomposition:

   Before proving Lemma 4.27, we first make some preparations. Let Gk be the set of all simple graphs G
with node set S(G) = [k] (Definition 2.2 (7)). For any 1 ≤ sc ≤ k, we shall define a mapping πsc , which
maps any G ∈ Gk to a tuple F0 = (F, E, R, C) in Fsc ,Λ,k ∪ Fserr
                                                              c ,Λ,k
                                                                     , in the next definition.
46                                        YU DENG, ZAHER HANI, AND XIAO MA


Definition 4.28. Let G ∈ Gk be a graph such that S(G) = [k], we shall define the tuple
                                   πsc (G) = F0 = (F, E, R, C) ∈ Fsc ,Λ,k ∪ Fserr
                                                                               c ,Λ,k
                                                                                                                        (4.72)
by the following constructions.
    First, define R = [sc ]. For each 1 ≤ r ≤ sc , define the subset Br so that j ∈ Br if and only if r is the
smallest value such that j is in the same connected component of r after removing from G the nodes in
[sc ]\{r}. Next, for each q ≥ 0, define the subset Brq such that j ∈ Brq if and only if j ∈ Br , and the shortest
distance between j and r in the graph G after removing the nodes in [sc ]\{r} is q. Note that all the Brq are
disjoint and Br = q Brq .
                        S

    Now, if |Br | < Λ for each r, we define the trees F (r) as follows: the node set of F (r) is Br . For each
q and each j ∈ Brq+1 , choose the smallest node j ′ ∈ Brq ∩ N (j) in the ordering of positive integers (where
N (j) is the neighborhood of j, and such node always exists by the definition of Brq+1 ), and consider the edge
{j, j ′ }. Let the edge set of F (r) be formed by all these edges {j, j ′ } with fixed r. Let (F, E) be the union of
all these trees F (r).
    Next, for each (r, r′ ) with r′ < r, if r is connected to some node j ∈ F (r′ ) in G, then consider the smallest
such j and fix the node pair {r, j}. Then, inductively in (r, r′ ), we decide whether or not to include {r, j}
int C as follows. For each (r, r′ ), consider all the above fixed pairs {r1 , j1 } corresponding to some (r1 , r1′ )
satisfying r1 < r or r1 = r and r1′ < r′ , that have been included into C by the induction hypothesis; denote
the set of these pairs by C0 (r, r′ ). Then, we decide to include {r, j} into C, if and only if the graph with
node set F and edge set E ∪ C0 (r, r′ ) ∪ {{r, j}} does not contain a cycle. This way we have decided the
choice of C inductively, and then define F0 = (F, E, R, C).
    Finally, if |Br | ≥ Λ for some r, then find the smallest such r and denote it by r0 . Define the trees F (r)
for r < r0 same as above, and define the trees F (r) = {r} for r > r0 . For r = r0 , we shall order the nodes
in Br0 (inductively) by some ≺, as follows: put nodes in Brq0 with smaller q before those with bigger q. For
j1 , j2 ∈ Brq+1
              0
                  , let j1′ ∈ Brq0 ∩ N (j1 ) and j2′ ∈ Brq0 ∩ N (j2 ) be defined as above, then define j1 ≺ j2 if either
j1 ≺ j2 or j1 = j2′ and j1 < j2 (as positive integers). Then, let the node set of F (r0 ) to be the first Λ nodes
 ′       ′      ′

in Br0 under ≺, and let the edge set of F (r0 ) be defined same as above. Let (F, E) be the union of all these
F (r0 ). Let C be defined same as above but only involving r′ < r ≤ r0 , and define F0 = (F, E, R, C).

Lemma 4.29. For any G ∈ Gk we have πsc (G) ∈ Fsc ,Λ,k ∪ Fserr
                                                           c ,Λ,k
                                                                  .

Proof. First, each Br contains r and becomes a tree with the edge set of F (r) in our construction (because
each node j ∈ Brq+1 has only one edge in F (r) connected to a node in Brq , thus each node in Br has a unique
path in F (r) reaching r). This tree property is preserved even if we select the first Λ nodes in Br0 (because
if j ∈ Brq+1
          0
              is selected then all nodes in Brq0 are also selected). Clearly the sizes of F (r) satisfy the Fsc ,Λ,k
requirement if all |Br | < Λ, and satisfy the Fserrc ,Λ,k
                                                          requirement if some |Br | ≥ Λ. Finally, note that F (r)
is a tree, so the fact that the graph with node set F and edge set E ∪ C does not contain any cycle follows
from the construction of C in Definition 4.28.                                                                    □

Definition 4.30 (Pivotal, forbidden and allowed node pairs). Fix F0 = (F, E, R, C) ∈ Fsc ,Λ,k ∪ Fserr                  c ,Λ,k
                                                                                                                              .
                                                                           q
Let F (r) (1 ≤ r ≤ sc ) be the trees forming F , and let F (r) be the set of nodes j ∈ F (r) such that the
shortest distance between j and r is q. Define the notion of pivotal, forbidden, and allowed node pairs
{j, j ′ }, where j ̸= j ′ and j, j ′ ∈ [k], as follows. First {j, j ′ } is pivotal if and only if {j, j ′ } ∈ E ∪ C. Next we
define {j, j ′ } to be forbidden if and only if one of the following conditions holds:
     (1) When |F (r)| < Λ for all r:
                                           ′
         (a) If j ∈ F (r)q and j ′ ∈ F (r)q with |q − q ′ | ≥ 2; or
         (b) If j ∈ F (r)q and j ′ ∈ F (r)q+1 and j < j ′′ , where j ′′ is the parent node of j ′ ; or
         (c) If j ∈ F (r)\{r} and j ′ ∈ F (r′ ) with r′ < r; or

        (d) If j = r and j ′ ∈ F (r′ ) with r′ < r, and {r, j ′′ } ∈ C with some j ′′ ∈ F (r′ ) and j ′ < j ′′ ; or

        (a) If one of (a)–(e) in case (1) holds for some r ≤ r0 ; or
        (b) If j ̸∈ F and either j ′ ∈ F (r) for some r < r0 , or j ′ ∈ F (r0 )q for some q < q0 − 1, or
            j ′ ∈ F (r0 )q0 −1 and j ′ ≺ j0+ , or j ′ = j0+ and j < j0 as positive integers. Here j0 ∈ F (r0 )q0 is the
            last element of F (r0 ) under ≺ (defined same as above), and j0+ is the parent node of j0 .
Finally, define {j, j ′ } to be allowed if it is not pivotal and not forbidden. Define the sets of pivotal, forbidden
and allowed node pairs to be PV(F0 ), FB(F0 ) and AL(F0 ).

Lemma 4.31. We have πsc (G) = F0 if and only if {j, j ′ } ∈ E(G) for all {j, j ′ } ∈ PV(F0 ) and {j, j ′ } ̸∈ E(G)
for all {j, j ′ } ∈ FB(F0 ).

Proof. Throughout this proof, the items (1a)–(1f) and (2a)–(2b) always refer to those in Definition 4.30.
Since all edges in E ∪ C are edges in G, clearly πsc (G) = F0 implies {j, j ′ } ∈ E(G) for all {j, j ′ } ∈ PV(F0 ).
Note also that when πsc (G) = F0 , we have Brq = F (r)q except when r ≥ r0 and |Br0 | ≥ Λ, in which case
F (r0 ) consists of the first Λ nodes in Br0 under ≺, and F (r) is a single point for r > r0 .
    Next we prove that if πsc (G) = F0 then {j, j ′ } ̸∈ E(G) for all {j, j ′ } ∈ FB(F0 ). Assume {j, j ′ } ∈ E(G)
for contradiction, and consider several cases according to the definition of forbidden pairs:
                                                                  ′
    (1a) In this case we may assume j ∈ Brq and j ′ ∈ Brq with q − q ′ ≥ 2, then by definition the distance
between j ′ and r in G after removing the nodes in [sc ]\{r} is at most q + 1 < q ′ , which is impossible.
    (1b) In this case j ′ is connected to j in G, thus the parent of j ′ will not be the smallest node in Brq ∩ N (j ′ )
in the ordering of positive integers (j ′ > j), which contradicts the definition of Bq+1     r
                                                                                                .
                                   ′                                            ′
    (1c) In this case we have j ∈ Br′ and that j ̸∈ R is connected to j by a bond, thus j ∈ ∪r′′ ≤r′ Br′′ by
definition, which contradicts the fact that j ∈ F (r) = Br .
    (1d) In this case the fixed node pair corresponding to (r, r′ ) in Definition 4.28 cannot be {r, j ′′ } as j ′ < j ′′
and {r, j ′ } is an edge in G, so by the construction in Definition 4.28 we cannot have {r, j ′′ } ∈ C, contradiction.
    (1e) In this case, by the construction in Definition 4.28, once we have included all the node pairs in
Ce0 (r, r′ ) into C (they are the same as C0 (r, r′ ) defined in Definition 4.28), at the step of considering (r, r′ ),
we must include some node pair {r, j1 } into C (where j1 ∈ F (r′ ), but may or may not equal j ′ ), which is
again a contradiction.
    (1f) In this case j ∈ F = r Br and j ′ ̸∈ F , say j ∈ Br , but then this j ′ , which is connected to j by an
                                  S

edge in G, must belong to Br′ for some r′ ≤ r (or j ′ ∈ [sc ]) by definition, which contradicts the assumption
that j ′ ̸∈ F .
    (2a) This case is basically the same as (1a)–(1e) under the extra assumptions r ≤ r0 .
    (2b) Assume j ̸∈ F . If j ′ ∈ F (r) for r < r0 , then j ∈ ∪r<r0 Br ; but since Br = F (r) for r < r0 we get
j ∈ supr<r0 F (r) which is impossible. Now suppose j ′ ∈ F (r0 ), then we have j ∈ Br0 ; moreover the nodes
in Br0 can be ordered by ≺ as defined above, and the assumption implies that j ≺ j0 in this ordering, hence
F (r0 ), which is formed by choosing the first Λ elements in Br0 , will contain j instead of j0 , contradiction.
    Finally we prove that if {j, j ′ } ∈ E(G) for all {j, j ′ } ∈ PV(F0 ) and {j, j ′ } ̸∈ E(G) for all {j, j ′ } ∈ FB(F0 ),
then we must have πsc (G) = F0 . This is basically done by reverting the arguments in (1a)–(1f) and (2a)–(2b)
above. Assume first that |F (r)| < Λ for all r. Then using that each pair {j, j ′ } in case (1c) and (1f) does
not belong to E(G), we can deduce that Br = F (r) for each r. Then using the same assumptions for (1a),
we can inductively show that Brq = F (r)q for each r and each q. Then using the assumptions for (1b), we
see that the choice of edges in the construction of πsc (G) exactly leads to those edges already in F . Finally
the assumptions for (1d)–(1e) imply that the node pairs {r, j} constructed are exactly those already in C.
48                                                YU DENG, ZAHER HANI, AND XIAO MA


   The case when some |F (r0 )| = Λ is similar; we again get F (r) = Br for r < r0 , and that the tree containing
r > r0 in πsc (G) is just {r}. Moreover, the assumptions for (2b) imply that the first Λ0 elements in Br0 are
exactly those already in F (r0 ), and this then yields that πsc (G) = F0 .                                     □

  Now, by applying the mapping πsc defined in Definition 4.28 and using Lemma 4.31, we can complete the
proof of Lemma 4.27.

   To prove (3) we notice that, under the assumption of (3) (which may be formally written as F0 = F10 ∪F20 ),
and after suitable relabeling and renaming, we must have PV(F0 ) = PV(F10 ) ∪ PV(F20 ) and IAL(F0 ) =
IAL(F10 ) ∪ IAL(F20 ). This is because, by Definition 4.30 (1a)–(1f), the sets PV(F0 ) and IAL(F0 ) depend only
on the set of edges in E and node pairs in C, as well as the ordering structure of the nodes in each tree
F (r) of the forest (F, E) (note also that {j, j ′ } ∈ FB(F0 ) for any j ∈ F 1 and j ′ ∈ F 2 , due to Definition 4.30
(1c)–(1e)). The same property also implies (4), because these structures are preserved by the q : F → F ′ in
Definition 4.26, which is order preserving within each subtree F (r).                                              □

Remark 4.32. Note that χF0 in Lemma 4.27 is defined for F0 . In later proof, if F0 = (F, E, R, C) occurs as
part of F = (F0 , (λr ), (mj )), then we may also write χF = χF0 (cf. Definition 4.15). We still always keep in
mind that χF0 depends only on F0 and not on the structure of the other parts of F.

   Step 4. A sub-forest decomposition. It suffices to prove (4.36)–(4.38). By the above upper bound (4.110)
for Err1 , and the norm bounds (4.115) for Err2,j , we only need to analyze the formula for (Main1 ) in (4.113).
   We start by decomposing F ∈ Fℓ,Λ into a disconnected union of subtrees and one sub-forest. Note that
the definition of Fℓ,Λ (Definition 4.16) requires that F = {ℓ} × [|F |]; however, by suitable relabeling, we may
replace this set [|F |] by any fixed set of cardinality |F |. Now define H = a(F) ∪ q(F) ∪ bF (H ′ ) as in Definition
4.13 and denote |Q\H| = k. Define R1 to be the set of all r ∈ R such that λr ⊆ Q\H, and R2 = R\R1 .
Note that by the definition of a(F), q(F), and bF (H ′ ), we have |λr | = 1 for each r ∈ R1 , so |R1 | = |Q\H| = k.
           LONG TIME DERIVATION OF THE BOLTZMANN EQUATION FROM HARD SPHERE DYNAMICS                              57


Moreover, with H and |R| fixed, the number of choices for R1 , as well as the choice of which element of Q\H
belongs to which root cluster in R1 , is equal to
                                                                           |R|!
                                     |R|(|R| − 1) · · · (|R| − k + 1) =          .                         (4.119)
                                                                          |R2 |!
We may now fix such a choice, and thus identify R1 with Q\H, and write R1 = Q\H by abusing notations.
    Assume H and |R| are fixed. For any r ∈ R1 = Q\H, we also have that (i) the tree F (r) is not connected
to any other tree F (r ′ ) by C (cf. Definition 4.13 (4)), and (ii) we have H ′ ∩ [mj ]+ = ∅ for any j ∈ F (r).
For each such r, define Tr to be the cluster tree formed by F (r) and its associated (single-element) set λr
and its associated (mj )j∈F (r) . It can be shown that there is a unique way of relabeling F (r) so that Tr
becomes a new tree Tr ∈ Tℓ,Λ .
    Therefore, for any F with F ∈ Fℓ,Λ and r(F) = Q, we can split the cluster forest F as the disjoint union
of F = F1 ∪ F2 where F1 = r∈R1 Tr . Moreover F2 is the part of F corresponding to the trees F (r) with
                                  S

r ∈ R2 , keeping the same edges in E and node pairs in C from the the trees F (r) (apart from a possible
relabeling), and with their associated sets (λr )r∈R2 and (mj )j∈F2 . With appropriate relabeling, we can turn
Tr into a cluster tree in Tℓ,Λ and turn F2 into a cluster forest in Fℓ,Λ . Note also that after this relabeling,
we have bF (H ′ ) = bF2 (H ′ ) and hence a(F2 ) ∪ q(F2 ) ∪ bF2 (H ′ ) = r(F2 ) = H.
    In conclusion, if H and |R| are fixed as above, once we have fixed a choice of R1 and a bijection between
R and Q\H (and thus identified R1 with Q\H), we then have a bijective correspondence between (F, H ′ )
  1

and ((Tr )r∈Q\H , F2 , H ′ ), i.e.

                               (F, H ′ ) : F ∈ Fℓ,Λ , r(F) = Q, a(F) ∪ q(F) ∪ bF (H ′ ) = H
                             

                                                         ↕
      (Tr )r∈Q\H : Tr ∈ Tℓ,Λ × (F , H ) : F ∈ Fℓ,Λ , H ′ ⊆ p(F2 ), a(F2 ) ∪ q(F2 ) ∪ bF2 (H ′ ) = r(F2 ) = H .
                                   2 ′       2

                                                                                                           (4.120)
   Let us describe an example of this bijection as follows. Assume Q has 5 elements, which we denote by
A, · · · , E (they are actually elements of PL[L]), and fix H = {C, D, E} so Q\H = {A, B}. For simplicity we
also ignore the layer digit ℓ. Assume |R| = 4, and fix the set R1 = {1, 3} and bijection A ↔ 1, B ↔ 3, see
the discussion before (4.119). Suppose the tuples (TA , TB , F2 , H ′ ) are given (with corresponding subscripts
and superscripts such as λA and E 2 etc.) by:
      • TA : we have RA = {1} and TA = [3] with EA = {{1, 2}, {2, 3}}, and (λA )1 = {A} and ((mA )j )1≤j≤3 =
        (a, b, c);
      • TB : we have RB = {1} and TB = [4] with EB = {{1, 2}, {2, 3}, {3, 4}} and (λB )1 = {B} and
        ((mB )j )1≤j≤4 = (d, e, f, g);
      • F2 : we have R2 = {1, 2} and F 2 = [6] with E 2 = {{1, 3}, {3, 4}, {2, 5}, {5, 6}} and C 2 = {{2, 4}},
        and (λ2 )1 = {C, E} and (λ2 )2 = {D}, and ((m2 )j )1≤j≤6 = (r, s, t, u, v, w);
      • H ′ ⊆ p(F2 ) contains the element C, and an element in {3} × [t], and an element in {5} × [v].
Then by appropriate relabeling, we can construct the corresponding (F, H ′ ) as follows: we have R = [4] and
F = [13] with E = {{1, 5}, {5, 6}, {2, 7}, {7, 8}, {3, 9}, {9, 10}, {10, 11}, {4, 12}, {12, 13}} and C = {{4, 8}},
and (λj )1≤j≤4 = ({A}, {C, E}, {B}, {D}) and (mj )1≤j≤13 = (a, r, d, s, b, c, t, u, e, f, g, v, w). Finally H ′ ⊆
p(F) contains the element C, and an element in {7} × [t], and an element in {12} × [v] (strictly speaking this
H ′ is different from the H ′ ⊆ p(F2 ) defined above, but we shall abuse notations and identify these two with
the relabeling (1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6) ↔ (2, 4, 7, 8, 12, 13)). This then leads to the bijective correspondence (4.120).
