   The rest of this paper is devoted to the proofs of Propositions 5.2–5.4. We will first prove Proposition
5.3; this is the center of this paper, and will occupy Sections 6 to 11. Then we will prove Proposition 5.2 in
Section 12, and finally prove Proposition 5.4 in Section 13.

                           6. Interaction diagrams and molecule expansions
   From this section until Section 11, we will focus on the proof of Proposition 5.3, which concerns the
quantities EH (ℓτ, zH ) defined in (4.49), and Err1ℓ (zs ) defined in (4.50).
   In this section, we introduce the notion of layered interaction diagrams D and prove that EH (ℓτ, zH ) has
a decomposition into terms of form IN (F , D, H ′ ) that depend on these diagrams. Such decomposition has
the form
                                             (∗)     ℓ
                                            X      Y       1      1
                              EH (ℓτ ) =                                 IN (F , D, H ′ ),              (6.1)
                                                 ′ ′
                                                        g R ℓ′ ! m Fℓ′ !
                                           (F ,D,H ) ℓ =1
                    Qℓ                                                                      P(∗)
where H ′ is a set, ℓ′ =1 (gRℓ′ !mFℓ′ !)−1 is a combinatorial factor associated with D, and        represents the
summation with certain constraints. For the detailed setup, see (6.16)–(6.15).
                                                                                P∗        Qℓ
   Given a formula of form (6.1), we will prove an upper bound for the sum (F ,D,H ′ ) ℓ′ =1 (gRℓ′ !mFℓ′ !)−1 ,
see Proposition 6.12. With this upper bound, Proposition 5.3 will follow from estimates for individual term
IN (F , D, H ′ ), see Proposition 6.15. The proof of Proposition 6.15 will occupy Sections 7 –11.
   In the proof below, all dynamics only concern particles p ∈ p(F ), and we will abuse notation and use z to
abbreviate zp(F ) (instead of zN ). We also fix ℓ and consider a layered cluster forest F = (Fℓ , · · · , F1 ) ∈ FΛℓ
defined in Definition 4.19. The parameters Aℓ and Λℓ etc. are defined as in Proposition 4.21 and (2.11).

6.1. Layered interaction diagrams. We start with the definition of layered interaction diagrams D.

Definition 6.1 (Layered interaction diagram D). Given a layered cluster forest F ∈ FΛℓ , a layered
interaction diagram D based on F , is defined to be structure D = (F , G, ≺), where G and ≺ satisfying
the following requirements.
     (1) G is an undirected graph with node set p(F ) (which is the union of all [mj ]+ for j ∈ ∪ℓ′ Fℓ′ ), and there
         exist graphs Gj and Oℓ′ such that the union of their edge sets equals the edge set of G. Moreover,
         Gj and Oℓ′ satisfy the requirements (2) and (3) below.
     (2) For each j ∈ Fℓ′ , Gj is an undirected graph with node set [mj ]+ and edge set E(Gj ), which equals
         a tree plus at most Γ edges (allowing multiple edges between two nodes). For c ∈ E(Gj ), we refer to
         them as collisions and define the layer to be ℓ[c] := ℓ′ .
     (3) For each ℓ′ , Oℓ′ is an undirected graph with node set p(Fℓ′ ) = ∪j∈Fℓ′ [mj ]+ . For each {j, j ′ } ∈
         Eℓ′ ∪ Cℓ′ , the edge set E(Oℓ′ ) contains one single edge oj,j ′ between one element p ∈ [mj ]+ and one
         element p′ ∈ [mj ′ ]+ . For oj,j ′ ∈ E(Oℓ′ ), we refer to them as overlaps and define the layer to be
         ℓ[oj,j ′ ] := ℓ′ .
            LONG TIME DERIVATION OF THE BOLTZMANN EQUATION FROM HARD SPHERE DYNAMICS                                   63


     (4) We use the symbol p |= n to denote that p ∈ p(F ) is an endpoint of the edge n ∈ E(G). Then,
         the ≺ is a partial ordering on the edge set E(G) that satisfies the following: if p |= n1 , n2 for some
         p ∈ p(F ) and n1 , n2 ∈ E(G), then n1 and n2 are comparable (n1 = n2 , n1 ≺ n2 or n2 ≺ n1 ), and
         we must have n1 ≺ n2 if ℓ[n1 ] < ℓ[n2 ]. Moreover, ≺ is the smallest partial ordering that contains all
         these relations.
   Note that in (2), for j1 and j2 in different layers, there may exist edges n1 ∈ E(Gj1 ) and n2 ∈ E(Gj2 ) such
that n1 and n2 connect the same pair of nodes. Similarly, in (3), such n1 and n2 may also exist in different
layers E(Oℓ′1 ) and E(Oℓ′2 ). These edges are always treated as different edges, so when taking the union in
(1), they will become multiple edges in G.
   For each ℓ′ , denote Dℓ′ := (Fℓ′ , Gℓ′ , ≺ℓ′ ) to be the layer ℓ′ interaction diagram in D, where Gℓ′ is the layer
ℓ subgraph of G with edge set E(Gℓ′ ) consisting of all edges n ∈ E(G) of layer ℓ′ , and ≺ℓ′ is the restriction
 ′

of ≺ to E(Gℓ′ ). With this we can write D = (Dℓ , · · · , D1 ).

     The proof of Proposition 6.12 relies on the following two combinatorial lemmas.

Lemma 6.13. Given a positive integer n, the number of rooted trees T with node set {ℓ} × {1, · · · , n} is
nn−2 ≤ C n · n!. Moreover we also have
                                        XY  n
                                              dj ! ≤ C n · n!,                                     (6.27)
                                                       T   j=1
where the sum is taken over all rooted trees with node set {ℓ} × {1, · · · , n}, and dj is the degree of node
j in T . (It can be shown that the left hand side of (6.27) actually equals n(3n−3)!
                                                                              (2n−1)! , see [29]; but this is not
needed in our proof.)

Proof. Denote the left hand side of (6.27) by An , then it equals the number of rooted trees of node set
{1, · · · , n}, with all the edges at each single node being linearly ordered. Suppose the root is fixed to be 1,
and such a structure is fixed, we will generate a permutation π of {1, · · · , n} as follows: first define π(1) = 1.
Suppose π(j) has been defined for j < k, then choose π(k) to be an unchosen node that is adjacent to some
π(j) (j < k); if there are more than one, we choose the one with the smallest j and then the smallest edge
under the linear ordering at j.
   Since π has n! choices, it follows that An = n! · Bn , where Bn is the number of structures that lead to the
                                                                                                   P
permutation π(k) = k. Now consider such a structure. Let the degree of node k be dk , then k dk = 2n − 2.
Note that all edges in T are completely determined by the sequence (d1 , · · · , dn ); in fact k and j < k are
adjacent, if and only if (i) the number of nodes in {1, · · · , k − 1}\{j} that are adjacent to j is strictly less
than dj , and (ii) this property is not true if j is replaced by any j ′ < j. Moreover, each node k > 1 has only
one edge connected to nodes j < k, and the linear ordering of all edges between k and ℓ > k must be fixed
by the corresponding ordering of ℓ; this then leaves only dk ways of inserting the edge between k and j < k
into this existing linear ordering. It then follows that
                                                            X          n
                                                                       Y
                                            Bn ≤                            dj ≤ C n ,
                                                     d1 +···+dn =2n−2 j=2

where the last inequality is because the sequence (d1 , · · · , dn ) has only 2n−3
                                                                                    n                            n
                                                                              n−2 ≤ 4 choices, and d2 · · · dn ≤ 2
by AM-GM inequality.                                                                                              □

                               7. Integral expressions and CH molecules
   From now on until Section 11, we will prove Proposition 6.15. In this section, we will introduce the notion
of molecules M and their associated integrals IN (M, H, H ′ ), and prove Proposition 7.8 which provides upper
bounds of IN (F , D, H ′ ) using IN (M, H, H ′ ). Then, assuming certain upper bounds for IN (M, H, H ′ ), see
Proposition 7.11, we can prove Proposition 6.15. The proof of Proposition 7.11 is left to Sections 8–11.
72                                   YU DENG, ZAHER HANI, AND XIAO MA


7.1. Definition of molecules. In this subsection we define the notion of molecules and their associated
integrals. Recall that in the layered interaction diagram D = (F , G, ≺) (Definition 6.1), the collisions and
overlaps n are edges in G. We now introduce a new graph, i.e. a collision history molecule, where the
collisions and overlaps are treated as nodes. For later use, we start by defining the more general notion of
molecules.

Definition 7.1 (Molecules). We define a (layered) molecule to be a structure M = (M, E, P), see Figure
9, which satisfy the following requirements.




         Figure 9. Illustration of the molecule M in Definition 7.1 (empty ends are omitted). Here
         a C-atom (resp. O-atom) is represented by a black diamond shape (resp. a black round
         dot). The arrow indicates the direction of an edge, and we always require that this direction
         goes from top to bottom (cf. Definition 7.2). Serial edges are indicated by the same color,
         and free (resp. fixed) ends are indicated by corresponding labels. Finally, note that the free
         and fixed ends at the O-atom form a simple pair (see Definition 8.6 below).

     (1) Atoms, edges, bonds and ends. Assume M is a set of nodes referred to as atoms, and E is a set of
         edges consisting of bonds and ends to be specified below.
          (a) An atom n ∈ M is marked as either C-atom (collision) or O-atom (overlap), and is assigned
              with a unique layer ℓ[n] which is an integer in [1, L].
          (b) Each edge in E is either a directed bond (n → n′ ), which connects two distinct atoms n, n′ ∈ M,
              or a directed end, which is either connected to one atom n ∈ M or an empty end that does
              not connect to any atom in M. Each non-empty end is either incoming (→ n) or outgoing
              (n →), and is also marked as either free or fixed. We understand that all the empty ends are
              viewed as outgoing and free. We may optionally define an initial layer ℓ1 [e] (resp. a final
              layer ℓ2 [e]) for each outgoing (resp. incoming) end e, which are integers in [1, L].
     (2) Serial edges. The set P specifies the particle line structure of M. More precisely, P is a set of maps
         such that, for each atom n, P contains a bijective map from the set of 2 incoming edges at n to the
         set of 2 outgoing edges at n. Given an incoming edge e+ and its image e− under this map, we say
         e+ and e− are serial or on the same particle line at n (the notion of particle lines will be defined
         in Definition 7.2 (5)).
     (3) (M, E) satisfies the following requirements.
          (a) Each atom has exactly 2 incoming and 2 outgoing edges.
          (b) There does not exist any closed directed path (which starts and ends at the same atom).
          (c) There do not exist two atoms m and m′ connected by two different paths, such that the atoms
              strictly between them in each path are all O-atoms (including when there is no atom between
              them), and the edges at these O-atoms in each path are all serial.
          (d) For any bond n → n′ , we must have ℓ[n] ≥ ℓ[n′ ]. If the initial and final layers are defined as
              in (1b), then for any outgoing end e = (n →) (resp. incoming end e = (→ n)), we must have
              ℓ1 [e] ≤ ℓ[n] (resp. ℓ2 [e] ≥ ℓ[n]).
           LONG TIME DERIVATION OF THE BOLTZMANN EQUATION FROM HARD SPHERE DYNAMICS                                       73


  We introduce some additional notions that are defined for molecules.

Definition 7.2 (More on molecules I). Here are some related concepts defined for molecules.
    (1) Full molecules. If a molecule M has no fixed end, we say M is a full molecule.
    (2) Parents, children and descendants. Let M be a molecule. If two atoms n and n′ are connected by
        a bond n → n′ , then we say n is a parent of n′ , and n′ is a child of n. If n′ is obtained from n by
        iteratively taking parents, then we say n′ is an ancestor of n, and n is a descendant of n′ (this
        includes when n′ = n).
    (3) The partial ordering of nodes. We also define a partial ordering between all atoms, by defining
        n′ ≺ n if and only if n′ ̸= n and is a descendant of n. In particular n′ ≺ n if n′ is a child of n. Using
        this partial ordering, we can define the lowest and highest atoms in a particular set A of atoms,
        to be the minimal and maximal elements of A under the partial ordering (such element need not be
        unique).
    (4) Top and bottom edges. We also refer to the incoming and outgoing edges as top and bottom edges.
    (5) Particle lines. For any M, we define a particle line of M to be a sequence of distinct edges
        (e1 , · · · , eq ) (together with all atoms that are endpoints of these edges), such that e1 is a top (free
        or fixed) end, eq is a bottom (free or fixed) end, and (ej , ej+1 ) are serial (Definition 7.1 (2)) at some
        atom for each 1 ≤ j < q. We also include each empty end as a particle line.
                             −
    (6) Sets E∗ and Eend       . Define E∗ to be the set of all bonds and free ends. Define also the sets Eend (resp.
          −
        Eend ) to be the set of all ends (resp. all bottom ends). Note that these sets include empty ends.
   It is clear, with the definition of particle lines in (5), that each edge belongs to a unique particle line, and
each particle line contains a unique bottom end, so the set of particle lines is in one-to-one correspondence
                −
with the set Eend   of bottom ends (and also with the set of top ends if we exclude the empty ends).

Definition 7.3 (Associated integral of a molecule). Consider the molecule M = (M, E, P), we introduce
the following definitions.
    (1) Associated variables zE and tM . We associate each node n ∈ M with a time variable tn and each
        edge e ∈ E with a position-velocity vector ze = (xe , ve ). We refer to them as associated variables
        and denote the collections of these variables by zE = (ze : e ∈ E) and tM = (tn : n ∈ M).

        and top edges at n respectively, such that e1 and e′1 (resp. e2 and e′2 ) are serial. We define the
        associated distribution ∆n = ∆ = ∆(ze1 , ze2 , ze′1 , ze′2 , tn ) as follows.
         (a) If n is an C-atom, then

Remark 7.4. Note the following difference between C-atoms and O-atoms. In (7.1), the distribution ∆ is
symmetric under permutation (e1 , e2 , e′1 , e′2 ) ↔ (e2 , e1 , e′2 , e′1 ), and also (e1 , e2 , e′1 , e′2 ) ↔ (e′1 , e′2 , e1 , e2 ) except
for the [(ve1 − ve2 ) · ω]− factor. In other words, for any C-atom we must distinguish top and bottom edges,
and also distinguish serial edges, in order for (7.1) to be well-defined; however there is also symmetry between
“top” and “bottom” in the case when the [(ve1 − ve2 ) · ω]− factor does not play a role.
   On the other hand, in (7.2), the distribution ∆ is symmetric under permutation exchanging e1 with e′1 ,
or exchanging e2 with e′2 , or exchanging (e1 , e′1 ) with (e2 , e′2 ). Therefore, for any O-atom we do not need to
distinguish top and bottom edges, as long as the serial edges (or particle lines) are properly distinguished.
Remark 7.5. Note that general products of δ functions, such as δ(x)2 , may not be well-defined; however, any
such product can be written as a single factor of form δ(F (x)), where F : Rm → Rn is a function. This δ
function is then well-defined (as a measure supported on a submanifold of Rm ), as long as F is a submersion.
In the case of (7.4), Rm is the space of all (ze )e∈E and (tn )n∈M , and Rn is the space consisting of all the
vectors and scalars occurring in (7.1)–(7.2). This F is indeed a submersion, because once (ze ) for all bottom
ends e are fixed, then F is a bijective diffeomorphism, from the space consisting of the remaining vector and
time variables (ze ) and (tn ) to its range. This will be clear in the proof of Proposition 7.8 below.
   The above allows us to make sense of the integral (7.4). This integral will be manipulated below (such
as in Propositions 8.10–8.11), and all the subsequent integrals occurring in these manipulations can be
well-defined in the same way, and the corresponding equalities and inequalities in Propositions 8.10–8.11
unambiguously make sense. Finally, in the proof of Proposition 7.11 in Section 9.3, we are evaluating this
integral by integrating in a certain order of the (ze ) and (tn ) variables. As will be clear from the proof, when
integrating in each set of (ze ) and (tn ) variables, the F function corresponding to those δ functions that are
involved in this particular integration is always an explicitly defined mapping that can be explicitly verified
to be a submersion. Such integrals are then well-defined as integrals on submanifolds (or Lebesgue integrals
on lower dimensional Euclidean spaces), and all the estimates unambiguously make sense.
   Alternatively, we can make sense of these δ functions in a more elementary way, by using approximate δ
functions. For example, let δθ (y) = θ−p φ(y/θ) for y ∈ Rp and small θ, and let θ = (θn ) where each θn is
small enough depending on ε, then we may define INθ by replacing all the δ functions in (7.1) and in the
second line of (7.2) by the corresponding δθn . It is then clear that INθ converges to IN in a suitable iterated
limit θn → 0 (in the order of integration in the proof of Proposition 7.8 below), so it suffices to prove that
INθ are uniformly bounded in θ as claimed in the current estimates for IN . Note that with such change
of δ to δθn Propositions 8.10–8.11 become trivially true, so the uniform bounds follow from the same proof
presented here. In particular, each integral estimate in Section 9 (such as in Propositions 9.1–9.2) would
now involve an extra averaging in the extra variables yn taken in the tiny ball |yn | ≤ θn , corresponding to
the δθn functions. Since θn is small enough, these extra averagings would not affect any estimate or any
conclusion, either in Section 9 or in any other part of the proof.
7.2. From layered interaction diagrams to molecules. In this subsection, we shall construct a molecule
M b from any layered interaction diagram D. Then we prove that the integral IN (F , D, H ′ ) associated with
D (Definition 6.6) can be bounded by the integral IN (M, H, H ′ ) associated with M (Definition 7.3 (4)).
           LONG TIME DERIVATION OF THE BOLTZMANN EQUATION FROM HARD SPHERE DYNAMICS                             75




        Figure 10. (Part of) an interaction diagram within one layer, and the corresponding mole-
        cule. In the interaction diagram (left): particles (1 ∼ 5) are represented by boxes, collisions
        by segments (cf. Figure 5) and overlaps by red dashed segments (a ∼ e), with the partial
        ordering indicated below. In the molecule (right): particle lines (1 ∼ 5) are distinguished
        by colors, and collisions and overlaps are represented by atoms (a ∼ e). Note the directions
        of bonds are opposite to the actual particle trajectories by our convention.


7.2.1. From layered interaction diagrams to molecules. As demonstrated by Figure 10, we can construct a
molecule from a layered interaction diagram.

Definition 7.6 (Molecules from layered interaction diagrams). Let D = (F , G, ≺) be a layered interaction
diagram. We define a molecule M = (M, E, P) as follows. This M will be called the collision history
molecule, or CH molecule constructed from D.
    (1) The set of atoms M consists of n that are elements of E(G), which is a C- (or O-) atom if n is a
        collision (or overlap), and define ℓ[n] as in D in Definition 6.1.
    (2) The set of edges E can be constructed as follows. By Definition 6.1 (4), for each p ∈ p(F ), the set
        L(p) := {n ∈ E(G) : p |= n} is linearly ordered by ≺. For any two consecutive elements n ≺ n+ in
        L(p), we draw a bond (n+ → n) from n+ to n; we also draw a top (resp. bottom) free end e at the
        highest (resp. lowest) element in L(p) and define ℓ2 [e] := ℓ2 [p] (resp. ℓ1 [e] := ℓ1 [p]). Finally, for
        each p such that L(p) = ∅, draw an empty end e with ℓ1 [e] = ℓ1 [p].
    (3) The particle line structure P is specified as follows. Two edges (n+ → n) and (n → n− ) at the same
        atom n are made serial (Definition 7.1 (2)), if there exists p such that p |= n+ , n, n− ; in other words,
        the two edges are serial if and only if n+ , n and n− are the collisions of the same particle p with
        other particles.

  We can show that the molecule M constructed above satisfies the following properties.

Proposition 7.7. The molecule M defined in Definition 7.6 is a full molecule. Moreover, the partial ordering
of atoms of M defined in Definition 7.2 coincides with the same partial ordering ≺ in D. The particle lines
of M are in one-to-one correspondence with the particles p ∈ p(F ), such that the set of atoms in this particle
line is precisely the set L(p) = {n ∈ E(G) : p |= n}. Finally, we have |M| = Z and |E| = 2|M| + X + |H|,
and the total number of particle lines equals X + |H| (cf. Definition 6.8).

Proof. First, for any atom n of M, it is a collision or overlap between exactly two particles p and p′ . Each
of the two particles gives rise to a linearly ordered set containing n, as well as one top edge and one bottom
edge at n. Therefore n has exactly 2 top and 2 bottom edges, and no fixed end by definition. Also by
definition, if there is an edge n → n′ , then we must have n′ ≺ n in the partial ordering ≺ in D; in particular
there is no closed directed path, and no bond n → n′ with ℓ[n] < ℓ[n′ ], and the partial ordering for atoms of
M defined in Definition 7.2 is a subset of the partial ordering ≺ in D. These two partial orderings actually
76                                                 YU DENG, ZAHER HANI, AND XIAO MA


coincide, as the ≺ in D is the smallest partial ordering containing all the linear orderings on the subsets
{n ∈ E(G) : p |= n} for different p.
   Next, by the definition of serial in M, it follows that each particle line of M corresponds to a unique
particle p ∈ p(F ), such that this particle line contains exactly all the atoms in L(p) = {n ∈ E(G) : p |= n},
and all edges constructed from the linear ordering of this set (if L(p) = ∅ then p corresponds to an empty
end). Now suppose two atoms m and m′ are connected by two different paths, such that the atoms strictly
between them in each path are all O-atoms (including when there is no atom between them), and the edges
at these O-atoms in each path are all serial. In this case, these two paths must belong to two different particle
lines, which correspond to two different particles p, p′ ∈ p(F ). Then, for some initial configuration z 0 , in the
dynamics for z 0 , these two particles p and p′ will have two collisions or dynamical overlaps m and m′ such
that no collision involving either p or p′ happens between m and m′ . This means that the trajectory of both
p and p′ are linear between m and m′ , and these two linear trajectories will have two instances where the
distance becomes ε with previous distance > ε, which is impossible by elementary geometric considerations.
   Finally, by (6.20) we have |M| = Z, and the total number of particle lines is |F |p = |F |r + |F |p\r , which
equals X + |H| in view of (6.19). By adding up the number of edges at all atoms in M and recalling that
E is the set of all edges (bonds and free ends) of M, we get that 4|M| = 2(|E| − #ee ) − 2(X + |H| − #ee ),
where #ee is the number of empty ends, hence (8.1) is true.                                                      □

    Now, to prove (7.8), we shall consider its extension involving an extra parameter tmin ∈ [0, ℓτ ] (and taking
tmin = 0 recovers (7.8)). This extension is the following: given tmin ∈ [(ℓ′ − 1)τ, ℓ′ τ ], we require that ℓ[n] ≥ ℓ′
for all atoms in n ∈ M; moreover we require tn > tmin for each n in addition to (7.3) in the definition of D
in (7.8), and in the definition of 1Oe on the right hand side of (7.8), we require that all the collisions and
dynamical overlaps in the dynamics H(ℓτe ) defining 1 e happen after time tmin . We will prove that (7.8) still
                                                        O
remains true under this extension.
    With the above extension, we may also further fix an (extra) ordering of the times of all collisions and
dynamical overlaps in the dynamics H(ℓτ  e ); such ordering has to be consistent with the directed graph
structure of M (i.e. if there is an edge n → n′ in M then the collision/dynamical overlap n must happen
before n′ in time), and the time evolution of zp = (xp , vp ) of the particles p depends only on M and does not
depend on this extra ordering. Thus the effect of this extra ordering is only adding certain restrictions of form
tn1 < tn2 to the definition of D in (7.3), as well as introducing new indicator functions to 1Oe corresponding
to the extra time ordering of collisions and dynamical overlaps in e  H(ℓτ ). If we can prove (7.8) for each fixed
extra ordering, then adding all these equalities then yields the desired (7.8).
    Step 4 : With the above reductions, we now proceed by induction on the number of collisions and dynamical
overlaps. If there is no collision or dynamical overlap, then the graph M is empty and both sides of (7.8)
are vacuously 1. Suppose the extension of (7.8) is true for fewer collisions and dynamical overlaps, now we
choose the first (in time) collision or dynamical overlap, denoted by n, which depends on M and also the
extra orderings we have fixed above. We will assume n is a collision happening between two particles p1 and
p2 ; the dynamical overlap case is similar and much easier (where the incoming velocity coincide with the
outgoing ones).
    Consider the C-atom n ∈ M, and the two bottom ends (e1 , e2 ) and two top edges (e′1 , e′2 ) at n, such that
e1 and e′1 are serial and belong to the same particle line for p1 , and e2 and e′2 are serial and belong to the
same particle line for p2 . We will assume e′1 and e′2 are both bonds; the case when one or both of them
are ends only requires trivial modifications. Now, if we remove the atom n and turn (e′1 , e′2 ) to two bottom
ends at the other atoms they connect to, we will reduce M to another CH molecule M′ (with the extra time
ordering inherited from M).

7.5. Diagram Dictionary between interaction diagrams and (CH) molecules. At this point, we
have presented two different diagrammetic expansions related to the cumulants EH (ℓτ, zH ). On the one
hand we have the layered interaction diagram expansion in D = (F , G, ≺), see the IN (F , D, H ′ ) in (6.15)–
(6.16); on the other hand we have the CH molecule expansion in M, see the IN (M, H, H ′ ) in (7.4), which
controls the IN (F , D, H ′ ) as in (7.5).
   These two formalisms are closely related; while in the proof below we will adopt the molecule formulation
which provides more flexibility, it is also important to exploit properties of the interaction diagram D to get
information for the molecule M, especially in Sections 8 and 10. In this subsection we list, in a book-keeping
way, some relations between these two notation systems, which will be useful for the proof below. Some of
these are stated in a less precise way, but they can and will be made rigorous in the revelant proofs below
in Sections 8–11. For an illustration of this see Figure 11.
          LONG TIME DERIVATION OF THE BOLTZMANN EQUATION FROM HARD SPHERE DYNAMICS                               81




        Figure 11. Illustration of a multi-layer molecule. Here green (G), red (R), blue (B),
        brown (BR) and purple (P) represent particles lines in these colors. Here the horizontal
        lines indicate the times separating different layers. Particle lines in r(Fℓ ) = {R, B} (resp.
        in r(Fℓ−1 ) = {G, R, B} or in r(Fℓ−2 ) = {G, R, B, P }) are precisely those crossing the time
        ℓτ (resp. (ℓ − 1)τ or (ℓ − 2)τ ) line. They interact with other particle lines in p(Fℓ ) =
        {G, R, B, BR} (resp. in p(Fℓ−1 ) = {G, R, B, P } or in p(Fℓ−2 ) = {G, R, B, P }) within layer
        ℓ; these particle lines in p(Fℓ ) (resp. p(Fℓ−1 ) or p(Fℓ−2 )) may or may not cross the time ℓτ
        (resp. (ℓ − 1)τ or (ℓ − 2)τ ) line. Some particle lines in p(Fℓ ) (i.e. BR) do not cross the time
        (ℓ − 1)τ line, and others (G, R and B) cross this line and belong to r(Fℓ−1 ). They again
        interact with other particle lines (P ) in p(Fℓ−1 ) within layer ℓ − 1.

   (1) Correspondences:
        (a) Collisions (resp. overlaps) in D are C- (resp. O-) atoms in M, and particles in D are particle
            lines in M;
       (b) Particles p ∈ p(Fℓ′ ) are (essentially) those particle lines that exist in layer ℓ′ ;
        (c) Particles p ∈ r(Fℓ′ ) = p(Fℓ′ ) ∩ p(Fℓ′ +1 ) are (essentially) those particle lines that exist in layer
            ℓ′ and crosses into layer ℓ′ + 1;
       (d) Two particles are connected by collisions or overlaps in layer ℓ′ in D, if and only if their particle
            lines intersect the same connected component of layer ℓ′ of M;
        (e) For each layer ℓ′ , the clusters in this layer can be mapped to sets of C-atoms, which are connected
            by O-atoms (overlaps).
   (2) Parameters:
        (a) The number of particle lines in M equals |F |p = X + |H| (cf. Definition 6.8);
       (b) The number of atoms in M equals |M| = Z, and the number of edges in M satisfies that
            |E| − 2|M| = X + |H|;
        (c) For ρ := |H ′ | + Z − X defined in Lemma 6.11, we have ρ = ρcyc + O(|H| + |H ′ |) where ρcyc is
            the number of idnependent cycles in M.

                                       8. Operations on molecules
8.1. More notions about molecules and CH molecules. From now on until Section 11, we will prove
Proposition 7.11. In this section, we make some preparations by defining the basic cutting operations for
molecules and CH molecules, and proving certain associated properties. These will be used in the algorithm
defined in Sections 10 and 11 below.
Definition 8.1. Let M be a molecule. For any atom n ∈ M, we also write n ∈ M (so we may identify M
with the set of atoms M). Define the degree of n (abbreviated deg) to be the number of edges at n that
are not fixed ends. Note that any atom has deg at most 4, and every atom has deg 4 if and only M is full.
If M is a molecule and M′ is a subset of M, we may regard M′ as a molecule (i.e. a sub-molecule of M), by
82                                     YU DENG, ZAHER HANI, AND XIAO MA


viewing all the bonds between atoms in M′ and M\M′ as free ends at atoms in M′ . For any subset A ⊆ M,
define ρ(A) := |B(A)| − |A| + |F(A)|, where B(A) is the set of bonds between atoms in A, and F(A) is the
set of components of A (where A is viewed as a subgraph of M).
   For any layered molecule M, define Mℓ′ , M>a and M(a:b) , M[a:b] etc. to be the sub-molecule formed by
all atoms n with layer ℓ[n] = ℓ′ , ℓ[n] > a and a < ℓ[n] < b, a ≤ ℓ[n] ≤ b etc, and abbreviate M[a] := M[1:a] .
Recall the notion of generalized links defined in Section 7.4. Let p and p′ be two particle lines of M, and A
be a subset of M, we say
      • p and p′ are connected via A, if p and p′ intersects the same component of A (where the components
        are taken viewing A as a subgraph of M, and viewing the bonds as undirected);
      • p and p′ are linked via A, if a generalized link exists between the bottom ends of two particle lines
        p1 and p′1 , where p1 is either equal to or connected to p via A, and same for p′1 and p′ ;
      • p forms a recollision within A, if p intersects a component of A that contains a cycle within this
        component;
      • p forms a self-link within A, if a generalized link exists between the bottom ends of two particle
        lines p1 and p′1 , and each of them is either equal to or connected to p via A.
   Finally, for any atom n, we define Sn to be the set of all descendants of n. This is a subset of M with
only one highest atom, which is n. In some cases (for example Definition 11.1 (3)), we may also restrict this
Sn to some specific subset of M depending on the context. We define a subset T of M to be an extended
binary tree, if T contains a unique highest atom r, and each atom in T \{r} has a unique parent atom in T .

   In the structure of molecules M, the O-atoms (coming from overlaps) play a special role. On the one hand
they do not correspond to “real” collisions and do not change the particle velocities (so they are somewhat
easier to deal with), on the other hand they are also exceptional cases for some arguments in our algorithm.
For this reason, we need to introduce some additional notations associated with these O-atoms.

Definition 8.2 (Notations associated with O-atoms). Let M be a molecule. We say M is regular, if for
any O-atom n ∈ M, any fixed end at n must be serial with a free end at n. This property will be assumed
for all the molecules studied below, and will be preserved throughout the reduction process. For any regular
molecule M, define an ov-segment σ to be a consecutive part of a particle line, such that each of the two
terminals of σ is either an atom or a (free or fixed) end, and each intermediate atom (i.e. non-terminal atom)
of σ is a O-atom; in particular, each edge (bond or end) is also an ov-segment by this definition. We say an
ov-segment is maximal if it cannot be extended to a longer ov-segment.
   For two atoms n and n′ , if there exists an ov-segment σ with two ends at n and n′ (we shall say σ connects
n and n′ , or it is between n and n′ ), we say they are ov-adjacent; if n is higher than n′ then we say n′ is
an ov-child of n, and n is an ov-parent of n′ . For any disjoint subsets A, B ⊆ M, define the ov-distance
between A and B to be the smallest integer m > 0 such that there exists a path between an atom of A and
atom of B that can be divided into m ov-segments. If A and B are not connected by a path in M, we define
their ov-distance to be ∞; if A ∩ B ̸= ∅, define their ov-distance to be 0. We define a molecule M to be
ov-proper, if it is a forest, all its atoms have deg 3 or 4, and the ov-distance between any two distinct deg
3 atoms is at least 3. Finally, let A ⊆ M be a set, we say A is ov-connected if for any n, n′ ∈ A, there
exists nj ∈ A (1 ≤ j ≤ q) such that (n1 , nq ) = (n, n′ ) and nj and nj+1 are ov-adjacent (each nj ∈ A, but the
the O-atoms on the ov-segment between nj and nj+1 need not be in A). Define the ov-components of A
to be the maximal ov-connected subsets of A.

Remark 8.3. By Definition 7.1 (3c) we know that, in the language of Definition 8.2, that two ov-segments
that belong to two different particle lines cannot intersect at two different atoms.

Definition 8.4. Let M be a molecule and o be an O-atom with 4 edges ei (1 ≤ i ≤ 4), where e1 and e3 are
serial and same for e2 and e4 , and none of them is a fixed end. Let ni be the atom connected to o by ei .
By deleting the O-atom o (see Figure 12), we remove it from M and the 4 edges ej , and add two bonds
e′1 = (n1 , n3 ) and e′2 = (n2 , n4 ) with same directions as (e1 , e3 ) and (e2 , e4 ). Here if some ni is absent, say
           LONG TIME DERIVATION OF THE BOLTZMANN EQUATION FROM HARD SPHERE DYNAMICS                                83


e3 is a free end at o, then the bond (e1 , e3 ) should be replaced by a free end at n1 , and so on. Note that
deleting o only removes this o from the two particle lines it belongs to, and does not affect any other particle
lines. Moreover, if a particle line becomes absent of atoms after deleting o (i.e. when e1 and e3 are both free
ends), then we shall view it as an empty end.




         Figure 12. Deleting an O-atom (Definition 8.4). The relevant notations correspond to
         those in Definition 8.4.

    (1) With the notions in Section 6.3 and Definition 8.1, the total number of particle lines (and the total

    (2) For any p ∈ p(F ), let ℓ1 = ℓ1 [p] and ℓ2 = ℓ2 [p] be as in (4.44). Then for any atom n in the particle

        the particle line of p is an empty end, then p ∈ H ∩ H ′ .
    (3) For 1 ≤ ℓ′ ≤ ℓ, each particle line p ∈ p(Fℓ′ ) must be equal to, or connected to a particle line
        p′ ∈ r(Fℓ′ ) via the layer ℓ′ subset Mℓ′ . If ℓ′ < ℓ, then we also have p(Fℓ′ ) ∩ p(Fℓ′ +1 ) = r(Fℓ′ ).

   (2) First, recall that p ∈ p(Fℓ′ ) if and only if ℓ1 [p] ≤ ℓ′ ≤ ℓ2 [p] (see the proof of Proposition 7.8, Step 2 ).

   (4) Consider any p ∈ r(Fℓ′ ). By (4.41) we have either p ∈ a(Fℓ′ ) or p ∈ q(Fℓ′ ) or p ∈ bFℓ′ (r(Fℓ′ −1 )). In

p1 ∈ [mj ]+ ), then r (such that j ∈ Fℓ′ (r)), then p (such that λr = {p}).
   Now we replace p by p1 and ℓ′ by ℓ′ − 1 and repeat the above argument, to see that either the particle line
p1 is connected to another particle line p′1 via Mℓ′ −1 , or we can construct p2 ∈ r(Fℓ′ −2 ) from p1 as above.
Then we can repeat this process, and there are only two possibilities; in the first possibility, there is some
0 ≤ i ≤ ℓ′ − 1 and k = ℓ′ − i, such that we can construct pi and find p′i ̸= pi , with pi , p′i ∈ r(Fk ) and the
particle lines pi and p′i being connected via Mk . Starting from p′i , we can repeatedly apply (3) to obtain
            LONG TIME DERIVATION OF THE BOLTZMANN EQUATION FROM HARD SPHERE DYNAMICS                                         85


(p′i−1 , · · · , p′1 , p′ ) such that p′ ∈ r(Fℓ′ ), p′1 ∈ r(Fℓ′ −1 ) etc., and the particle lines p′ and p′1 are either equal,
or are connected via Mℓ′ etc. See Figure 14 for an illustration.
    Now, if p ̸= p′ , then the above relations imply that the particle lines p and p′ are connected via M[k:ℓ′ ]
(by going from p to (p1 , · · · , pi ) in this order, then going to (p′i , · · · , p′1 , p′ ) in this order). If p = p′ , choose
the smallest 1 ≤ n ≤ i such that pn ̸= p′n , then pn and p′n are both connected to pn−1 via Mℓ′ −n+1 (so
pn and p′n are also conected via Mℓ′ −n+1 ), and are also connected to each other via M[k:ℓ′ −n] by similar
arguments as above. This implies that the particle line p forms a recollision within M[ℓ′ ] .
    In the second possibility, we can construct pℓ′ from pℓ′ −1 ∈ r(F1 ), and pℓ′ ∈ H ′ . Here, if pℓ′ ∈ H ′ \Z,
since each pi is uniquely constructed from pi+1 , we know that p belongs to a set H                  e ℓ′ which has cardinality
|He ℓ′ | ≤ |H ′ \Z|. If pℓ′ ∈ Z, then the bottom end of pℓ′ is generalized linked to the bottom end of some
p′ℓ′ ̸= pℓ′ . We can then find p′ by backtracking from p′ℓ′ as in the first possibility above; by the same proof,
it follows that p is linked to p′ via M[ℓ′ ] if p′ ̸= p, and p forms a self-link within M[ℓ′ ] if p′ = p.




         Figure 14. Proof of Proposition 8.5 (4). Here each pj is connected to pj+1 , and each p′j
         is connected to p′j+1 , via Mℓ′ −j ; moreover pi is connected to p′i via Mℓ′ −i , which results in
         p and p′ being connected via M[ℓ′ −i:ℓ′ ] .

    (5) By the same arguments as in the proof of (3), we know any connected component in Mℓ′ must intersect
some particle line p with p ∈ r(Fℓ′ ); by applying (3) for each of these p, we can inductively prove that, any
connected component of M[a:b] must intersect some particle line p with p ∈ r(Fb ). This implies that the
number of connected components of M[a:b] is at most |r(Fb )| = sb . The same proof implies that the total
number of components and empty ends of M is at most sℓ = |H|.
    Now consider all p ∈ r(Fℓ ) = H, where either (i) the particle line p is connected to some other particle
line p′ ∈ H via M, or (ii) p is linked to other particle line, or forms a recollision or a self-link within M, or
(iii) neither of the above. Let the number of p in cases (i)–(iii) be (r1 , r2 , r3 ), then r1 +r2 +r3 = |H| and r3 ≤
|H ′ \Z| due to (4), so the number of non-coincidental objects is at most (r1 /2) + r3 ≤ (|H| + |H ′ \Z|)/2. □
8.2. Cutting operations. The building block of our algorithm is an operation for molecules, which we call
cutting, defined below.
Definition 8.6. Let M be a molecule (which we always assume to be regular) and A ⊆ M be a set of atoms.
Define the operation of cutting A as free, as follows. See Figures 15–16 for an illustration.
    (1) In the simple case where M contains no O-atoms, we simply turn each bond between n ∈ A and
        n′ ∈ M\A into a free end at n and a fixed end at n′ . Below we discuss the case of overlaps.
    (2) Consider all the maximal ov-segments σ that contain at least one atom in A. This σ is divided by
        these atoms in A and the terminals (which may be ends, or atoms in or not in A) into sub-ov-segments
        σ ′ . We apply (3)–(5) below for each σ ′ .
86                                    YU DENG, ZAHER HANI, AND XIAO MA


     (3) Suppose σ ′ contains two atoms n, n′ ∈ A, and the O-atoms between them are mi ∈ M\A (1 ≤ i ≤ q).
         Denote (m0 , mq+1 ) = (n, n′ ), and let ei be the bond connecting mi and mi+1 . Then, for each 0 ≤ i ≤ q,
         we break the bond ei into one free end at mi (but do not construct this free end if i = 0) and one
         fixed end at mi+1 (but do not construct this free end if i = q), and make the two ends at each
         mi (1 ≤ i ≤ q) serial. Finally we add one bond between n and n′ (denoted by e+ ) in the same
         particle line and the same direction as the bonds in σ ′ . The other parts of M are unaffected.
     (4) Suppose σ ′ contains one atom n ∈ A and has one terminal atom n′ ∈ M\A (which must be a C-
         atom, otherwise we are in case (5) below), and the O-atoms between them are mi ∈ M\A (1 ≤ i ≤ q).
         Denote (m0 , mq+1 ) = (n, n′ ), and let ei be the bond connecting mi and mi+1 . Then, for each 0 ≤ i ≤ q,
         we break the bond ei into one free end at mi and one fixed end at mi+1 , and make the two ends at
         each mi (1 ≤ i ≤ q) serial. The free end at n and fixed end at n′ (denoted by e+ and e− ) are such
         that, the new particle lines containing them are parts of the particle line in M containing the bonds
         in σ ′ , and they are in the same direction as the bonds in σ ′ . The other parts of M are unaffected.
     (5) Suppose σ ′ contains one atom n ∈ A and has one terminal end, and the O-atoms between them are
         mi ∈ M\A (1 ≤ i ≤ q). Denote m0 = n, and let ei be the bond connecting mi and mi+1 (let eq be the
         end at mq that is a terminal of σ). Then, for each 0 ≤ i ≤ q − 1, we break the bond ei into one free
         end at mi and one fixed end at mi+1 , and make the two ends at each mi (1 ≤ i ≤ q) serial. The free
         end at n (denoted by e+ ) and fixed end at mq are such that, the new particle lines containing them
         are parts of the particle line in M containing the bonds in σ ′ , and they are in the same direction as
         the bonds in σ ′ . The other parts of M (including the end eq ) are unaffected.

We also define cutting A as fixed to be the same as cutting M\A as free. For 1 ≤ i ≤ q, define the newly
formed pair of serial free/fixed ends at mi to be a simple pair; these are characterized by having a free end
serial with a fixed end at an O-atom. There is no need to specify the directions of ends in a simple pair, cf.
Remark 7.4.
   It is clear that after cutting A as free, the molecule M turns into a new molecule with two disconnected
sub-molecules: M1 with atom set A and M2 with atom set M\A (each M1 and M2 may have several connected
components). This new molecule is still regular, and has the same atom set as M (so the atom layers and
C/O atom types are preserved). If no occasion in (3) happens, then M1 exactly coincides with the molecule
formed by viewing A as a sub-molecule of M as in Definition 8.1; note that this condition is guaranteed if
A contains each atom in each ov-segment connecting two atoms in A. If Mj are the components of M, then
cutting A as free (or fixed) is equivalent to cutting each A ∩ Mj as free (or fixed); if A ⊆ Mj then cutting A
as free or fixed from M is equivalent to cutting A as free or fixed from Mj and leaving all components other
than Mj unaffected. Thus, without loss of generality, we may assume below that each individual cutting
operation happens within one component of M.
   Given a molecule M, we define a cutting sequence to be a sequence of cutting operations, each applied
to the result of the previous cutting in the sequence, starting with M. In considering cutting sequences
below, we will adopt the following convention. Note that after cutting any A as free or fixed from M, the
atom set of M remains unchanged, but M is divided into two disjoint molecules, M1 with atom set A and
M2 with atom set M\A. Now depending on the context, we may tag M1 as protected, in which case we
will not touch anything in M1 and will only work in M2 in subsequent cuttings. Throughout this process we
will abuse notations and replace M by M2 .

Proposition 8.7. Suppose M′ is obtained from M by a cutting sequence. Then the followings are true:

     (1) If M is regular, the it remains regular during the cutting sequence.
     (2) If a free or fixed end e exists in M, or is created in some cutting operation, then it will remain
         unaffected for the rest of the cutting sequence.
     (3) If n and n′ are adjacent (or ov-adjacent) at any time in the cutting sequence (including when they
         are adjacent in M′ , then they are also ov-adjacent in M.
           LONG TIME DERIVATION OF THE BOLTZMANN EQUATION FROM HARD SPHERE DYNAMICS                           87




        Figure 15. An illustration of cutting operation in Definition 8.6 (3), when n, n′ ∈ A. The
        relevant notations corresponds to those in Definition 8.6 (3).




        Figure 16. An illustration of cutting operation in Definition 8.6 (4), when n ∈ A and
        n′ ̸∈ A. The relevant notations corresponds to those in Definition 8.6 (4).


    (4) Each free or fixed end e that is created in some cutting operation, must be created by breaking some
        bond ei in Definition 8.6 (3)–(5), where 0 ≤ i ≤ q and i ̸= q in Definition 8.6 (5).
   For any atom n ∈ M and edge e′ at n in M′ , we define a mapping Ba(n, e′ ) = e where e is an edge at n
in M, as follows. If e′ is a bond between n and n′ , then it corresponds to an ov-segment σ between n and n′
in M due to (3), and we define e to be the bond at n that belongs to σ. If e′ is a free or fixed end described
in (4), which is created by breaking some bond ei at n in Definition 8.6 (3)–(5), then we define e = Ba(n, ei )
following the bond case above. If e′ is a free or fixed end that exists in M, then we define e = e′ .
   Then, with the above definition, e′ is top/bottom edge at n in M′ if and only if e = Ba(n, e′ ) is top/bottom
edge at n in M, except when e′ belongs to a simple pair. Moreover, for any e′ , the edge e = Ba(n, e′ ) always
belongs to the particle line that contains n and the particle line of e′ (in fact e′ comes from a part of
the maximal ov-segment in M containing e), so we can identify the particle line of e′ in M′ with that of
e = Ba(n, e′ ) in M.

Proof. First (1)–(4) follow directly from Definition 8.6, by examining all the new bonds and ends formed
in Definition 8.6 (3)–(5). The definition of Ba(n, e) follows from (3) and (4), and its properties also follows
from the properties of ov-segments which are easy to verify.                                                 □

Proposition 8.8. Let M be a full molecule, and M′ be the result of M after any cutting sequence. Then,
with M, M′ and the cutting sequence fixed, we can define an ordering between the components of M′ :
    (1) If the first cutting in the sequence cuts M into M1 with atom set A and M2 with atom set M\A,
        where A is cut as free, then each component of M′ with atom set contained in A shall occur in the
        ordering before each component of M′ with atom set contained in M\A.
88                                      YU DENG, ZAHER HANI, AND XIAO MA


     (2) The ordering between the components with atom set contained in A, and the ordering between
         the components with atom set contained in M\A, are then determined inductively, by the cutting
         sequence after the first cutting.

Denote the above ordering by ≺cut , where X ≺cut Y means X occurs before Y in this ordering.
   Next, we define a mapping Sh(e) = Sh(M′ ; e), from the set of bonds and free ends of M and fixed ends of
M , to the set of bonds and free ends in M′ , as follows:
 ′


     (3) For any free end or bond e in M, define e0 = e. If e plays the role of eq in Definition 8.6 (5), or plays
         the role of any ei in Definition 8.6 (3)–(5), in some cutting operation, then define e1 = e+ , where e+
         is the new bond added between n and n′ in Definition 8.6 (3), and the unique newly formed free end
         at n in Definition 8.6 (4)–(5).
     (4) Then, if e1 plays the role of eq in Definition 8.6 (5), or occurs as any ei in Definition 8.6 (3)–(5),
         in some cutting operation in the cutting sequence after the one in (3), then define e2 = e+ in the
         same way as (3), but for the current cutting operation. Repeat until we get the last term er . Define
         Sh(e) = er .
     (5) For any fixed end e in M′ , it must be formed in some cutting operation in Definition 8.6 (3)–(5).
         Then define e1 := e+ in the same way as (3), and define (e2 , · · · , er ) in the same way as in (4).
         Define Sh(e) = er .

   With the above definitions, the following statements are true. First, if e is a fixed end in M′ that belongs
to a component Y of M′ , and Sh(e) belongs to a component X of M′ , then necessarily X ≺cut Y. Second,
any free end e of M′ belongs to the range of Sh, if and only if e does not belong to a simple pair. In this case
there is a unique e∗ which is either a free end of M or a fixed end at a C-atom of M′ , such that Sh(e∗ ) = e.
We define e∗ = Sh∗ (e) in this case.
   Finally, recall the mapping Ba(n, e) defined in Proposition 8.7. We then have Sh(Ba(n, e)) = Sh(e) or
Sh(Ba(n, e)) = e depending on whether or not e is a fixed end, unless e belongs to a simple pair; in the latter
case we have Sh(Ba(n, e)) = Sh(f ), where f is the fixed end in the simple pair.

Proof. Clearly the ordering between components is well-defined by (1)–(2) and the mapping Sh is well-defined
by (3)–(5). If e is a fixed end that is formed in some cutting (say cutting A as free as in Definition 8.6), then
e is at an atom in M\A, while e1 = e+ in (5), and subsequently each of (e2 , · · · , er ), is either a free end at
an atom in A or a bond connecting two atoms in A, so we must have X ≺cut Y.
    Next, suppose Sh(e) = e′ is a free end of M′ , then we have e′ = er , where (e1 , · · · , er ) is defined in (3)–(5).
If e′ belongs to a simple pair, then this fixed end must be formed in a cutting; moreover, this cutting must
be after the cutting that involves er−1 and er = e′ , and e′ must play the role of eq in this cutting. Therefore,
the sequence (e1 , · · · , er ) can be extended to get some er+1 , which means Sh(e) ̸= er = e′ , contradiction.
    Now assume e′ is a free end of M′ that does not belong to a simple pair, then in particular e′ does not
play the role of eq in any cutting. If e′ exists in the original molecule M, then we have Sh(e′ ) = e′ ; otherwise
e′ is formed in some cutting, which must be in some case (4)–(5) of Definition 8.6. In case (4) we get a fixed
end e∗ at the C-atom n′ in M′ (which plays the role of e− in this cutting), and Sh(e∗ ) = e′ . In case (5),
e′ must play the role of e+ , so we get a free end er−1 which is the term preceding e′ = er in the sequence
(e1 , · · · , er ); this er−1 plays the role of eq in this cutting, and is also not serial with a fixed end at an O-atom
before this cutting. By repeating this discussion at er−1 and iterating, eventually we get e∗ which is either a
free end of M or a fixed end at a C-atom of M′ , such that Sh(e∗ ) = e′ .
    The uniqueness of e∗ above also follows from the same backtracking process above. Note that in the
cutting that involves er−1 and er = e′ , say e′ = er plays the role of e+ and er−1 plays the role of eq , we
also have the fixed ends at mj (1 ≤ j ≤ q) that are formed in this cutting, and they are all pre-images of
e′ under Sh, however each of them is a fixed end at an O-atom, which does not meet the requirement. The
same holds for any previous cuttings involving ej and ej+1 for each j ≤ r − 1, which then implies that the
only pre-image satisfying the requirement is the e∗ constructed above.
            LONG TIME DERIVATION OF THE BOLTZMANN EQUATION FROM HARD SPHERE DYNAMICS                                 89


   Finally, the equalities involving Ba(n, e) follows from the definition of Ba(n, e) and similar discussions as
above. For example, if e is a free or fixed end in M′ that is formed by breaking some bond ei in Definition
8.6 (3)–(5), then e either plays the role of e+ in Definition 8.6 (4)–(5), or belongs to a simple pair in M′ ; in
both cases the desired equality can be verified from definition of Sh.                                         □
Definition 8.9. We define a molecule M to be elementary, if it is regular, and satisfies one of the followings:
    (1) This M contains only one atom that has deg 2, 3 or 4; we further require that the two fixed ends are
        not serial, and are either both top or both bottom for C-atom.
    (2) This M contains only two atoms connected by a bond, and their degs are either (3, 3) or (4, 4).
Depending on the type of M, we shall denote it by {2}-, {3}-, {4}-, {33}- and {44}-molecules in the obvious
manner. Moreover, for a {33}-molecule M, we denote it by {33A} if we can cut one atom as free, such that
the other atom becomes a deg 2 atom that satisfies the requirement in (1). Otherwise denote it by {33B}.
Note that a regular {33} molecule is {33B} if and only if both atoms are C-atoms and there is one top fixed
end at the higher atom and one bottom fixed end at the lower atom. For an illustration see Figures 17–18.
   Suppose applying a cutting sequence to M generates a number of elementary components, plus the rest
of the molecule M whose atoms are not cut by this cutting sequence, then we define #{2} and #{33} etc. to
be the number of {2}- and {33}- etc. components generated in this cutting sequence. We also understand
that, when any elementary component is generated in any cutting sequence, this component is automatically
tagged as protected.




         Figure 17. An illustration of {2}-, {3}-, {4}-, {33A}- and {44}-molecules (Definition 8.9).
         Here each end is either free or fixed, obeying also the restrictions in Definition 8.9. For
         two-atom molecule, we always assume n1 is parent of n2 , and (e1 , e7 ) are the two specified
         ends at (n1 , n2 ) respectively. If both are fixed ends we get a {33A}-molecule (note that, in
         the picture, we have two top free ends at n1 ); if both are free ends we get a {44}-molecule.

Proposition 8.11. Let M be a full molecule, and M′ be the result of M after deleting any set of O-atoms
as in Definition 8.4. Then the value of |E| − 2|M| is unchanged. for the I(M) defined in (8.2), we have

ze′1 and ze′2 after the deletion, and then get rid of the integrals in (ze3 , ze4 ) in I(M) by a simpler version of

the arguments in the proof of Proposition 8.10. This then essentially reduces I(M) to I(M′ ); the inequality

(cf. the indicator function 1col (ze1 , ze2 ) in (9.3) below, which equals to the evaluation of the time integral in
to where o is the deleted overlap atom), which is present in I(M) but absent in I(M′ ).                           □



                                             9. Treating the integral
9.1. Integrals for elementary molecules. In this section we make some preparations for the proof of
Proposition 7.11 concerning the integral IN (M, H, H ′ ) as in (7.4) and modified as in (7.14). The goal is to
reduce it to Proposition 9.7, a pure combinatorial statement, which will be proved in Sections 10 and 11.
   Suppose M′ is formed from M by cutting operations, and M′ contains only elementary components and
empty ends. Then, Proposition 8.10 allows us to reduce (7.4) to an integral of form (8.3), which can then be
viewed as an iterated integral where each individual integration is performed on an elementary component
(see Section 9.3 below). In this subsection we study in detail these “local” integrals.
   Let M be an elementary molecule as in Definition 8.9, and consider the integral
                                       ˆ               Y
       J (M) := ε−(d−1)(|E∗ |−2|M|)                          ∆(ze1 , ze2 , ze′1 , ze′2 , tn ) · Q(zE , tM ) dzE∗ dtM .   (9.1)
                                        R2d|E∗ | ×R|M| n∈M


Here in (9.1), the relevant notions are as in (8.3) (except we write ze instead of zee ), where we only integrate
in the variables zE ∗ where E∗ is the set of all bonds and free ends. For fixed ends e of M, the variable ze is
not integrated in (9.1) and acts as a parameter in this integral. The time variables in (9.1) will be denoted
by t1 and t2 . When M contains only one atom, we will also denote (e′1 , e′2 ) by (e3 , e4 ); when M contains two
atoms, we may also denote the edges by ej (1 ≤ j ≤ 7) depending on the context. The variable zej is then
abbreviated as zj = (xj , vj ).

   We always assume that Q is supported in |ve | ≤ | log ε|C for each e, and |xe − x0e | ≤ | log ε|C for some
fixed x0e ∈ Rd , and tn ∈ [(ℓ[n] − 1)τ, ℓ[n]τ ] for each n ∈ M, where ℓ[n] is a fixed integer for each n. We may
make other restrictions on the support of Q, which will be discussed below depending on different scenarios;
such support may also depend on some other external parameters such as (x∗ , t∗ ), which will be clearly
indicated when they occur below.

Proposition 9.1. Let J (M) be defined as in (9.1), where M contains only one atom.
    (1) If M is a {2}-molecule as in Definition 8.9, i.e. the two fixed ends are either both bottom or both

Proof. We start with (9.2) and consider the C-atom case (the O-atom case is similar and much easier). Note
that here we are fixing (x1 , v1 , x2 , v2 ) and integrating in (x3 , v3 , x4 , v4 ) and t1 , which is the same as in (7.10)
in the proof of Proposition 7.8. The equality (9.2) then follows from the same proof, where we first integrate
in (x3 , x4 ), then in (v3 , v4 ), and then in t1 . Here note that the assumption that no two collisions happen at
the same time and no collision happens at ℓτ , see Remark 7.10, allows us to integrate the δ function in t1
unambiguously, same as in the proof of Proposition 7.8.

the volume of support of t1 . Next assume |vi − vj | ≤ ευ in (9.7) and M is a C-atom (the O-atom case is the
same as long as {i, j} ̸= {1, 3}, {2, 4}); let v1 − v2 := u and u − (u · ω)ω := u⊥ , then we have

Note that |u − 2(u · ω)ω| = |u|, and |u|2 = |u · ω|2 + |u⊥ |2 , we may reduce to one of the cases |u · ω| ≤ ευ ,
or |u⊥ | ≤ ευ . With fixed ω (say ω = (1, 0, · · · , 0) by symmetry), in either case, at least one coordinate of
u is bounded by ευ , and all the other coordinates (as well as the weight ((v1 − v2 ) · ω)− ) are bounded by
         ∗
| log ε|C , which implies the volume bound.
    Next, assume |xj − x∗ | ≤ ε1−υ (or similarly |xi − xj | ≤ ε1−υ ) in (9.7), then since the distance between any
two xj + t1 vj is at most ε, we must have |(x1 − x∗ ) + t1 (v1 − vj )| ≤ 2ε1−υ ; by dyadically decomposing |t1 |
and gaining from both the support of t1 and the restriction on v1 − vj as above, the desired volume bound
then follows. The cases of (9.9) involving xi − xj and vi − vj follow similarly, and the case involving xi − x∗
is trivial as we gain from integrating in x1 .                                                                   □

Proposition 9.2. Consider the same setting as in Proposition 9.1, but now M is an elementary molecule
with two atoms (n1 , n2 ), where n1 is a parent of n2 , which satisfies one of the following assumptions:
    (1) Suppose M is a {33A}-molecule with atoms (n1 , n2 ). Let e be the bond connecting them and (e1 , e7 )
        be the two fixed ends at n1 and n2 respectively. Assume that either (i) {n2 } becomes a {2}-component
        after cutting n1 as free, and e1 is not serial with e if n1 is an O-atom, or (ii) {n1 } becomes a {2}-
        component after cutting n2 as free, and e7 is not serial with e if n2 is an O-atom. Assume moreover
        that either (iii) ℓ[n1 ] ̸= ℓ[n2 ] for the two atoms n1 and n2 , or (iv) Q is supported in the set
                                     max(|x1 − x7 |, |v1 − v7 |) ≥ ε1−υ    or |t1 − t2 | ≥ ευ                              (9.15)
94                                        YU DENG, ZAHER HANI, AND XIAO MA


         for some 0 < υ < 1/3, where (xj , vj ) = zej as above.
     (2) Suppose M is a {33B}-molecule which contains two C-atoms. Assume that Q is supported in the set
                   |t1 − t2 | ≥ ευ ,    |vi − vj | ≥ ευ   for any edge (ei , ej ) at the same atom,          (9.16)
         for some 0 < υ < 1/(6d), where again (xj , vj ) = zej .
     (3) Suppose M is a {44}-molecule with atoms (n1 , n2 ) and (e1 , e7 ) being two free ends at n1 and n2
         respectively, such that M becomes a {33A}-molecule after turning these two free ends into fixed
         ends. Moreover assume that Q is supported in the set (where 0 < υ < 1/(4d))
                                            max(|x1 − x7 |, |v1 − v7 |) ≤ ε1−υ .                             (9.17)
Then, in any of the above cases, we have (possibly up to a finite constant coefficient combination)
                                               ˆ              ˆ
                                   J (M) ≤ κ ·    Q dw ≤ κ ·       Q dw.                                     (9.18)
                                                          Ω               Ω′

Here w ∈ Ω and Ω ⊆ Ω′ (in fact Ω = Ω′ in cases (2) and (3)) are opens set in some Rp , and Ω depends
on (xe , ve ) for fixed ends e while Ω′ does not, and the input variables of Q are explicit functions of w and
                                                               ∗                            ∗
(xe , ve ) for fixed ends e. Moreover we have |Ω′ | ≤ | log ε|C and |κ| · |Ω| ≤ ευ | log ε|C .

Proof. (1) By symmetry we may assume (i) is true. First we assume that also (iv) is true. To calculate
J (M), we first fix the values of t1 and all zf for edges f at n1 , and integrate in t2 and all zf for all the free
ends f at n2 . By assumption, we know that n2 becomes a deg 2 atom with two top fixed ends (or a deg 2
O-atom with two non-serial fixed ends) after cutting n1 as free. Let the inner integral be J ({n2 }), then we
can apply (9.2) in Proposition 9.1 to get an explicit expression of J ({n2 }).
   Let the bond between n1 and n2 be e, denote the edges at n1 by (e1 . · · · , e4 ) as in Proposition 9.1, then
e = ej for some j ∈ {2, 3, 4} and j ̸= 3 if n1 is an O-atom. After plugging in the above formula for the inner
integral J ({n2 }), we can reduce J (M) by an integral of form J ({n1 }) as described in (2) of Proposition 9.1,
namely                           ˆ

where the input variables of Q are explicit functions of (t1 , ω, v2 ) and (z1 , z7 ) using Proposition 9.1 and
the fact that zj satisfies (9.5) or (9.6). Now (9.19) already has the form (9.18) (upon dividing Sd−1 into
                                        ∗
coordinate patches) with |κ| ≤ | log ε|C and the bound for |Ω′ | is easy, so we only need to control the volume
|Ω|, where Ω is restricted to the support of 1col (which is a set depending on z1 and z7 ).
   By definition (9.3) of 1col , we know this restriction implies that
                                                                                   ∗
                                          |(xj − x7 ) ∧ (vj − v7 )| ≤ ε| log ε|C                             (9.20)
for some 2 ≤ j ≤ 4, where j ̸= 3 if n is O-atom. Let v1 −v2 = u and u−(u·ω)ω = u⊥ . Plugging in the formula
(9.5) or (9.6), and noting that |xj − (x1 + t1 uj )| ≤ ε where uj = v1 − vj and (u2 , u3 , u4 ) = (u, (u · ω)ω, u⊥ )

                                                                      ∗                              ∗
belong to a one-dimensional tube in Rd which has length ≤ | log ε|C and width ε1−β | log ε|C . Note that

(u, ω) must belong to a subset of Rd × Sd−1 with volume ≤ ε1−β | log ε|C . This already implies the desired

we shall exploit the t1 integral in (9.19). If |v1 − v7 | ≳ ε1−υ , then t1 belongs to an interval of length

as in the proof of Proposition 9.1 (2).
    Finally, assume (iii) is true but (iv) is not, by the above proof, we may assume that Q is supported in the
set where |t1 − t2 | ≤ ευ . But since t1 and t2 must belong to disjoint time intervals in view of ℓ[n1 ] ̸= ℓ[n2 ],
we conclude that t1 itself must belong to a fixed interval of length at most ευ . The desired bound for |Ω|
then follows from exploiting the volume of support of t1 . This completes the proof of (1).

9.2. Absorbing the weights. In the integral in (9.4), note that v belongs to a non-compact domain, and
the weight [(v1 − v2 ) · ω]− is also a priori unbounded. To avoid potential divergences, we will need to absorb
them using decay properties of the integrand, which is done in this subsection.

   Step 3. Now consider a molecule M that is a forest, again in a single layer ℓ′ . By restricting to components,
we may assume M is a tree (which need not be an extended binary tree). We shall divide the atom set of
M into finitely many extended binary trees and upside down extended binary trees (where the notions of
parent and child etc. are the opposite from extended binary trees), as follows. For an illustration see Figure
19.
   First choose any atom n ∈ M, and let Tn be the set of descendants of n, which is an extended binary
tree. Moreover, all bottom ends of Tn are also bottom ends of M. Consider all the atoms nj ̸∈ Tn that are
connected to Tn by a bond. Since M is a tree, if we remove Tn from M (and turn any bond between nj and
Tn to a free end at nj ), then M becomes the disjoint union of trees Mj , where nj ∈ Mj for each j. Note also
that each Mj has only one bond connected to Tn , which is turned to a bottom free end at nj ∈ Mj .
   Now for each j, let Tn∗j be the set of ancestors of nj in Mj , which is now an upside down extended binary
tree. Clearly all top ends of Tn∗j are also top ends of M, and all the discussions in Steps 1–2 above also work
for upside down extended binary trees due to time reversal symmetry. Consider all the atoms njk ∈ Mj \Tn∗j
that are connected to Tn∗j by a bond, then after removing Tn∗j from Mj , the Mj will become the disjoint
union of trees Mjk where njk ∈ Mjk for each k. Also each Mjk has only one bond connected to Tn∗j , which is
turned to a top free end at njk ∈ Mjk . Thus, we can choose Tnjk to be the set of descendants of njk in Mjk ,
which is an extended binary tree, so that all bottom ends of Tnjk are also bottom ends of M, and so on.
   By repeating the above, we have divided M into the disjoint union of finitely many T (j) , where each T (j)
is either an extended binary tree or an upside down extended binary tree. If we apply (9.46) and (9.47) in

Remark 9.6. In the whole cutting process, the molecule M will always stay regular (Proposition 8.7). This
implies that at any time, there is no O-atom with one bond serial with one fixed end. As such, it is easy
to verify that (i) any {33}-component in M′ that contains at least one O-atom must be {33A}-; (ii) any
{33A}-component in M′ must satisfy one of assumptions (i) or (ii) of Proposition 9.2 (1).

Proposition 9.7 below.
