                          10. Cutting algorithm I: reducing to UD molecules
   From this section until Section 11, we prove Proposition 9.7. This is done by constructing a cutting
sequence (and possibly a set of O-atoms to delete) for M. This sequence depends on different cases for M,
and is based on a number of cutting algorithms. In each subsection below, we will first define the relevant
cutting algorithms, and then apply them to different cases of Proposition 9.7. Also, in the proof below we
                                                                               err
will only focus on the case F ∈ FΛℓ . This is because, in the case of F ∈ FΛ     ℓ
                                                                                   , the only difference (and loss)
is caused by the s root particle lines in layer ℓ that may disobey (4.41), see e.g. (6.29) and (6.30) in Section
                                                                                                                 ∗
6.3.1, or Proposition 8.5. It is easy to see by checking the proof below that all such loss is at most ε−C s
which is covered by the term −C ∗ s · 1F ∈FΛerr in (9.76), and the rest of the proof is the same.
                                              ℓ
   Recall the convention of protected components in Definition 8.6. In the arguments below, if a subset
M1 of a molecule M has already been cut (as free or fixed) from M, and subsequently cut into elementary
components, then we will tag it as protected (cf. also Definition 8.9); for the rest of the algorithm we will
not touch M1 , and will replace M by M2 := M\M1 throughout.

10.1. Outline of this section. In the current section, the goal is to reduce the general molecules M in
Proposition 9.7 to a specific type of molecules, called Up-Down, or UD molecules. Such special molecules are
then treated in Section 11. These UD molecules are formed of two layers (see Proposition 10.15 (3)(i)–(iii)),
an Up layer MU and a Down layer MD ; they are both forests (i.e. no collisions within each layer) and are
connected via “UD connections” with MU being “above” MD . The number of these UD connections will be
comparable to ρ, and we also have good control on the number and properties of the connected components
of MU and MD , see Proposition 10.17.
   The reduction process begins by covering some easy or degenerate cases. This includes the case when
|H| + |H ′ \ Z| ≳ ρ (Proposition 10.3), the case when there are many “strongly degenerate pairs” of atoms
(Proposition 10.5, where we gain from good {3} or {44} components instead of {33A} components), and the
case when there are many recollisional clusters in the interaction diagram (Proposition 10.10).
   After excluding these simple cases, we then perform a process of layer refining and layer selection. In layer
refining, we subdivide each layer into O(1) “thin layers” such that each thin layer is a forest, and recollisions
happen exclusively between different thin layers. Then, in layer selection, we select two particular thin layers
such that the number of recollisions caused by these two thin layers alone is already ≳ ρ. This then defines
MU and MD and reduces to the UD molecule case, see Proposition 10.17.
   In this section we will introduce a basic cutting algorithm, called UP (and its inverse DOWN). This
applies to any regular molecule with no top fixed end at any C-atom (these are abbreviated as C-top fixed
ends, same for C-bottom fixed ends) and cuts it into elementary components. We will routinely invoke
          LONG TIME DERIVATION OF THE BOLTZMANN EQUATION FROM HARD SPHERE DYNAMICS                        109


it, along with its inverse DOWN and modifications, in various parts of the algorithm. The key property
of this UP algorithm is that it minimizes the number of “lossy” {4} components, and allows to extract at
least one {33A} molecule from each connected component that contains no deg 2 atoms, and either two deg
3 atoms or a cycle.
    The main drawback of UP is that the gain we can guarantee from it is one gain per connected component
in the worst case scenario. However, it is still very useful when dealing with cases where one can isolate
many components with cycles or two deg 3 atoms, which is the situation for most of the cases considered
in this section. On the other hand, it cannot efficiently handle cases where we need to extract many gains
from a single connected component, like the situation for the UD molecule studied in Section 11.
    We conclude this outline subsection with a diagram that presents the logical flow of this section and the
proof of Proposition 9.7, modulo Proposition 10.17 which is proved in Section 11.




        Figure 20. Outline of this section, which reduces the proof of Proposition 9.7 to that of
        Proposition 10.17, which will be proved in the following Section 11.


10.2. The UP algorithm and some easy cases. Our starting point is a most intuitive cutting algorithm,
which we call UP. The proof of Proposition 9.7 in many cases only requires this simple algorithm.

Definition 10.1 (The algorithm UP). Define the following cutting algorithm UP. It takes as input any
molecule M that is regular (we always assume so; this will not be repeated below), and has no top fixed end
at any C-atom (we henceforth abbreviate it as C-top fixed ends; same for C-bottom fixed ends). For
any such M, we define the cutting sequence as follows:
   (1) If M contains any deg 2 atom n, then cut it as free, and repeat until there is no deg 2 atom left.
   (2) Choose a lowest atom n in the set of all deg 3 atoms in M (or a lowest atom in M, if M only contains
       deg 4 atoms). Let Sn be the set of descendants of n (Definition 8.1).
   (3) Starting from n, choose a highest atom m in Sn that has not been cut. If m has deg 3, and either an
       ov-parent m+ or an ov-child m− of m (Definition 8.2) also has deg 3, then cut {m, m+ } or {m, m− }
       as free; otherwise cut m as free. Repeat until all atoms in Sn have been cut. Then go to (1).
110                                   YU DENG, ZAHER HANI, AND XIAO MA


   We also define the dual algorithm DOWN, by reversing the notions of parent/child, lowest/highest,
top/bottom etc. It applies to any molecule that has no C-bottom fixed end.
   In the following proposition we list the basic properties of the algorithm UP: it minimizes the lossy
{4}-components, and grants at least one {33A}-component whenever there is a recollision. Some further
properties of it (and its variants) will be explored later.
Proposition 10.2. Let M be any molecule as in Definition 10.1. We may assume M is connected (otherwise
consider each component of M). Then after applying algorithm UP to M (and same for DOWN), it becomes
M′ which contains only elementary components. Moreover, among these elementary components:
      (1) We have #{33B} = #{44} = 0 and #{4} ≤ 1, and #{4} = 1 if and only if M is full. If M has no deg
          2 atom, and either contains a cycle or contains at least two deg 3 atoms, then we have #{33A} ≥ 1.
          If M has no deg 2 atom and at most one deg 3 atom, and is also a tree, then M′ contains (at most
          one) {4}-component and others are all {3}-components.
      (2) If M has no deg 2 atom and at most one deg 3 atom, and contains a cycle, then we can decompose
          1 into ≤ C indicator functions 1(∗) as in (9.69), such that for each indicator function, M′ contains
          at least one good component.
Proof. Note that by regularity, any fixed end at each O-atom must be serial with a free end. We first
prove that, throughout the process of the UP algorithm, Sn \{n} will not have any C-bottom fixed end, and
(M\Sn ) ∪ {n} will not have any C-top fixed end. In fact, if n has deg 2 as in Definition 10.1 (1), and is either
O-atom or has no top fix end, then the absence of C-top fixed end in the whole molecule is preserved after
cutting n as free, as this operation does not create any C-top fixed end.
   If n is as in Definition 10.1 (2), then all atoms in Sn \{n} have deg 4 by definition, and hence have no
bottom fixed end. Now consider an operation of cutting m, or {m, m+ }, or {m, m− }, as in Definition 10.1 (3).
Then cutting m does not create any C-bottom fixed end in Sn \{n} (because m is highest, so any ov-parent
of m in Sn \{n} has already been cut), and does not create any C-top fixed end in (M\Sn ) ∪ {n} (because
any ov-child atom of m must be in Sn \{n}). The same can be proved for the operation of cutting {m, m+ }
(or {m, m− }), because m+ (or m− ) has deg 3, hence any ov-child of m+ (or any ov-parent of m− ) that is
C-atom must also be an ov-child (or ov-parent) of m. This proves the above claim by induction.
   Consequently, all the single-atom components in M′ must have deg at least 2, and those of deg 2 must
have two fixed ends either both being top or both being bottom (or non-serial for O-atoms). Similarly, all
the {33}-components {m, m+ } and {m, m− } must be {33A}, because if we cut m as free from it, then m± will
become a deg 2 atom that satisfies the requirements for deg 2 atoms above. This means that all components
of M′ are elementary, and #{33B} = #{44} = 0 by our construction.
   If M has only deg 4 atoms, then the first cutting in M must create a {4}-component; at any time thereafter,
M will contain at least one non-deg-4 atom, and by Definition 10.1, any subsequent cutting will not create
any {4}-component (note that any m ∈ Sn \{n} will have deg at most 3 when we cut it in Definition 10.1
(3), because m has an ov-parent atom in Sn that has already been cut before). The same holds true for any
M that contains non-deg-4 atoms initially. This proves the statement about #{4} in (1).
   Next, assume M does not have deg 2 atoms. We claim that if #{33A} = 0 in M′ , then necessarily also
#{2} = 0 in M′ . In fact, if not, consider the first atom m that has deg 2 when we cut it, and consider the
cutting operation that turns m into deg 2, which must be cutting some deg 3 atom p as free (this p cannot
have deg 4, as cutting one deg 4 atom cannot turn a deg 4 atom into deg 2; cf. Remark 8.3. This cutting
creates a fixed end at m, so by Definition 8.6, this p must be an ov-parent or ov-child of m; moreover this m
and this p must both be deg 3 at the time of cutting p, but this violates Definition 10.1 (3).
   Now consider the quantity σ := |E∗ | − 3|M|, where E∗ is the set of bonds and free ends (not counting
fixed ends), for both M and M′ . This quantity is invariant under cutting, and equals −1 (or 0 or 1 resp.) for
{2}- and {33}- (or {3}- or {4}- resp.) components, so #{2} + #{33A} = ν − σ in M′ , where ν = 1 or ν = 0
depending on whether or not M contains only deg 4 atoms. Note that |E∗ | is the total degree of all atoms
in M minus the number of bonds in M, and the number of bonds is at least |M| − 1 (since M is connected)
           LONG TIME DERIVATION OF THE BOLTZMANN EQUATION FROM HARD SPHERE DYNAMICS                                  111


and at least |M| if M contains a cycle, we conclude that ν − σ = 0 if and only if M is a tree with at most
one deg 3 atom. This proves the last two statements in (1).
    Next we prove (2). Following UP, we first cut n ∈ M as free, which is either the only deg 3 atom, or
some deg 4 atom in M. This does not create any deg 2 atom (cf. Remark 8.3). In the subsequent cuttings,
consider the first {33A}-component (which exists due to (1)) and the two fixed ends (e1 , e2 ) at it, which
are created when cutting previous atoms p1 and p2 as free, and neither p1 nor p2 has deg 2 when it is cut,
by the same proof as above. If p1 ̸= p2 , say p1 is cut before p2 , then there exist two free ends e′1 and p1
and e′2 at p2 in M′ , such that zee′1 = zee1 and zee′2 = zee2 . Now, if |e         ee′2 | ≤ ε1−1/(8d) , we may insert the
                                                                            xe′1 − x
corresponding indicator function, so that {p2 } is cut as a good {3}-component due to Definition 9.5 (5) (note
that {p1 } ≺cut {p2 }); if |e xe′1 − xee′2 | = |e      ee2 | ≥ ε1−1/(8d) , we may insert the corresponding indicator
                                                x e1 − x

linked to some A contains a good component, and each pair of linked (B, B ′ ) provides a good component in
the one of the two that is cut after the other. Since each B is linked to at least one and at most two other
components, we are able to obtain at least (w − 2 · #ee )/2 good components, as desired.

   Next we prove a result concerning {33A}-components obtained in applying algorithm UP. We show they
are guaranteed to be good (or become good after further cutting), provided they involve non-primitive pairs
in the molecule M. This will be used in the proof of Proposition 10.10.

Proposition 10.4. Define an atom pair {p, p′ } in M to be primitive if p is ov-parent of p′ , and either
(i) there is no ov-parent p1 ̸= p of p′ such that p1 is C-atom and a descendant of p, or (ii) there is no
ov-child p′1 ̸= p′ of p such that p′1 is C-atom and an ancestor of p′ . Then after applying UP (or DOWN),
for each {33A}-component {p, p′ }, either it is good or can be cut into a good and a normal component (by
decomposing 1 into at most C indicator functions), or the atom pair {p, p′ } is primitive in M. The same is
true for any {33A}-component {p, p′ } (which need not come from UP or DOWN), if M has only C-atoms.




           Figure 21. A depiction of the primitive pair {p, p′ }, where p and p1 are ov-parents of p′
           such that p1 is a C-atom that is a descendent of p. The solid segments represent ov-segments
           whereas the dotted ones can contain both O and C atoms. The other case of a primitive
           pair is obtained by switching the role of the two segments at p1 .


Proof. Note that any {33A}-component {p, p′ } is formed in Definition 10.1 (3) with either (p, p′ ) = (m+ , m)
or (p, p′ ) = (m, m− ) and m ∈ Sn ; we will also assume it is not primitive.
    (1) First assume (p, p′ ) = (m, m− ). Then m− has a fixed end when it is cut, which comes from cutting
some atom q ∈ Sn that is ov-parent of m− (this q cannot be ov-child of m− , otherwise it would not be
highest when it is cut). By the non-primitive assumption, this q must be a descendant of m, which means
that we have the time inequality tm ≥ tq ≥ tm− (cf. (3)). If we restrict to |tm − tm− | ≥ ε1/(8d) , then
{m, m− } is already good by Definition 9.5 (6); otherwise we restrict to |tm − tm− | ≤ ε1/(8d) , which implies
|tm − tq | ≤ ε1/(8d) . We then cut m as free from {m, m− }, which produces a good {3}-component {m} and a
normal {2} component {m− }, due to Definition 9.5 (5) (as q belongs to a component that is ≺cut {m}).
    (2) Next assume (p, p′ ) = (m+ , m) with m ̸= n. Similar to (1), we know m has a fixed end when it is cut,
which comes from cutting some atom q ∈ Sn that is ov-parent of m. By the non-primitive assumption, this
q must be a descendant of m+ , which means that we have the time inequality tm ≤ tq ≤ tm+ , which implies
that we have a good component in the same way as in (1).
    (3) Next assume (p, p′ ) = (m+ , m) with m = n. By non-primitive assumption, there exists an ov-child
q ̸= n of n+ which is C-atom and ancestor of n. If q or any O-atom between q and n+ has been cut at the
time when n+ is cut, then the same argument in (1)–(2) implies that we get a good component. If not, since
           LONG TIME DERIVATION OF THE BOLTZMANN EQUATION FROM HARD SPHERE DYNAMICS                              113


n+ has deg 3 when it is cut, we then consider the cutting that turns n+ into deg 3. This cutting must create
either a top fixed end at q, or a top fix end at n+ in the case n+ is C-atom. This contradicts the fact that
M has no C-top fixed end at the time when n is chosen in Definition 10.1 (2).
   (4) Finally assume M has only C-atoms (where the {33A} component need not come from UP or DOWN).
If {p, p′ } is not primitive in M, say p is parent of p′ , then the other child q of p must be ancestor of p′ , and
the other parent q′ of p′ must be descendant of p (it is possible that q = q′ ). If q or q′ belongs to a component
that is ≺cut {p, p′ }, then since tq , tq′ ∈ (tp′ , tp ), we get a good component in the same way as (1)–(3) above;
if not, then p must have a top fixed end and p′ must have a bottom fixed end in {p, p′ }, so it cannot be a
{33A}-component. This completes the proof (the case of DOWN follows by symmetry).                                 □
   By Proposition 10.4, we know that the only way for a {33A} component obtained in applying UP (or
DOWN) to be not good, is that it involves a primitive pair in M. This pair must also violate the conditions
of Definition 9.5 (6), which leads to the notion of strongly degenerate and primitive strongly degenerate pairs
of atoms in Proposition 10.5 below. This proposition handles the case when there are many (i.e. ≳ ρ)
primitive strongly degenerate pairs of atoms, which is treated by a variant of the algorithm UP. More
precisely, depending on the specific scenario, we either obtain ≳ ρ many good {44}-components (Choice 1 in
the proof below), or obtain ≳ ρ many good components exploiting the recollisional clusters using Proposition
10.2 (2) (Choice 2 ), or obtain ≳ ρ many good {3}-components due to the strongly degenerate assumption
(Choice 3 ).

Proposition 10.5. Define a pair of atoms {n, n′ } is strongly degenerate if
     (i) They are ov-adjacent and in the same layer (ℓ[n] = ℓ[n′ ]).
    (ii) There exist edges (e, e′ ) at n and n′ respectively, such that cutting {n, n′ } as free in M and turning
         (e, e′ ) into fixed ends results in a {33A}-component, as in Definition 9.5 (8).
   (iii) We have the restriction (by indicator functions) that |xe − xe′ | ≤ ε1−1/(8d) and |ve − ve′ | ≤ ε1−1/(8d) .
   We also define a pair {n, n′ } to be primitive, as in Proposition 10.4. Then, if we insert the indicator function
1str.deg.pr whose support satisfies that there exist at least A := (C12   ∗ −1
                                                                            ) ρ disjoint strongly degenerate and
                                                                                            ∗ −1
primitive pairs, then Proposition 9.7 is true. Moreover, if there exist at least D := (C10    ) ρ disjoint strongly
degenerate pairs (which need not be primitive), and the total number of recollisional clusters (cf. Section
                         ∗ −1
6.3) is at most G := (C11  ) ρ, then Proposition 9.7 is true is also true.

Proof. By Proposition 10.3, we may assume |H| + |H ′ \Z| ≤ (C12     ∗ −1
                                                                      ) ρ. Note also that

as #ee ≤ |H|; in this proof we will also have #del = 0. If there exist at least A := (C12    ) ρ disjoint strongly

    (ii) then cut M<ℓ′ ∪ (Mℓ′ \H) into elementary components using DOWN.
It is easy to see that this can always be done, and the contribution of these steps to #{4} is at most 2|H| by
Proposition 8.5 (5) and Proposition 10.2 (1). Now we only need to cut H into elementary components; note
that each component of H is formed by a collection of sets Hj (j ∈ Fℓ′ ) of C-atoms (corresponding to collisions
in each E(Gj )) that are connected by O-atoms (corresponding to overlaps oj,j ′ for {j, j ′ } ∈ Eℓ′ ∪Cℓ′ ). Denote
by X the number of components of H, and by Y the total number of Hj such that j ∈ Fℓ′ is a recollisional
cluster (cf. Definition 6.1). We may then choose to perform one of the following three cutting sequences.
114                                    YU DENG, ZAHER HANI, AND XIAO MA


    Choice 1 : for each component H′ of H, choose one pair {nj , n′j } in it, then cut {nj , n′j } as free, which
is a good {44}-component due to the restrictions |xej − xe′j | ≤ ε1−1/(8d) and |vej − ve′j | ≤ ε1−1/(8d) . Since
{nj , n′j } is primitive, by symmetry we may assume nj is ov-parent of n′j , and no other ov-parent of n′j is a
C-atom in Snj (the set of descendants of nj ). Then after cutting {nj , n′j } as free, there is no C-top fixed
end in H′ \Snj , and no C-bottom fixed end in Snj . Then we can repeat the construction in Definition 10.1
(3) and subsequently apply UP to the rest of H′ , to cut it into elementary components. In total, for the
contribution of the cuttings involving H, we have #good ≥ X and #{4} = 0.
    Choice 2 : for each component of H, let Hj be defined as above, we may order these sets (unrelated to the
ordering of j in CL(ℓ)) such that each Hj has exactly one O-atom (corresponding to overlap) connecting it to
some Hj ′ (j ′ < j); this is because the edges {j, j ′ } ∈ Eℓ′ ∪ Cℓ′ form a forest between all the clusters j ∈ Fℓ′ .
Now we forget the layer digit and view j as an integer. Then we first cut H1 as free (and subsequently cut
it into elementary component using UP or DOWN; same below), which creates a unique deg 3 atom on
an ov-segment within H2 ; then we cut H2 and this atom as free, which creates a unique deg 3 atom on an
ov-segment within H3 , and so on. Note that when each Hj is cut, it always contains at most one deg 3 atom,
so by Proposition 10.2, we get a good component at each Hj that is a recollisional cluster. In this case, for
the contribution of the cuttings involving H, we have #good ≥ Y and #{4} ≤ X.
    Choice 3 : We cut each component of H using UP. In this case, note that for the total number of edges
|E| and total number of atoms |H| of H, we have |E| − 3|H| ≥ X − Γ · Y . In fact, this equals the difference
between the number of atoms and number of bonds of H, which equals
                                                 X

by performing a simple calculation following the same proof of (8.1) in Proposition 8.5, where the sum is

the proof of Proposition 10.2, using the invariance of the quantity |E| − 3|P|, we see that for the contribution
of the cuttings involving H, we have #{2} + #{33} + #{4} ≤ X + Γ · Y (as #{4} ≤ X). Now, by considering
each pair {nj , n′j } and the one of them (say n′j ) that is cut after the other, and excluding those n′j that do
not belong to a {3}-component, we get that #good ≥ L−1 · A − X − ΓY .

which proves (9.76).
   Finally, assume there exist ≤ G recollisional clusters, and ≥ D disjoint strongly degenerate pairs; we list
them as {nj , n′j } and define the relevant notions (X, Y ) etc. as above. Then we have Y ≤ G, and moreover
the number of components of H that contain a recollisional cluster is at most Y , and each component of H
that does not contain a recollisional cluster must be a tree, and hence cannot contain any non-primitive pair
{nj , n′j }. We may assume that the number of primitive pairs is at most A, and therefore X − Y ≤ A. Using
also Y ≤ G and the calculations in Choice 3 above, which does not rely on the primitive property of the
pairs {nj , n′j }, we still get (10.6) with the right hand side replaced by (C2∗ )−1 D, which still proves (9.76). □
10.3. Layer refinement and the large R case. Apart from the simple cases proved in Propositions 10.3
and 10.5, the proof of Proposition 9.7 in the main case requires to locate the major contribution of the
recollisions (i.e. cycles in M). As explained in Section 2.3.2, these recollisions come from two sources: the
recollisions that happen across different layers, and those that happen within a single layer. This is easily
                                                                  Pℓ
seen from Lemma 6.11 which implies that ρ ∼∗ |H ′ | + U + R = ℓ′ =0 sℓ′ + R (where s0 = |H ′ |).
   In this subsection, we will deal with the case when R ≳ ρ, which implies the existence of a single layer with
≳ ρ many recollisional clusters, see Proposition 10.10. In this case, we coule apply a variant of algorithm
UP as in Choice 2 in the proof of Proposition 10.5, but this would encounter a difficulty if these recollisional
clusters belong to too many different connected components in this layer. If this happens, we will need to
start moving upwards until we find the layer where these recollisional clusters are connected, see the proof of
           LONG TIME DERIVATION OF THE BOLTZMANN EQUATION FROM HARD SPHERE DYNAMICS                             115


Proposition 10.10; in this process, we will need the procedure of layer refinement which, roughly speaking,
allows us to split the molecule into thin layers such that each of those layers is a topological forest.
   We remark that the layer refinement process is also useful for the rest of the proof, where the number of
recollisional clusters R ≪ ρ. This case is mainly dealt with in Section 11 after we reduce to the two-layer
case in Section 10.4 below. This reduction is also based on the layer refinement arguments we develop here.
   We start with a simple proposition that explains how one can split a molecule with cycles into an ordered
disjoint union of subsets that are trees or forests (i.e. no cycle within each subset).

Proposition 10.6. Let M be a connected molecule with ρ(M) ≤ Γ (where ρ(M) is defined in Definition
8.1). Then we can divide M into at most Γ + 1 disjoint subsets M(k) for 1 ≤ k ≤ γ ≤ Γ + 1, such that (i)
each M(k) is a tree, and (ii) if k < k ′ then no atom in M(k) can be parent of any atom in M(k′ ) .

Proof. We induct on ρ(M). If ρ(M) = 0 then M is a tree and there is nothing to be done. If ρ(M) > 0, then
M must contain a cycle of atoms. Let a lowest atom in this cycle be n, which is connected to two atoms n1
and n2 in this cycle, then both nj must be parents of n. Let Sn be the set of descendants of n (including n
itself), we then define
                        
               Mhigh := m ∈ M : m is connected to n1 by a path not containing atoms in Sn ,          (10.7)

and Mlow := M\Mhigh . Note in particular Sn ⊆ Mlow and n1 , n2 ∈ Mhigh (n2 ∈ Mhigh because any atom in
the cycle other than n cannot belong to Sn ). Clearly Mhigh is connected, because for any m ∈ Mhigh and any
m′ on the path connecting m to n1 defined by (10.7), we must also have m′ ∈ n1 by definition, so each atom
in Mhigh is connected to n1 ∈ Mhigh by a path completely within Mhigh .
   To prove Mlow is connected, choose any atom m ∈ Mlow \Sn . Since M is connected, there is a path in M
connecting m to n. Let the first atom in this path (starting from m) that belongs to Sn be p, and consider
any atom q ̸= p between m and p on this path, then m is connected to q by a path not containing atoms
in Sn , which means q ∈ Mlow (as q ∈ Mhigh would imply m ∈ Mhigh ). Thus we know each m ∈ Mlow \Sn is
connected to an atom p ∈ Sn by a path completely within Mlow , but Sn is connected (every atom in Sn is
connected to n by a path completely within Sn ), so Mlow is also connected.
   Moreover, if an atom m1 ∈ Mhigh is connected to an atom m2 ∈ Mlow by a bond, then we must have
m2 ∈ Sn (otherwise m1 ∈ Mhigh would imply m2 ∈ Mhigh ). This means m1 must be a parent of m2 , since
otherwise m1 is a child of m2 , so m2 ∈ Sn implies m1 ∈ Sn ⊆ Mlow , which is impossible. Finally, since there
are at least two bonds between Mhigh and Mlow (those connecting n to n1 and n2 ), we see that

     |M| = |Mhigh | + |Mlow |, |B| ≥ |Bhigh | + |Blow | + 2 ⇒ ρ(Mhigh ) + ρ(Mlow ) ≤ ρ(M) − 1 ≤ Γ − 1.

We now apply induction hypothesis to Mhigh and Mlow , and note that no atom in Mlow is parent of an atom
in Mhigh , to divide M into at most (ρ(Mhigh ) + 1) + (ρ(Mlow ) + 1) ≤ Γ + 1 subsets that satisfy requirements
(i) and (ii), so the proof is complete.                                                                     □

   The following definition explains how we perform layer refinement for a general CH molecule M. This
involves applying the decomposition defined in Proposition 10.6 to each recollisional cluster, along with a
fixed fashion of choosing the thin layers for the rest of the molecule.

Definition 10.7 (Layer refining). Let (F , D, H ′ ) be fixed, and let M be the CH molecule defined from D as
in Proposition 7.7, which we know satisfies the properties in Proposition 8.5, and let Mℓ′ be the layer ℓ′ subset
of M. We define the following operation of layer refining to turn Mℓ′ into a new layered molecule M          e ℓ′ .
Here the atoms and edges of Mℓ′ are the same as Mℓ′ except some O-atoms being deleted as in Definition
                               e
8.4, but the layering of the atoms are different: the atoms in e  Mℓ′ are divided into at most Γ + 2 disjoint
subsets, each of them indicated by a refined layer number. We will call these thin layers.
   More precisely, consider all clusters j ∈ Fℓ′ , and associated sets of collisions E(Gj ) in D, which can be
viewed as subsets of C-atoms in Mℓ′ . For each {j, j ′ } ∈ Eℓ′ ∪ Cℓ′ , there is also an O-atom oj,j ′ indicating
an overlap between [mj ]+ and [mj ′ ]+ . Recall that a cluster j is recollisional if ∆j = |E(Gj )| − m
                                                                                                     cj + 1 > 0
116                                     YU DENG, ZAHER HANI, AND XIAO MA




          Figure 22. A depiction of the layer refinement process in Proposition 10.6. The dotted
          lines represent the new borders between the refined layers.

                                                                                                      P
(Definition 6.1), let the set of such j ∈ Fℓ′ be K and the number of such j be Rℓ′ (so R =                ℓ′ Rℓ ), and
                                                                                                              ′

note that ∆j ≤ Γ. We define the layer refining process by the following steps.
      (1) Consider the forest F (0) whose node set is Fℓ′ and edge set is Eℓ′ ∪ Cℓ′ , and the subset F (1) of nodes
          that are connected to some j ∈ K by a path in F (0) . Remove from F (1) all j ∈ K and all associated
          edges, and consider the connected components of the remaining forest.
      (2) For each of the above connected components C, collect all its edges, plus any edge in F (1) that
          connects this component to some j ∈ K (i.e. one for each component). Let the collection of all such
          edges be GC . Then in Mℓ′ , delete the O-atoms oj,j ′ as in Definition 8.4, for all j, j ′ ∈ F (1) with
          {j, j ′ } ̸∈ ∪C GC .
      (3) For each j ∈ K, consider the molecule Hj formed by all collisions c ∈ E(Gj ), with edges defined
          in the same way as in Proposition 7.7 from the partial ordering ≺j (in fact this is what one would
          get if one were to delete the overlaps oj,j ′ for all j ′ or cut the set Hj as free, which we will not be
          doing). Then we have ρ(Hj ) = ∆j following the same proof of (8.1) in Proposition 8.5, so we can
          apply Proposition 10.8 to divide its atoms into γ ≤ Γ + 1 layers (Hj )(k) for 1 ≤ k ≤ γ.
      (4) Now, for each 1 ≤ k ≤ γ, we collect all the C-atoms in (Hj )(k) (for all different j) into a set, which
          we call a thin layer, labeled by (ℓ′ , k). Then, for each component C of F (1) as defined in (2), there
          is a unique overlap oj,j ′ with {j, j ′ } ∈ GC and j ∈ K, which involves a unique particle p ∈ [mj ]+ .
          In the ordering ≺, this oj,j ′ is inserted between two consecutive collisions c ≺ c+ in E(Gj ) with
          p |= c, c+ (here c or c+ maybe absent, but not both, since j is recollisional); now we include all the
          C-atoms in E(Gj ) for j ∈ C, and all O-atoms oj,j ′ for {j, j ′ } ∈ GC , into the same thin layer of c is c
          exists, and into the same thin layer of c+ if c is absent.
      (5) Finally, we collect all C-atoms in E(Gj ) for j ∈ F (0) \F (1) , and all O-atoms indicating overlaps
          between them, and put them in a new thin layer (ℓ′ , 0). Note that this layer could be empty.
The thin layers defined above are indicated by ζ = (ℓ′ , k) for 0 ≤ k ≤ Γ + 1, which are linearly ordered
by lexicographic ordering (unrefined, original layers ℓ′ are identified with (ℓ′ , 0)), and the original layer ℓ′
precisely contains all the thin layers (ℓ′ , k). For any ζ and ξ, we define MTζ and MT(ζ:ξ) , MT[ζ] etc. in the same
way as in Definition 8.1, but for thin layers.
Proposition 10.8. If we perform the layer refining for ℓ′ as in Definition 10.7, then
      (1) The number of O-atoms deleted in the process is at most |K| = Rℓ′ .
      (2) Each MT(ℓ′ ,k) (where 1 ≤ k ≤ Γ + 1) is a forest, and has most Rℓ′ connected components.
      (3) The number of components of MT(ℓ′ ,0) is not more than the number of components of Mℓ′ before
          refining.
           LONG TIME DERIVATION OF THE BOLTZMANN EQUATION FROM HARD SPHERE DYNAMICS                               117


  Now consider the result of refining any set of layers in M, which we still denote by M for convenience.
Then we have the followings
    (4) For ζ < ξ, no atom in MTζ can be parent of any atom in MTξ .
    (5) Each connected component of MT(ℓ′ ,0) (where ℓ′ ≤ ℓ) must intersect some particle line p ∈ r(Fℓ′ ).
    (6) With at most 2Rℓ′ +1 exceptions, for each particle line p ∈ r(Fℓ′ ), either p intersects a component
        of MT(ℓ′ +1,0) , or it intersects a component of MT(ℓ′ +1,k) for some 1 ≤ k ≤ Γ + 1, or p ∈ r(Fℓ′ +1 ).
    (7) For each ℓ′ < ℓ, with at most |H ′ \Z| + 2R exceptions, each particle line p ∈ r(Fℓ′ ) either forms
        a recollision or a self-link within MT<(ℓ′ +1,0) , or is connected to or linked to another particle line
        p′ ∈ r(Fℓ′ ) via the subset MT<(ℓ′ +1,0) .

Proof. First consider a single layer refining operation in Definition 10.7. Since F (0) is a forest, we have
                                                                            X
                             (j, j ′ ) ∈ Eℓ′ ∪ Cℓ′ : j, j ′ ∈ F (1) ≤ |K| +
                            
                                                                              |C| − 1,
                                                                              C

where the summation is taken over all components C in Definition 10.7 (2). Also our construction implies
that |GC | = |C| for each C, and these sets are disjoint, thus it follows that the number of O-atoms deleted
(i.e. corresponding to those {j, j ′ } ̸∈ ∪C GC where also j, j ′ ∈ F (1) ) is at most |K| − 1, which proves (1).
Moreover, there is no overlap between any j ∈ F (0) and any j ′ ∈ F (1) , thus there is no bond between MT(ℓ′ ,0)
and any MT(ℓ′ ,k) for k > 0, so any component of MT(ℓ′ ,0) is also a component of Mℓ′ before the refining, which
proves (3).
    Next we prove (2). Imagine if we delete all O-atoms oj,j ′ in layer ℓ′ , then Mℓ′ will become a disjoint
union of Hj (j ∈ Fℓ′ ) without any bond between them. By Proposition 10.6, we know that Hj is a tree if
j ̸∈ K, and (Hj )k is a tree for j ∈ K and 1 ≤ k ≤ Γ + 1. Now we may add back all the O-atoms oj,j ′ for
{j, j ′ } ∈ ∪C GC and for j, j ′ ∈ F (0) \F (1) , by reverting the process of Definition 8.4. Each time we add back
oj,j ′ , we precisely connect Hj and Hj ′ together by this O-atom; If j, j ′ ̸∈ K, the result of this is still a tree.
If j ∈ K, then by Definition 10.7 (4), we know that this Hj ′ (as well as all Hj ′′ with {j ′ , j ′′ } ∈ C for some
C) is connected to (Hj )(k) and it is easy to verify that the result is also a tree. By repeating this, we obtain
that for each k, the thin layer MT(ℓ′ ,k) is a forest formed by at most Rℓ′ trees indexed by j ∈ K.
    Now suppose we perform layer refining to any set of layers ℓ′ , and denote the result still by M. Note
that the particle lines can be defined in the same way as before the refinement, since deleting an O-atom
corresponds to removing one atom from a particle line p and merging two consecutive edges. To prove (4)
we only need to prove it for a single layer ℓ′ . For fixed ℓ′ this conclusion follows from (i) Proposition 10.6,
and (ii) the choice of the thin layer k made in Definition 10.7 (4) concerning each component C, and (iii)
the fact that we have deleted all O-atoms oj,j ′ for {j, j ′ } ̸∈ ∪C GC where also j, j ′ ∈ F (1) , and that all the
atoms in Hj ′ and Hj ′′ (where j ′ , j ′′ ̸∈ K) are in the same thin layer whenever {j ′ , j ′′ } ∈ ∪C GC .
    Next, we prove (5)–(6). From the proof of Proposition 8.5 (5) we already know that before layer refining,
any connected component in Mℓ′ must intersect some particle line p with p ∈ r(Fℓ′ ); this then proves (5)
because MT(ℓ′ ,0) is not affected by layer refining: there is is no bond connecting it to any MT(ℓ′ ,k) for k > 0,
and it also does not contain any of the O-atoms deleted in the refinement process. Similarly (6) follows from
Proposition 8.5 (3): any particle line p ∈ r(Fℓ′ ) must either belong to r(Fℓ′ +1 ), or intersects Mℓ′ +1 (which is
then divided into MT(ℓ′ +1,k) for 0 ≤ k ≤ Γ + 1). The only exceptions are those containing one of the deleted
O-atoms, but the number of these particle lines is at most 2Rℓ′ +1 as each O-atom belongs to at most two
particle lines.
    Finally we prove (7). This follows from Proposition 8.5 (4) which is stated for M before layer refinement.
However, after the layer refinement, we have deleted at most R many O-atoms in M[ℓ′ ] (note that by our
conventions, M[ℓ′ ] is just MT<(ℓ′ +1,0) after the refinement). If we add back one O-atom, it at most creates one
cycle in one component, or merges two components of M[ℓ′ ] , and does not affect any link. Define a particle
line p to be single if it does not satisfy the requirement of (7) (or equivalently Proposition 8.5 (4)), then
adding back one O-atom at most reduces the number of single particle lines by 2. By Proposition 8.5 (4)),
it then follows that the number of single particle lines is at most |H ′ \Z| + 2R, as desired.                      □
118                                    YU DENG, ZAHER HANI, AND XIAO MA


  Before stating and proving the result in the case R ≳ ρ, we need one more auxiliary result concerning
application of UP to molecules with certain special structure.

Proposition 10.9. Suppose M is a full molecule divided into two subsets MU and MD , such that (i) MU
is a forest, and (ii) no atom in MD can be parent of any atom in MU . Define #comp(M) to be the number
of components of M, and define #rec.comp(MD ) to be the number of components of MD that contains a cycle
within this component. Then, after applying the algorithm UP, we have that #{33B} = #{44} = 0, and
                             #{4} ≤ #comp(M) ,     and       #{33A} ≥ #rec.comp(MD ) /6.                   (10.8)

Proof. The statements about #{33B} , #{44} and #{4} already follow from Proposition 10.2. Now we prove
the statement about #{33A} . First note that, if A is a component of MD that contains a cycle, and no atom
in A belongs to a {33A}-component in M′ , then at least one atom in A must be deg 2 when it is cut. In fact,
choose a cycle θ within A and the atom n ∈ θ that is cut after all the other atoms in θ. Then n is connected
to two atoms in θ which are cut before n, and after these two cutting operations there will be two fixed ends
at n, so n must be deg 2 when it is cut.
   Now, if A is as above, and suppose n ∈ A is the first atom in A that has deg 2 when we cut it, and
consider the cutting operation that turns n into deg 2. Then this cutting operation must involve either an
ov-child or an ov-parent p of n. In the former case, since p must also belong to A, by assumption we know
that p must be deg 3 when we cut it, and n also has deg 3 when p is cut, which violates Definition 10.1 (3).
   In the latter case, p is an ov-parent of n, so it either belongs to A or belongs to MU . If p ∈ A then
again it must be deg 3 when we cut it, and the same contradiction with Definition 10.1 (3) occurs as above.
Therefore we must have p ∈ MU . Since p is cut before n, it cannot be cut in Definition 10.1 (1) (as cutting
any deg 2 C-atom in Definition 10.1 (1) requires that this atom has two bottom fixed ends), nor can it have
deg 4 when it is cut (as cutting any deg 4 atom in Definition 10.1 (3) will not generate any deg 2 atom).
Suppose p belongs to Sq for some q as in Definition 10.1 (3), then since MU is a forest, we know that p has
at most one parent in Sq , so it must have deg 3 when it is cut, while at the same time n also has deg 3. By
Definition 10.1 (3), we then know that p belongs to a {33A}-component in M′ .
   As a conclusion, we know that each component A of MD that contains a cycle, must either contain one
atom in a {33A}-component in M′ , or intersect a particle line of some atom in some {33A}-component in
M′ . Clearly each {33A}-component can be so obtained by at most 6 components (as each particle line can
intersect at most one component of MD ), so we get #{33A} ≥ #rec.comp(MD ) /6.                             □
                                                                                                ∗ −1

10.4. Layer selection. By Remark 10.11, and hence R ≪ ρ, which implies that ℓ′ =0 sℓ′ ∼∗ ρ, i.e. most
recollisions are inter-layer recollisions. The final goal in this section is to reduce Proposition 9.7 to the special
case of a two-layer molecule MU D formed by a top layer MU and a bottom layer MD , which are both forests
and are connected by ≳ ρ many UD connections. This reductin relies on the layer refinement process defined
in Section 10.3, and the layer selection process to be introduced below.
   The idea of layer selection is to identify two special thin layers within M, which will (essentially) be set
as MU and MD , such that the number of recollisions directly involving them is ≳ ρ. To do this, we first
select a layer Mℓ1 such that sℓ1 −1 ≳ ρ and sℓ1 −1 ≫ sℓ1 . We then refine Mℓ1 into thin layers MTℓ1 ,k , and pick
the highest thin layer ζU = (ℓ1 , γ) within layer ℓ1 such that there exist ≳ ρ particle lines intersecting layer
ℓ1 in the thin layer ζU but not below. Denoting the set of these particle lines by S, we then refine all layers
below ℓ1 and pick the highest thin layer ζD for which ≳ |S| many particle lines in S are connected to each
other or form recollisions (in the sense of Definition 8.1) within the thin layers between ζD (inclusive) and
ζU (not inclusive). This process is made precise in Definition 10.12 and Proposition 10.13 below.

Definition 10.12 (Layer selection). Let (F , D, H ′ ) be fixed, and let M be the CH molecule defined from D
as in Proposition 7.7, which we know satisfies the properties in Proposition 8.5. Assume that |H| + |H ′ \Z| ≤
   ∗ −1                 ∗ −1

values of two thin layers ζU and ζD (in one case it is possible that ζD is absent and we only determine ζU ).
Recall that r(Fℓ ) = H and sℓ = |H|. In what follows we also denote r(F0 ) := H ′ and s0 := |H ′ |.
      (1) Choose 1 ≤ ℓ1 ≤ ℓ such that

Proposition 10.13. The construction process in Definition 10.12 is well-defined. Moreover, in Definition
10.12, consider all the connected components of MT(ζD :ζU ) (or MT<ζU if ζD is absent) that (i) intersect exactly
one particle line in S (as defined in Definition 10.12 (3)), and (ii) does not intersect any other particle line
in r(Fℓ1 −1 ), and (iii) does not contain a cycle. Denote the union of these components by Y . Then, each of
the following quantities is bounded by 10(C5∗ )−1 · ρ′′′ :
    (1) The number of components of MT(ζD :ζU ) \Y ;
    (2) The number of components of M, MT>ζU , MT>ζD , MTζU , and MT[ζD :ζU ] and MT(ζD :ζU ] ;
    (3) The number of components of MTζD that are not connected to MTζU ∪ MT(ζD :ζU ) by a bond.

any component of MTζD that is not connected to MTζU ∪ MT(ζD :ζU ) is also a component of MT[ζD :ζU ] .
    Finally consider (1). As above, with ≪ (C5∗ )−2 · ρ′′′ exceptions, for each component Z of MT(ζD :ζU ) , it
must intersect a particle line p ∈ r(Fℓ1 −1 ) that intersects MT(ℓ1 ,k) for some k ≤ γ. We may assume p does
not intersect MT(ℓ1 ,k) for k < γ (otherwise we again have ≪ (C5∗ )−2 · ρ′′′ choices for Z, using the upper
bound for the number of components of thin layers within layer ℓ1 ), so in particular p ∈ S, which means
that Z intersects some particle line p ∈ S. Let η be the lowest thin layer above ζD . By excluding at most
vη∗ ≤ (C5∗ )−1 · ρ′′′ exceptions, we may assume that p does not form a recollision within MT[η:ζ0 ] , and is not
connected to another particle line p′ ∈ r(Fℓ1 −1 ) via MT[η:ζ0 ] , but this means that Z ⊆ Y by definition of Y .
Putting all these together, we see that the number of components of MT(ζD :ζU ) that are not contained in Y
is at most 2(C5∗ )−1 · ρ′′′ , as desired.                                                                             □
  With the thin layers ζU and ζD chosen, we are now ready to perform the cutting sequence that reduces
M to the UD molecule MU D for which we will prove Proposition 9.7 in Section 11 below.
Definition 10.14. Recall the layer selection process in Definition 10.12 and the definition of Y in Proposition
10.13. Define also the set V to be the union of all components of MTζD that are connected to MTζU ∪ Y by a
bond, and let G be the number of those components of V that are connected to MTζU ∪ MT(ζD :ζU ) by at least
two bonds. Below we define two options of cutting sequences to be applied to M.
      • Option 1. We perform the following cutting operations (note that if ζD is absent, then the steps here
        involving MTζD and MT<ζD will be skipped).
          (i) We cut MT[ζD :ζU ] as free, then cut MT>ζU as free from MT>ζU ∪ MT<ζD and cut it into elementary
              components using UP, then cut MT<ζD into elementary components using DOWN.
         (ii) We cut as free all the components of MT(ζD :ζU ) (or MT<ζU if ζD is absent) that are not in Y , and
              cut each of them into elementary components using UP. If any atom in MTζU or MTζD becomes
              deg 2 then we cut it as free, and repeat until there is no deg 2 atom in MTζU and MTζD .
        (iii) We also cut as free all components of MTζD that are not connected to MTζU ∪ Y by a bond, and
              cut each of them into elementary components using DOWN.
        After the cutting sequence in Option 1, define the remaining molecule to be MU D (or MU if ζD is
        absent); it contains parts of MTζU , MTζD and Y and will be studied in Section 11.
      • Option 2. This cutting sequence applies only to the case where ζD exists, and cuts M completely
        into elementary components. We perform the following cutting operations.
          (i) With V defined as above, we cut MTζU ∪ MT(ζD :ζU ) ∪ V as free, then cut MT>ζU as free from MT<ζD ∪
              MT>ζU ∪ (MTζD \V) and cut it into elementary components using UP, then cut MT<ζD ∪ (MTζD \V)
              into elementary components using DOWN.
         (ii) If a component of V has only one bond connected to MTζU ∪ MT(ζD :ζU ) , then cut it as fixed,
              otherwise cut it as free. Then cut each component into elementary components using DOWN,
              and then cut MTζU ∪ MT(ζD :ζU ) into elementary components using UP.
Proposition 10.15. For Options 1 and 2 in Definition 10.14, we have the followings.
  (1) In any case, the contribution of cuttings in Option 1 to #{4} is at most 100(C5∗ )−1 · ρ′′′ . If ζD is
absent, then after Option 1, we can choose at least (ρ′′′ /2) particle lines p ∈ S such that (i) there exists a
subset T (p) of MU \MTζU which is either empty or a component of MU \MTζU ; (ii) if T (p) ̸= ∅ then it is a
           LONG TIME DERIVATION OF THE BOLTZMANN EQUATION FROM HARD SPHERE DYNAMICS                               123


tree and has only one bond connecting to MTζU , which is along p; (iii) consider the set E0 of all bottom free
ends e at atoms in T (p) (or along p if T (p) = ∅) for all the chosen p, then for at least ρ′′′ /4 chosen particle
lines p, there exist e ∈ E0 corresponding to p, such that we have the restriction |xe − xe′ | ≤ ε1−1/(8d) , where
e′ ̸= e and e′ belongs to either E0 or to some component already cut in Option 1.
    (2) If ζD exists, then after Option 2, M is cut into elementary components with #{33B} = #{44} = 0. We
also have #{4} ≤ 100(C5∗ )−1 · ρ′′′ + G, where G is defined in Definition 10.14 above and equals the number
of components of V that are cut as free in Option 2 (ii). Moreover, each atom in V belongs to a {3}- or
{4}-component.
    (3) If ζD exists, then after Option 1, the molecule MU D consists of parts of MTζU , and MTζD , and Y . Define
MU to be the union of MTζU and Y , and all the components in MTζD that are full (after Option 1 ) and have
exactly one bond connected to MTζU ∪ Y . Let also MD := MU D \MU . Then we have the following properties
for MU and MD :
     (i) Each of MU and MD is a forest and has no deg 2 atoms. No atom in MD is a parent of any atom in
         MU , and MU (and MD ) has no C-top (and C-bottom) fixed end.
    (ii) The number of full (i.e. all deg 4) components of MU is at most 10(C5∗ )−1 · ρ′′′ . Each component in
         MD either has at least two bonds connected to MU , or has one bond connected to MU and one deg
         3 atom.
   (iii) Define the set of UD connections to be the set of bonds connecting an atom in MU and an atom
         in MD . We say two UD connections are connected via MD , if the MD endpoints of them belong
         to the same connected component of MD . Then, there exist at least (ρ′′′ /4) many UD connections,
         such that each UD connection is either connected to another UD connection via MD , or has its MD
         endpoint belonging to component of MD that contains a deg 3 atom.

Proof. We first prove the easier statements, and leave the proof of (3)(iii) to the end. First, all the statements
about #{4} follow from Proposition 10.13 (1)–(3).
                                     ∗
    (1) Suppose ζD is absent (so v(1,0)    ≤ (C5∗ )−1 · ρ′′ in Definition 10.12 (3); the case ℓ1 = 1 is similar, in
which case each T (p) will be empty). Note that |S| = ρ′′ = ρ′′′ by Definition 10.12. Now choose all the
particle lines p ∈ S such that p either intersects a component of Y or does not intersect MT<ζU at all, then
all particle lines p ∈ S are chosen with at most (C5∗ )−1 · ρ′′′ exceptions. For each chosen p, set T (p) to be
the component of Y that intersects p (or ∅ if p does not intersect MT<ζU ). Next, by Proposition 10.8 (7), we
know that with at most (C5∗ )−1 · ρ′′′ exceptions, each chosen p must either form a self-link within M<ζU or be
linked to another particle line in r(Fℓ1 −1 ) via M<ζU . For each such p, define the corresponding bottom free
end e by the self-link condition (or link to another particle line), then these choices satisfy the requirements
(i)–(iii) in (1). Here note that if p is linked to some p′ ∈ r(Fℓ1 −1 ) that is not chosen, then either the atom
at the corresponding free end e′ has been cut in Option 1, or p′ does not contain any atom in MT<ζU ∪ MTζU
(which leads to at most (C5∗ )−1 · ρ′′′ choices for (p, p′ ) and is negligible). In summary, at least ρ′′′ /2 particle
lines p ∈ S must be chosen, and at least ρ′′′ /4 chosen p must satisfy the given restrictions.
    (2) The bound on #{4} is already proved above (with one extra {4}-component for each of the G component
of V cut as free; note that each component cut as fixed will not be full, so they do not contribute {4}-
components). Each atom in V belongs to a {3}- or {4}-component, simply because V ⊆ MTζD which is a
forest, so there will not be any {2}- or {33}-component by Proposition 10.2 (1).
    (3) First each of MTζU and MTζD is a forest and does not have deg 2 atoms, by construction in Option 1
(ii). Moreover MTζU (and MTζD ) also does not have any C-top (C-bottom) fixed end, because cutting as free
any deg 2 atom in MTζU (or MTζD ) does not generate any C-top (or C-bottom) fixed end in MTζU (or MTζD ) if
there is none initially. The fact that no atom in MTζD can be parent of any atom in MTζU is also clear. All
the above properties are preserved after adding Y to MTζU , and adding to MTζU ∪ Y a full component in MTζD
that has only one bond connecting to MTζU ∪ Y (the forest property is preserved because each component of
Y is a tree, and each component of MTζD is also a tree; the other properties are easy to prove, where we also
note that each component of Y has exactly one bond connected to MTζU ).
124                                   YU DENG, ZAHER HANI, AND XIAO MA


    Next, the number of full components of MTζU is at most 10(C5∗ )−1 · ρ′′′ by Proposition 10.13 (2), and this
cannot increase after cutting components not in Y as free. Moreover adding Y and the components in MTζD
will not increase this number, as these added sets are connected to MTζU . Next, each component in MD must
have at least one bond connected to MU (otherwise it would have been cut in Option 1 (iii)), and it must
either have 2 bonds connected to MU , or contain a deg 3 atom (otherwise it would have been included in
MU by the definition of MU ).
    It remains to prove (3)(iii). For p, p′ ∈ r(Fℓ1 −1 ) and ζD ≤ ζ ≤ ζ0 (recall ζ0 is the highest thin layer
below ζU ), define p ∼ζ p′ if the particle lines p and p′ are connected via MT[ζ:ζ0 ] . It is easy to see that ∼ζ
is an equivalence relation, because for any particle line p and any two atoms in p ∩ MT[ζ:ζ0 ] , they must be
connected by a path within MT[ζ:ζ0 ] (which is part of the particle line p).
    Recall the definition of S in Definition 10.12 (3); define S1 to be the set of p ∈ r(Fℓ1 −1 ) that do not
contain any atom in MT(ℓ1 ,k) for k ≤ γ. Define S2 to be the set of p ∈ S that either forms a recollision
within MT[ζD :ζ0 ] or satisfies p ∼ζD p′ for some other p′ ∈ r(Fℓ1 −1 ), but does not have the same property if
ζD is replaced by any ζ > ζD . By (10.19) and (10.20), we have |S1 | < (10C5∗ )−1 · ρ′′′ , |S2 | ≥ (0.9)ρ′′′ and
S1 ∩ S2 = ∅. Since ∼ζD is an equivalence relation, by excluding at most |S1 | elements, we may replace S2
by S3 ⊆ S2 with |S3 | ≥ (0.8)ρ′′′ , and restrict p′ ̸∈ S1 in the relation p ∼ζD p′ above.
    Now we claim that after Option 1, for each p ∈ S3 , there exists a UD connection e in MU D such that (a)
it is either connected to some other UD connection e′ via MD , or has its MD endpoint in a component of
MD that contains at least one deg 3 atom, and (b) it either belongs to p, or its MU endpoint belongs to a
connected component of Y intersecting p. Once this is proved, is follows from (b) that the UD connection
obtained for different p must be different, so the desired result (3)(iii) follows from (a).
    To prove the above claim, first assume there exists p′ ̸= p and p′ ̸∈ S1 such that p ∼ζD p′ . Since p ∈ S3
and p′ ̸∈ S1 , we know that p intersects MTζU , and either does not intersect MT(ζD :ζU ) , or intersects a unique
component Z of MT(ζD :ζU ) contained in Y ; moreover p′ ̸∈ S1 must either intersect MTζU , or intersect a unique
component W ̸= Z of MT(ζD :ζU ) . Since p ∼ζD p′ , by considering the path going from an atom on p to an
atom on p′ within MT[ζD :ζ0 ] and considering the atoms where the path first enters and first exits MTζD , we can
find two bonds e and e′ such that (a) each has only one endpoint in MTζD and these two endpoints (say n and
n′ ) belong to the same component X of MTζD before Option 1, (b) either e belongs to p or the other endpoint
of e belongs to Z, and (c) either e′ belongs to p′ or the other endpoint of e′ belongs to a component Z1 of
MT(ζD :ζU ) other than Z (which may or may not be W ).
    Now we consider the behavior of (e, e′ ) under the cutting operations in Option 1. Clearly each cutting
in Option 1 (ii) cannot break the maximal ov-segment containing e (i.e. cannot cut any atom in this ov-
segment as in Definition 8.6), because we only cut components not contained in Y , and any deg 2 atoms
that are subsequently cut in Option 1 (ii) must belong to MTζD (or MTζU ) and must be O-atom or have two
top (or bottom) fixed ends. Since the component Z has at least one bond connected to MTζU ∪ Y and is
thus not cut as free in Option 1 (iii), we know that e remains a UD connection after Option 1. Moreover, if
the component Z is not affected by the cuttings in Option 1 (i.e. no atom in Z is cut, and no fixed end is
created at atoms in Z), then we must have Z1 ⊆ Y so e′ is also a UD connection that is still connected to
e via MD even after Option 1 ; if X is affected, then the (possibly new) component of X containing n will
contain at least one deg 3 atom after Option 1. In either case the desired property for e follows.
    Finally, if the above p′ does not exist, then p must form a recollision. In this case, using this cycle within
MT[ζD :ζ0 ] and note that MTζD itself is a forest, we can still find the e and e′ satisfying (a)–(c) above, except
that it is now possible to have Z1 = Z; but this does not affect the proof, and the same arguments as above
then proves (3)(iii).                                                                                           □

   We now treat the relatively easy cases when ζD is absent in Definition 10.12, or when there are ≳ ρ weakly
degenerate atoms and pairs in the ζD thin layer (defined in Proposition 10.16 below; this weak degenercy
comes into the estimates for {33B}-components, see Proposition 9.2 (2) and Definition 9.5 (7)). In this case,
the generalized links (Section 7.4) or the weak degeneracies will provide ≳ ρ many good {3}-components.
           LONG TIME DERIVATION OF THE BOLTZMANN EQUATION FROM HARD SPHERE DYNAMICS                              125


Proposition 10.16. Suppose M satisfies the assumptions in Remark 10.11, and we apply the layer selection
process in Definition 10.12. Then, Proposition 9.7 is true if ζD is absent.
   Moreover, define the atom pair {n, n′ } ⊆ V (see Definition 10.14) to be weakly degenerate if both of them
are C-atoms and they are ov-adjacent, and we have the restriction (by indicator functions) |tn −tn′ | ≤ ε1/(8d) .
Define also one atom n ∈ V to be weakly degenerate if it is C-atom and we have the restriction |ve − ve′ | ≤
ε1/(8d) for two distinct edges (e, e′ ) at n. Then, if G ≤ (C3∗ )−1 · ρ′′′ (with G defined in Proposition 10.15) and
we insert the indicator function 1wea.deg whose support satisfies that there exist at least A := (C2∗ )−1 · ρ′′′
weakly degenerate atoms and atom pairs in V, then Proposition 9.7 is also true.
Proof. (1) If ζD is absent, let MU be the remaining molecule after applying Option 1 in Definition 10.14. We
simply cut it into elementary components using UP. Clearly #{33B} = #{44} = 0, and #{4} ≤ 200(C5∗ )−1 ·ρ′′′
by Proposition 10.15 (the number of full components of MU equals that of MTζU after Option 1 in Definition
10.14, which is at most 200(C5∗ )−1 · ρ′′′ ).
   Next, for each p as in Proposition 10.15 (1) and the corresponding bottom end e, consider the maximal
ov-segment containing e in MU , and first atom that is cut in this ov-segment. Since MU is a forest, this
atom cannot belong to a {2}-component; it may belong to a {4}-component but in at most 200(C5∗ )−1 · ρ
cases, otherwise it must belong to either a {33A}-component (which can be treated as good, see Remark
10.11) or a {3}-component X . Moreover, if we have the restriction |xe − xe′ | ≤ ε1−1/(8d) for some e′ , which
belongs to a component that is either X or is in MU and cut before X or is cut in Option 1 of Definition
10.14, then this X must be good component due to Definition 9.5 (5).
   In summary, from the ≥ ρ′′′ /4 particle lines p in Proposition 10.15 (1), we obtain at least ρ′′′ /20 good
components. Using also that #{4} ≤ 200(C5∗ )−1 · ρ′′′ and that ρ′′′ ≥ (C7∗ )−10L · ρ, this proves (10.17) which
implies (9.76).
   (2) Assume ζD exists, and G ≤ (C3∗ )−1 · ρ′′′ , and there exist at least A weakly degenerate atoms and
atom pairs in V. By decomposing 1 into at most | log ε|C1 A indicator functions, we may fix one choice of
these atoms and pairs. Then we apply Option 2 of Definition 10.14 and cut M into elementary components.
Clearly #{33B} = #{44} = 0, and
                                  #{4} ≤ 100(C5∗ )−1 · ρ′′′ + G ≤ 2(C3∗ )−1 · ρ′′′
by Proposition 10.15 (2) and assumption. Moreover, if we exclude the (at most 2(C3∗ )−1 · ρ′′′ ) exceptional
atoms in V that belong to {4}-components, then each atom in V belongs to a {3}-component. In addition,
if n is a weakly degenerate atom then {n} is a good component by Definition 9.5 (5); if {n, n′ } is a weakly
degenerate pair, and n is cut before n′ , then {n′ } is also a good component by Definition 9.5 (5). In either
case, from the A weakly degenerate atoms and pairs we get at least A/4 good components. Using also that
                                    #{4} ≤ 2(C3∗ )−1 · ρ′′′ ,   ρ′′′ ≥ (C8∗ )−1 · ρ,
this proves (10.17) and (9.76).                                                                                   □
   Now, summarizing Propositions 10.3–10.5, 10.10 and 10.16, we see that, for the rest of the proof of
Proposition 9.7, we may assume that the assumptions in Remark 10.11 are satisfied, that ζD in Definition
10.12 exists, and that either G ≥ (C3∗ )−1 · ρ′′′ with G defined in Proposition 10.15 or we can insert the
indicator function 1 − 1weadeg to restrict the number of weakly degenerate atoms and pairs in V to be less
than A.
   We then apply Option 1 in Definition 10.14 and reduce to the molecule MU D that satisfies (3)(i)–(3)(iii)
of Proposition 10.15. Note that all components of MTζD not contained in V (cf. Definition 10.14) have
already been cut, and those components in V that has only one bond connected to MTζU ∪ MT(ζU :ζD ) have
been included in MU . Moreover, for each of the G components of V that has at least two bonds connected
to MTζU ∪ MT(ζU :ζD ) , even after Option 1 in Definition 10.14, it must still contain a component that has at
least one UD connection and either another UD connection or a deg 3 atom (as any UD connection cannot
be broken by Option 1 in Definition 10.14, as is shown in the proof of Proposition 10.15). Therefore, after
Option 1 in Definition 10.14, the number of components of MD is still at least G.
126                                  YU DENG, ZAHER HANI, AND XIAO MA


   As such, using also the upper bound on the contribution to #{4} of Option 1 in Definition 10.14 (proved
in Proposition 10.15), the remaining parts of Proposition 9.7 can be deduced from the following

Proposition 10.17. Let MU D be a molecule satisfying the properties (3)(i)–(3)(iii) in Proposition 10.15
with some ρ′′′ ≥ (C8∗ )−1 · ρ. Moreover assume that either the number of components of MD satisfies
#comp(MD ) ≥ (C3∗ )−1 · ρ′′′ , or (we can restrict by some indicator function such that) the number of weakly
degenerate atoms and pairs in MD is less than (C2∗ )−1 · ρ′′′ . Then in either case, we can cut MU D into
elementary components such that #{44} = 0 and each {33B}-component is good, and moreover
                                   1
                                      · (#good ) − d · (#{4} ) ≥ (C4∗ )−1 · ρ′′′ ,                      (10.21)
                                  10d
where we again understand that all {33A}-components are good as in Remark 10.11.

                        11. Cutting algorithm II: analyzing UD molecules
   In this section we prove Proposition 10.17 for the two-layer molecule MU D . The logical outline of this sec-
tion is presented in Figure 24 below. We start with a modification of the UP algorithm, called 2CONNUP
(Definition 11.1), which is used to handle the case when there are ≳ ρ many 2-connections, i.e. atoms in MU
(or MD ) that are ov-adjacent to two atoms in MD (MU ) along two different particle lines, see Proposition
11.3. After this, we can assume that there are only ≪ ρ many 2-connections, so we can get rid of at negligible
cost if we need to.
   Then, let X be the set of atoms in MU that are connected to MD by a bond (for the precise definition
see X2 in Definition 11.10). We have the following dichotomy: either the number of ov-components of the
subgraph X ⊂ MU satisfies λ ≳ ρ, or λ ≪ ρ. In the first case we simply cut MD as free, so that X will be
the set of deg 3 atoms in MU . The fact that λ ≳ ρ implies that the deg 3 atoms in MU are separated by deg
4 atoms; we can then define a variation of UP, called 3COMPUP (Definition 11.6), that deals with this
scenario and obtains ≳ λ ≳ ρ many good {33}-components (Proposition 11.7).
   On the other hand, if λ ≪ ρ, then we will apply a different algorithm called MAINUD (Definition
11.10), which involves always cutting the lowest atom in X ⊂ MU and applying a greedy scheme in MD .
This leads to ≳ ρ many {33}-components, while only other hand the number of bad {4}-components is
bounded by λ ≪ ρ, see Proposition 11.11. Finally, a special procedure may be involved before applying the
greedy scheme in MD , which we refer to as pre-processing (Definition 11.8 and Proposition 11.9).




        Figure 24. Outline of this section, where the aim is to prove Proposition 10.17 for the
        two-layer molecule MU D .

   To simplify notation in this section, if a molecule MU D = MU ∪ MD satisfies that (i) each of MU and MD
is a forest and has no deg 2 atoms, and (ii) no atom in MD is parent of any atom in MU , then we say it is a
           LONG TIME DERIVATION OF THE BOLTZMANN EQUATION FROM HARD SPHERE DYNAMICS                          127


UD molecule. If in addition MU has no C-top fixed end and MD has no C-bottom fixed end, then we say
MU D is canonical.
 We start by introducing the following algorithm, which is used in the case when there are many atoms in
MU (or MD ) that either have two UD connections, or have one UD connection and also have deg 3.

Definition 11.1 (The algorithm 2CONNUP). Define the following cutting algorithm 2CONNUP. It
takes as input any canonical UD molecule MU D , such that MD has no full component that has no bond
connected to MU . Define M12conn to be the set of atoms n ∈ MD that have deg 3 and are ov-adjacent to
one atom m ∈ MU , such that no other atom in MD on the ov-segment between n and m has deg 3. Define
M22conn to be the set of atoms n ∈ MD that are ov-adjacent to two atoms in MU along two different particle
lines, and M2conn = M12conn ∪ M22conn . For any such M = MU D , we define the cutting sequence as follows:
    (1) If any particle line contains at least 400d2 atoms in M22conn , then we choose n0 to be any one of these
        atoms, and cut it as free. Repeat until no such particle line exists.
    (2) If M contains any deg 2 atom n, then cut it as free, and repeat until there is no deg 2 atom left; if
        MU contains only deg 4 atoms, then choose a lowest atom n in MU and cut it as free.
    (3) Choose an atom n ∈ MU that has deg 3, which is the lowest among all atoms of deg 3 in MU . Let
        Sn be the set of descendants of n in MU .
    (4) Now, starting from n, each time choose a highest atom m in Sn . If m has deg 3, and m is ov-adjacent
        to an atom p ∈ M2conn that also has deg 3, then cut {m, p} as free. Otherwise just cut m as free.
        Repeat until all atoms in Sn has been cut. Then go to (2).
    (5) Repeat (2)–(4) until all atoms in MU has been cut. Then apply algorithm DOWN to cut the rest
        of MD into elementary components.
We also define the dual algorithm 2CONNDN, by reversing the roles of MU and MD and the notions of
parent/child etc., just as how we define DOWN from UP in Definition 10.1.

  The key estimate for the algorithm 2CONNUP is stated in the following proposition.

Proposition 11.2. For the algorithm 2CONNUP, we have #{33B} = #{44} = 0, and
                     #{33A} − 50d2 · #{4} ≥ C −1 (|M12conn | + |M22conn |) − C · #fucp(MU ) ,            (11.1)
where #fucp(MU ) is the number of full components of MU . Similar results hold for 2CONNDN, if we reverse
the roles of MU and MD .

Proof. It is clear that throughout the algorithm 2CONNUP, there is no C-bottom fixed end in Sn \{n},
and there is no C-top fixed end in (MU \Sn ) ∪ {n}, and there is no C-bottom fixed end in MD . The first two
claims are proved in the same way as in Proposition 10.2, and the third claim is also easy to verify. It then
follows that we only have {2}, {3}, {4} and {33A}-components in M′ , in particular #{33B} = #{44} = 0, so
we only need to prove (11.1).
    Consider the set L of all the particle lines that contain at least one atom in MU and at least one atom
in M2conn . For each such particle line p, let σp ⊆ p be the unique maximal ov-segment that contains atoms
in both MU and MD ; note that M2conn ∩ p ⊆ σp . Clearly each atom in Mj2conn (j ∈ {1, 2}) belongs to
exactly j particle lines in L; we define p ∼ p′ if they intersects at an atom in M22conn . We know that any p
and p′ cannot intersect at two atoms in M2conn (Remark 8.3); also since MD is a forest, there cannot exist
p1 , · · · , pq ∈ L such that pj ∼ pj+1 with pq+1 = p1 . If we define the auxiliary graph G with nodes p ∈ L
and edges (p, p′ ) where p ∼ p′ , then this G is a forest.
    Recall by Definition 8.6 that, if any atom (or any two atoms) in σp is cut as free, then we either get a
{33A}-component, or get a fixed end at each remaining atom in σp . In this case we say σp is broken. Note
that each cutting in Definition 11.1 (1)–(4) breaks at most two σp ; moreover, if a cutting in Definition 11.1
(2)–(4) breaks both σp and σp′ , then p ̸∼ p′ , again due to Remark 8.3. Now suppose p ∼ p′ , and σp is
broken before σp′ , and σp′ is broken in a cutting operation in Definition 11.1 (4), then this cutting operation
must involve some m ∈ MU that belongs to σp′ . Suppose m ∈ Sn as in Definition 11.1 (4), since MU is a
128                                    YU DENG, ZAHER HANI, AND XIAO MA


forest, we know that m has at most one parent in Sn , so m must have deg 3 when it is cut in Definition 11.1
(4). Let p = p ∩ p′ ∈ M22conn , then the previously broken σp provides a fixed end at p, so p also has deg 3 at
the time when m is cut. By construction in Definition 11.1 (4), we get a {33A}-component with higher atom
m, such that p′ is one of the two particle lines at m. In the same way, if p contains an atom in M1conn and
σp is broken in a cutting operation in Definition 11.1 (4), then we also get a {33A}-component with higher
atom m, such that p is one of the two particle lines at m.
   Let L0 ⊆ L be the set of particle lines selected in Definition 11.1 (1), and L1 ⊇ L0 be the set of all particle
lines p where σp is broken in Definition 11.1 (1), then we have |L1 | ≤ 2|L0 |. Recall the auxiliary graph G
defined above; let N (L1 ) be the set of nodes in L\L1 that are adjacent to a node in L1 , and let P1 be the
set of edges in G connecting a node in L1 to a node in L\L1 . Since each node p ∈ L0 has at least 400d2
edges in G, and using also that N (L1 ) ∪ L1 contains no cycle in G, we obtain that
                               |P1 | ≤ |N (L1 )| + |L1 |,   |P1 | ≥ 400d2 |L0 | − 2|L1 |.                     (11.2)
Moreover, let (p1 , · · · , ps ) be the permutation of p ∈ L\L1 in the order of σp being broken in Definition 11.1
(2)–(4), then by construction in Definition 11.1 (1), each pj is adjacent to at most 400d2 nodes pk (k ̸= j)
in G. Let P2 be the set of edges in G among these pj , then |P1 | + |P2 | ≥ |M22conn | − |L1 | (where |M22conn | is
the total number of edges in G).
   Now, by the above discussion, for each p ∈ N (L1 ), and each p containing an atom in M12conn , and each
p = pj that is adjacent to some pk (k < j) in G, if σp is broken in a cutting operation in Definition 11.1
(4), we get a {33A}-component corresponding to p when σp is broken, and each {33A}-component can be
so obtained from at most two such p. In the case p = pj , each j also corresponds to at most 400d2 different
(pj , pk ) edges in G. This implies that
                                        1
                            #{33A} ≥ max |P1 | − |L1 |, |M12conn |, (400d2 )−1 |P2 | − #exc ,
                                                                                    
                                                                                                            (11.3)
                                        2
where #exc is the number of atoms cut in Definition 11.1 (1) plus the number of deg 4 atoms cut in (2), as
cutting any deg 2 atom in Definition 11.1 (2) will not break any σp .
   On the other hand, it is easy to see that the number of deg 4 atoms cut in (2) does not exceed the number
of full components #fucp(MU ) of MU , and also max(#exc , #{4} ) ≤ #fucp (MU ) + |L1 |, because (i) any new
component of MU generated in the cutting sequence cannot be full, and (ii) any component of MD also
cannot be full when all atoms in MU are cut. By combining these inequalities with (11.2) and (11.3) and
elementary calculations, we then get (11.1).                                                                     □

   Using the algorithm 2CONNUP (and its dual 2CONNDN) and Proposition 11.2, we can prove Propo-
sition 10.17 under the assumption that we either have ≳ ρ components for MD , or have ≳ ρ atoms in MU
(including the 2-connections) that become deg 2 after cutting MD as free. In the first scenario, we may
cut MU as free and apply DOWN to MD to get ≳ ρ good {33}-components, unless there are ≳ ρ many
2-connections in MD , in which case we may apply 2CONNUP; in the second scenario we directly apply
2CONNDN.

Proposition 11.3. Proposition 10.17 is true if either #comp(MD ) ≥ (C3∗ )−1 ·ρ′′′ , or if #comp(MD ) ≤ (C3∗ )−1 ·ρ′′′
       2                                2

Remark 11.4. With Proposition 11.3, in the remaining proof of Proposition 10.17, we may now assume for

      • That #comp(MD ) ≤ (C3∗ )−1 · ρ′′′ , and the number of weakly degenerate atoms and pairs in MD is less
