  The rest of this section is devoted to the proof of Proposition 10.17 under the assumption of Remark 11.4.
For this we need to introduce two more algorithms. We start with the following auxiliary lemma.

Proposition 11.5. Let MU D be a UD molecule such that MD = ∅, and MU does not have C-top fixed
end. Suppose A ⊆ MU is ov-connected within MU (cf. Definition 8.2) such that each atom in A has deg 3.
Then A contains a unique lowest atom n, such that (i) each atom m ∈ A is obtained from n by iteratively
taking ov-parents in A, and (ii) each ov-child of m ∈ A (m ̸= n) is either one of the terms preceding m in the
ov-parent sequence defined in (i), or an O-atom between two consecutive terms preceding m, or an ov-child
of n.
   Similarly, if we assume MD ̸= ∅, A is ov-connected within MU , each atom of A is C-atom, and each deg
4 atom of A has at most one ov-child in A, then conclusion (i) is still true.

Proof. Choose a lowest atom n ∈ A. We will prove (i), which also establishes the uniqueness of n, and prove
(ii) in the process. For any m ∈ A, there exists a sequence pj ∈ A (0 ≤ j ≤ r) such that (p0 , pr ) = (n, m)
and pj+1 is ov-adjacent to pj ; we may assume this sequence is shortest. Now, since n is lowest, p1 must
be ov-parent of p0 = n; since p1 has deg 3 (thus it has one bottom fixed end), we know that p2 must be
ov-parent of p1 (otherwise p2 is ov-child of p1 along the same maximal ov-segment containing p1 and p0 ,
which means p2 is ov-adjacent to p0 and we can skip p1 to get a shorter sequence), and so on. This proves
(i). Then (ii) follows from the same proof (any ov-child of pj is either an ov-child of pj−1 , or belongs to the
ov-segment between pj and pj−1 ). The proof of (i) in the case MD ̸= ∅ is similar, where we note that pj has
at most one ov-child in A in the case when pj has deg 4, so pj+1 still cannot be ov-child of pj if pj+1 ∈ A
and is C-atom.                                                                                                □

   We are now ready to define the algorithm 3COMPUP (and its inverse version 3COMPDN), which
will be used on either MU or MD in situations when the subgraph formed by deg 3 atoms has ≳ ρ many
ov-components.
130                                    YU DENG, ZAHER HANI, AND XIAO MA


Definition 11.6 (The algorithm 3COMPUP). Define the following cutting algorithm 3COMPUP. It
takes as input any molecule M, such that M is a forest, M has no deg 2 atoms and each component of M
has at least two deg 3 atoms, and M has no C-top fixed end. Denote the set of deg 3 atoms of M by M(3) .
For any such M, we define the cutting sequence as follows:
       (1) If a connected subset A ⊆ M contains only deg 4 atoms, and has only one bond connected to M\A,
           then cut A as fixed and cut it into elementary components using either UP or DOWN. Then we
           recursively apply 3COMPUP to each component of M\A.
                  (3)
       (2) Let Mmin be the set of deg 3 atoms n of M that is lowest among all deg 3 atoms, and let Sn be the
                                                         (3)
           set of descendants of n. If there exists n ∈ Mmin such that Sn has only one bond connected to M\Sn
           at an atom r ∈ Sn \{n}, then cut Sn as fixed. Then cut n as free from Sn (or cut {n, p} as free if n
           is ov-adjacent to an atom p ∈ Sn \{n} of deg 3), and cut the rest of Sn into elementary components
           using DOWN. After this, we cut any component of M\Sn that contains only one deg 3 atom into
           elementary components using either UP or DOWN, and then recursively apply 3COMPUP to
           other components of M\Sn .
                           (3)
       (3) For any n ∈ Mmin , we say n is remote if Sn = {n}, and n is also one terminal of a maximal ov-segment
           σ whose other terminal is an ov-parent atom p of n, such that (i) all atoms in σ except n have deg
           4, and (ii) no bond exists between σ\{p} and M\σ.
                                (3)
       (4) If there exists n ∈ Mmin that is not remote, we choose this n and let Sn be the set of descendants of
           n. Let B be the ov-component of M(3) ∪ (Sn \{n}) that contains n (and Sn \{n}).
                                (3)                                    (3)
       (5) If every atom n ∈ Mmin is remote, then choose an n ∈ Mmin such that the corresponding p defined
           in (3) is lowest among all such atoms p. Let B = {n}.
       (6) After choosing n and B as in (3)–(5) above, we cut B as free; if B is not already elementary, we cut
           it into elementary components using UP, and fix the first atom we choose in Definition 10.1 (2) to
           be n. Then we recursively apply 3COMPUP to each non-elementary component of M\B.
      We also define the dual algorithm 3COMPDN by reversing the orders, in the same way as before.
Proposition 11.7. Let the number of ov-components of M(3) be #3ovcp , and the number of components of
M be #comp . Then for the algorithm 3COMPUP (and same for 3COMPDN), we have
                                                                    1              1
                      #{33B} = #{44} = #{4} = 0;        #{33A} ≥       · #3ovcp +    · #comp .            (11.6)
                                                                   100            10
Proof. The proof is by induction on the size of M.
   Step 1. The parts concerning #{33B} , #{44} and #{4} are easy to show, so we only need to prove the
lower bound of #{33A} . First, if A exists as in Definition 11.6 (1), then after cutting A as fixed, it will have
at most one C-fixed end, so we can subsequently apply UP or DOWN to A. Moreover, all atoms in A
initially have deg 4, and cutting A as fixed does not affect the degree of any atom in M\A. If a component
B of M\A after cutting A as fixed contains at most one deg 3 atom, then originally A ∪ B also contains at
most one deg 3 atom, which is not possible by assumption. Therefore, cutting A as fixed does not decrease
the value of #3ovcp or #comp , and does not violate the assumptions of Definition 11.6. This means that
(11.6) follows from the induction hypothesis (11.6) for components of M\A.
   Next, assume n exists as in Definition 11.6 (2). Similar to the above, we know that the operations in
Definition 11.6 (2) decrease #comp by at most 1 and #3ovcp by at most 2, and does not violate the assumptions
of Definition 11.6. Moreover, after cutting Sn as free and cutting n as free from Sn , the remaining Sn will
contain no full component, no C-bottom fixed end and no deg 2 atoms (unless n is ov-adjacent to some
p ∈ Sn \{n} of deg 3, in which case we should cut {n, p} as in Definition 11.6 (2) and get a {33A}-component).
This remaining Sn must also contain two deg 3 atoms in the same component, which guarantees #{33A} ≥ 1
for the operations in Definition 11.6 (2), by Proposition 10.2, so (11.6) again follows from the induction
hypothesis (11.6) for components of M\A.
                                                                                        (3)
   Now suppose no scenario in Definition 11.6 (1) or (2) exists, and choose any n ∈ Mmin as in Definition 11.6
(3). We first consider the case when n is not remote; in this case B is the ov-component of M(3) ∪ (Sn \{n})
          LONG TIME DERIVATION OF THE BOLTZMANN EQUATION FROM HARD SPHERE DYNAMICS                          131


that contains n, and we cut B as free. Clearly B does not have any C-top fixed end, so we can apply UP
                                                                                            (3)
to B; also we can fix the first atom we choose in Definition 10.1 (2) to be n, as n ∈ Mmin . Note that,
if Sn ̸= {n}, then by Definition 10.1, all atoms in Sn \{n} will be cut in subsequent steps; moreover, if
r ∈ Sn \{n} is ov-adjacent to any deg 3 atom t ̸= n, then r must belong to a {33A}-component by Definition
10.1 (3). Let µ be the number of ov-components of M(3) that are contained in B and do not contain n.
Then the contribution to #{33A} , provided by the steps in the process of cutting B, is at least µ; also this
contribution is at least 1 if the ov-component of M(3) that contains n does not equal {n}. Finally, cutting
B as free will not introduce any C-top fixed end in M\B. This follows from the proof of Proposition 11.5,
where we also notice that the lowest atom of each component of M(3) in B is either n or an ov-parent of an
atom in Sn \{n}, and each ov-child of any atom in Sn \{n} still belongs to Sn \{n}.
   Consider the effect of cutting B as free. Since M is a forest, after this cutting, we get a set of newly
generated components. Using that M is a forest, we know that each new component M1 has exactly one
bond in an ov-segment with B (viewed as a free end within M1 ) that is turned into a fixed end. Moreover,
each M1 does not contain any deg 2 atom (otherwise this deg 2 atom would be deg 3 in M and ov-adjacent
to an atom in B, so it would have belonged to B which is impossible), so after this cutting, exactly one
deg 4 atom in M1 has become deg 3. Also M1 must contain another deg 3 atom (unless M1 is itself a
{3}-component which is elementary), otherwise it would be all deg 4 before the cutting, which contradicts
the nonexistence of Definition 11.6 (1).
   Next, for each M1 , denote its new deg 3 atom by m. This m may decrease the number of ov-components
of the set of deg 3 atoms in M - in fact, this is the only reason that this number of ov-components may
decrease in addition to B being cut - by merging several ov-components of deg 3 atoms in M1 . In other
words, any ov-segment between two deg 3 atoms in M1 either comes from an ov-segment already existing
in M, or has m as one of its terminals. However, m belongs to at most 3 ov-segments, and the other atoms
on these ov-segments are already ov-connected before m is turned into deg 3. This means that within the
component M1 , at most 3 ov-components of deg 3 atoms can be merged together, so the contribution of M1
to #3comp decrease by at most 2.
   As such, let the number of new non-{3}-components M1 be q, then after cutting B as free and ignoring
the (elementary) {3}-components, the increments of the quantities are

                      ∆(#comp ) = q − 1,    ∆(#3ovcp ) ≥ −(2q + µ + 1);    #{33A} ≥ µ,                  (11.7)

where µ is the number of ov-components of M(3) that are contained in B and do not contain n, and #{33A}
only counts those created in the process of cutting B. Since we apply 3COMPUP recursively to each of
these components, we know that (11.6) will follow from the induction hypothesis (11.6) for these components,
together with (11.7), unless q ∈ {0, 1} and #{33A} = µ = 0; in the latter case we must have B = Sn .
   Now suppose q ∈ {0, 1} and #{33A} = µ = 0, then we must have q = 1 (otherwise B = Sn has only one
deg 3 atom and is also a component of M, which is impossible). In this case, if Sn ̸= {n}, then Sn \{n}
must have a bond connected to M\Sn (otherwise violating the nonexistence of Definition 11.6 (1)), and this
bond is also the only bond between Sn and M\Sn since q = 1. But this n then violates the nonexistence of
Definition 11.6 (2).
   If instead Sn = {n} (so B = {n}) and q = 1, let the atoms in one maximal ov-segment involved in cutting
n as free be (n1 = n, n2 , · · · , nr ), then each nj must have deg 4, and nr must be adjacent to some other
atom in M (otherwise contradicting Definition 11.6 (1)). Since the number of non-{3}-components does not
increase after cutting n as free (i.e. q = 1), the only possibility is that each nj (j < r) has no bond with
any atom in M other than nj−1 and nj+1 . By Definition 11.6 (3), this means n is remote, contradicting the
assumption that n is not remote.
   Step 2. At this time, we are only left with the case where n is remote, and B = {n}. Let p = nr as above,
note that n is chosen by Definition 11.6 (5), so p is lowest in its class. If p is not ov-adjacent to any deg 3
atom other than n, then we will cut n as free, and note that p is the only atom that becomes deg 3 in the
resulting molecule (again ignoring the {3}-components). Moreover this new deg 3 atom is not ov-adjacent to
132                                   YU DENG, ZAHER HANI, AND XIAO MA


any existing ov-component of deg 3 atoms, thanks to the non-ov-adjacent assumption made above. In this
case we have ∆(#comp ) = 0 and ∆(#3ovcp ) = 0 instead of (11.7) because the new deg 3 atom p increases
the number of ov-components of deg 3 atoms by 1, which balances the removed deg 3 atom n. Since also
#{33A} = 0 for the cutting of n, we again proves (11.6) by induction hypothesis.
   The final possibility is when p is ov-adjacent to some deg 3 atom q ̸= n. Let Sp be the set of descendants
                                                                                           (3)
of p. If Sp contains a deg 3 atom other than n, then it contains an atom n ̸= n1 ∈ Mmin . By assumption
in Definition 11.6 (5) we know n1 is remote, so it corresponds to a unique p1 in Definition 11.6 (3), just as
n corresponds to p. However, there is a path from n1 to p formed by iteratively taking parents (because
n1 ∈ Sp ), and by the remoteness assumption, this path must pass through p1 . By the lowest assumption of
p, we know the only possibility is p1 = p, so Sp consists only of the two ov-segments from p to n and n1 . On
the other hand, if Sp does not contain any deg 3 atom other than n, then q must be an ov-parent of p, and
p belongs to the new set M  e (3) of lowest deg 3 atoms after cutting B = {n} as free.

   Recall the notion of ov-proper molecules from Definition 8.2. We now define a function SELECT acting
on UD molecule that outputs a subset S ⊂ MD , such that the molecule MD becomes ov-proper after cutting
this set S as free.
Definition 11.8 (The function SELECT). Let M be any UD molecule, such that MD has no deg 2 atoms,
and has no C-bottom fixed end. Let Z be the set of deg 3 atoms in MD , and Y be a subset of the set of deg
4 atoms in MD . Given (MD , Z, Y ), we define a set S := SELECT(MD , Z, Y ) as follows:
      (1) Consider all the ov-components of Z ∪ Y in MD (this means we only consider the O-atoms in MD
          when defining ov-connectedness in Definition 8.2), which is a finite collection of disjoint subsets of
          MD .
      (2) If any two of these subsets, say A and B, have the shortest ov-distance (within MD ) which is at
          most 4, then we choose the shortest path between an atom in A and an atom in B, which can
          be divided into at most 4 ov-segments. Write this path as (n0 , · · · , nq ), where q ≤ 4 and n0 ∈ A,
          nq ∈ B, and each (nj , nj+1 ) is an ov-segment. Then replace the two sets A and B by a single set
          A ∪ B ∪ {n1 , · · · , nq−1 }.
      (3) Repeat (2) until this can no longer be done (i.e. when only one set is left, or any two sets have of
          distance at least 5). Then output S := SELECT(MD , Z, Y ) as the union of all the current sets.
Proposition 11.9. Let the function SELECT be as in Definition 11.8. Then we have the followings:
      (1) After cutting S := SELECT(MD , Z, Y ) from M as free, then the set MD \S in the resulting molecule
          M\S is ov-proper (i.e. does not contain deg 2 atoms, and the ov-distance between any two deg 3
          atoms is at least 3). Note that MD \S may have both C-top and C-bottom fixed ends, however this
          is compensated by the good property of being ov-proper.
      (2) Consider all the maximal ov-segments in M that contains at least one atom in S and one atom in
          MU . Then the number of these maximal ov-segments does not exceed 10(#3ovcp(Z) + |Y |), where
          #3ovcp(Z) is the number of ov-components of Z (i.e. the set of all deg 3 atoms in MD ), where the
          ov-components are determined by considering MD only.
Proof. (1) At the end of the construction in Definition 11.8 (3), we get a number of disjoint subsets, such
that each one is ov-connected and the ov-distance between each two of them is at least 5, and S is the union
          LONG TIME DERIVATION OF THE BOLTZMANN EQUATION FROM HARD SPHERE DYNAMICS                          133


of all these subsets. Note also that the sub-molecule MD \S of the molecule formed by cutting S as free from
M, is equal to the molecule formed by cutting S as free from MD .
    Now suppose after cutting S as free, there exist two deg 3 atoms n, n′ ∈ MD \S of ov-distance at most 2;
the case of a deg 2 atom in MD \S can be treated similarly. Since n, n′ ̸∈ S, they must have deg 4 initially in
MD . As they become deg 3 after cutting S as free, we know that for each of n and n′ , it must be ov-connected
to an atom in S. Assume say n is ov-connected to m ∈ S, and n′ to m′ ∈ S. If m and m′ belong to two
different subsets A and B defined above, then the ov-distance between m and m′ (and hence the ov-distance
between A and B) is at most 1 + 2 + 1 = 4, contradicting the assumption about these subsets. Therefore,
m and m′ belong to the same set A defined above. But then there exists a path µ1 : m → n → n′ → m′
(composed of ov-segments), and another path µ2 between m and m′ composed of ov-segments, such that
all terminals of ov-segments in µ2 belong to S. Since MD is a forest, we know that µ2 must coincide with
µ1 , but this is impossible as µ1 cannot be decomposed into ov-segments with all terminals belonging to S
(unless m and m′ are ov-adjacent in MD and the path between n and n′ is part of this ov-segment, but in
this case this path between n and n′ will no longer exists after cutting S as free, see Definition 8.6 (3)).
    (2) In each step in Definition 11.8 (2) we add at most 3 atoms to the union of the current subsets.
Moreover, the number of such steps that can be carried out cannot exceed the initial number of subsets (as
each step reduces the number of subsets by at least 1), which is the number of ov-components of Z ∪ Y ,
and is at most #3ovcp(Z) + |Y |. This means that the number of newly added atoms in the construction of S
(starting from Z ∪ Y ) is at most 3(#3ovcp(Z) + |Y |).
    Therefore, we only need to consider the required maximal ov-segments that contains an atom in Z. But
by (the dual version of) Proposition 11.5 (ii), if such maximal ov-segment contains an atom in one ov-
component W of Z, then it must contain the unique highest atom of W . Therefore, the number of such
maximal ov-segments does not exceed 2 · #3ovcp(Z) (since #3ovcp(Z) is the number of ov-components of Z in
MD ), as desired.                                                                                            □

   We are now ready to define the second algorithm, called MAINUD, that will be used in the proof of
Proposition 10.17. This is basically a greedy algorithm within MD that prioritizes cutting {33} (and {343})
components whenever they appear before moving back to MU . This algorithm also requires that the molecule
MD is ov-proper, which is why we need to perform preprocessing by using the function SELECT before
executing the algorithm MAINUD.

Definition 11.10 (The algorithm MAINUD). Define the following cutting algorithm MAINUD. It takes
as input any UD molecule MU D that satisfies the following assumptions:
                                                 2                    2
    (i) MU contains no C-top fixed end, and M2conn = ∅, where M2conn is defined as in Proposition 11.3.
   (ii) MD is ov-proper (Definition 8.2), and does not have any full component that is not connected to
        MU by a bond.
  For any such M, we define the sets Xj (0 ≤ j ≤ 3) of C-atoms in MU as follows:
     • Define n ∈ X0 if n has deg 3 and has one ov-child in MD .
     • Recursively, define n ∈ X0 , if n has deg 3 and has one ov-child in X0 ∪MD , or if n has two ov-children
       in X0 ∪ MD along two ov-segments in two different particle lines.
     • With X0 fixed, define n ∈ X1 if and only if n ̸∈ X0 , and n either has deg 3 or has one ov-child in
       X0 ∪ MD . In the latter case define σn to be the ov-segment between n and its ov-child in X0 ∪ MD .
     • Define n ∈ X2 , if and only if n ∈ X1 , and n is connected to some (C- or O-) atom MU ∋ m ̸= n that
       either has deg 3 or has one ov-child in MD , by a path in MU that does not contain any atom in
       σn \{n}. Define X3 := X0 ∪ X2 .
  Then, we define the cutting sequence as follows:
    (1) If MU contains any deg 2 atom n, then cut it as free, and repeat until there is no deg 2 atom left.
    (2) Choose an atom n ∈ MU as follows. If all atoms in X3 have been cut, then choose n to be a deg 3
        atom in MU that is lowest among all deg 3 atoms (or any lowest atom in MU if MU only contains
134                                     YU DENG, ZAHER HANI, AND XIAO MA


          deg 4 atoms; in this case we prioritize those n ∈ X1 \X2 and their descendants). If some atom in X3
          has not been cut, then choose m to be lowest among them; if all descendants of m in MU have deg
          4, then choose n = m; otherwise choose n to be a lowest descendant of m in MU that has deg 3. Let
          Sn be the set of descendants of n in MU , and fix it until the end of (6).
      (3) Starting from n, choose a highest atom p in Sn ; if p is ov-adjacent to an atom q ∈ MD , and q has
          deg 3, then cut {p, q} as free, and cut q as free from {p, q} is p has deg 4. Otherwise cut p as free.
      (4) If any two deg 3 atoms (r, r′ ) in MD becomes ov-adjacent (within MD ), then choose {r, r′ } such this
          ov-segment contains the fewest bond, and cut {r, r′ } as free. Repeat until no such instances exist.
      (5) If the ov-distance of any two deg 3 atoms (r, r′ ) in MD becomes 2 (within MD ), say r and r′ are both
          ov-adjacent to some r′′ ∈ MD , then cut {r, r′ , r′′ } as free and then cut r as free from {r, r′ , r′′ }. Go
          to (4). Repeat until the ov-distance of any two deg 3 atoms in MD within MD is at least 3.
      (6) When the ov-distance of any two deg 3 atoms in MD within MD is at least 3, go to (3). Repeat until
          all atoms in Sn have been cut.
      (7) When all atoms in Sn have been cut, go to (1). Repeat until all atoms in MU have been cut.
      (8) When all atoms in MU have been cut, choose any deg 3 atom in MD and cut it as free. If the
          ov-distance between any two deg 3 atoms becomes at most 2, go to (4) and repeat (4)–(5) until the
          ov-distance of any two deg 3 atoms in MD is at least 3 (within MD ). Then choose the next deg 3
          atom in MD and cut it as free, and repeat until all atoms in MD have been cut.

Proposition 11.11. Let M be any UD molecule. Consider the number of UD connections e that are either
(i) connected to some other UD connection via MD , or (ii) connected to a component of MD that contains
deg 3 atoms. We call such UD connections good and let the number of them be #UD . Let #fucp(MU ) be the
number of full components of MU , and let #ovcp(X2 ) be the number of ov-components of the set X2 defined
in Definition 11.10. Then, for the algorithm MAINUD, we have #{44} = 0, and each {33B}-component
must have both its atoms in MD , and that
                           1
      #{33A} + #{33B} ≥      · (#UD − #fucp(MU ) − #ovcp(X2 ) ),    and    #{4} ≤ #fucp(MU ) + #ovcp(X2 ) .     (11.8)
                           5
Proof. We will establish the following three facts.
    (1) At any time in the cutting sequence, there is no C-top fixed end in (MU \Sn ) ∪ {n} and no C-bottom
fixed end in Sn \{n}. In fact, when we choose n as in Definition 11.10 (2), every atom p ∈ Sn \{n} must have
deg 4. In the process of Definition 11.10 (3)–(6), if we only consider the cutting within MU , then the desired
property follows from the same proof as in Proposition 10.2. Now suppose an arbitrary set of atoms in MD
are also cut in the process, then this will only create C-bottom fixed ends, and only at C-atoms t that are
ov-adjacent to some atom in MD . By definition we must have t ∈ X0 ∪ X1 ; if t ∈ Sn \{n} (otherwise it does
not affect the desired property), then by the choice of n we must have t ∈ X1 \X2 . Let T be the set of atoms
that are connected to t by a path in MU that does not contain any atom in σt \{t}, then no bond exists
between T \{t} and (MU \T ) ∪ MD . Consider the first atom s ∈ T \{t} that is cut, then this cutting must
happen before σt is broken (as t still has deg 4 when n is cut), so s must have deg 4 when it is cut. By our
priority of choice in Definition 11.10 (2), and note that no ancestor of s can be in X3 , we know s must be
descendant of t (so s ̸= n); but then at the time n is chosen, not all atoms in Sn \{n} can have deg 4 (because
s has been cut), contradiction. This proves the desired property; similarly, for any {33}-component {p, q}
cut in Definition 11.10 (3), this p must be O-atom or p = n (which has no top fixed end), so this {p, q} must
be {33A}-component. Note also that p cannot have deg 2 in Definition 11.10 (3) because MU is a forest.
    (2) The number of deg 4 atoms n ∈ MU cut in Definition 11.10 (3) is at most #fucp(MU ) + #ovcp(X2 ) . In
fact, there are two possibilities for such n: either the component of MU containing n contains only deg 4
atoms (which leads to at most #fucp(MU ) choices for n), or n = m is a lowest atom in X3 that has not been
cut. If m ∈ X0 , by definition of X0 , we know that either m has deg 3 originally, or it has an ov-child m′ ∈ X0
that has already been cut when m is cut, creating a fixed end at m. In either case m will not be deg 4 when
it is cut.
           LONG TIME DERIVATION OF THE BOLTZMANN EQUATION FROM HARD SPHERE DYNAMICS                                  135


    Now suppose n = m ∈ X2 , then m must belong to one of the initial components of X2 , say A (there are at
most #ovcp(X2 ) choices for A). If any atom m′ ∈ A is an ov-child of m, then again m will not be deg 4 when
it is cut; otherwise each atom in A that is ov-adjacent to m must be an ov-parent of m. By Proposition 11.5
(i), there can be only one such m for each component A, leading to at most #ovcp(X2 ) choices for m.
    (3) Each atom in MD belongs to a {3}- or {33}- (which can be either {33A}- or {33B}-) component. To
see this, note first that initially MD is ov-proper (so does not have deg 2 atoms), and also does not have full
components not connected to MU , so the cuttings in Definition 11.10 (8) will not create any {4}-component.
Next, suppose MD is ov-proper at some point, but becomes no-ov-proper after one cutting operation in
Definition 11.10 (3) (or in Definition 11.10 (8), which is similar). Note this cutting is either cutting p ∈ MU
as free, or cutting {p, q} as free, where p ∈ MU and q ∈ MD are ov-adjacent. Note that p can be ov-adjacent
to atoms q ∈ MD along only one particle line, so the effect of this cutting operation to MD is either (i)
introducing one fixed end at q, breaking the MD part of the ov-segment between p and q and creating one
free and one fixed end at each O-atom in this ov-segment, or (ii) cutting q as free.
    Note that MD is a forest. In case (i), after the operation, q and each of these intermediate O-atoms
must belong to different components of MD ; in case (ii), after the operation, q and each of the (at most 3)
ov-adjacent atoms in MD , as well as each of the intermediate O-atoms, must belong to different components
of MD . Moreover, in each of these components, the effect of this operation is exactly creating one extra fixed
end, and this fixed end must be created at an atom originally of deg 4 in MD (otherwise we will contradict
the construction in Definition 11.10 (3), or contradict the ov-proper assumption of MD ). Therefore, to prove
the desired claim, we only need to prove the following statement:
   (♣) Let M1 be any ov-proper molecule that is a forest. Suppose we turn one free end at a deg 4 atom
       r in M1 into a fixed end (while keeping M1 regular - i.e. if r is O-atom, then this free end that is
       turned into fixed end must be serial with a free end at r), then we can always subsequently perform
       cuttings in Definition 11.10 (4)–(5) to turn M1 back to ov-proper again.
   To prove (♣), the key observation is the following: note that cutting {r, r′ } or {r, r′ , r′′ } as free again
divides M1 into different components, and the effect of this cutting to each individual component is again
creating exactly one fixed end. Now we only need to show that each such fixed end must again be created
at an originally deg 4 atom, which then allows us to pass to each individual component and prove (♣) by
induction.
   Now suppose some fixed end is created at an originally deg 3 atom r∗ , then r∗ must be ov-adjacent to
some point in {r, r′ } or {r, r′ , r′′ } in M1 (this r∗ cannot belong to an ov-segment within {r, r′ } or {r, r′ , r′′ },
due to the construction in Definition 11.10 (4)–(5)). We consider different cases:
      • If r∗ is ov-adjacent to r′ : then they both have deg 3 in M1 , contradicting the ov-proper assumption.
      • If r∗ is ov-adjacent to r′′ : then both r∗ and r′ have deg 3 in M1 and they are both ov-adjacent to r′′ ,
        again contradicting the ov-proper assumption.
      • If r∗ is ov-adjacent to r, and we are cutting {r, r′ }: then both r∗ and r′ have deg 3 in M1 and they
        are both ov-adjacent to r, again contradicting the ov-proper assumption.
      • If r∗ is ov-adjacent to r, and we are cutting {r, r′ , r′′ }: then after turning one free end into fixed end
        at r, the atoms r and r∗ are both deg 3 and ov-adjacent, but we still choose to cut {r, r′ , r′′ } instead
        of {r, r∗ }, contradicting the construction in Definition 11.10 (4).
This then completes the proof of (♣) and hence the claim (3).
   With claims (1)–(3) proved, we can now prove (11.8). The bound for #{4} follows directly from (2), as
no deg 4 atom can be cut in Definition 11.10 (4)–(5), or Definition 11.10 (8) (as shown in (3)). To prove the
bound for #{33} (which is #{33A} + #{33B} ), note that with at most µ := #fucp(MU ) + #ovcp(X2 ) exceptions,
each {3}-component in MD created in Definition 11.10 (4)–(5) is always paired with a {33}-component, so
the number of atoms in MD cut in these steps is at most 3 · #{33} + µ. Now consider the #UD many good
UD connections and the corresponding atoms r ∈ MD . If any such r is not cut Definition 11.10 (4)–(5), then
in the molecule MD after Definition 11.10 (7), this r must be a deg 3 atom that belongs to a component of
136                                  YU DENG, ZAHER HANI, AND XIAO MA


MD that contains at least two deg 3 atoms. This means that, for the MD after Definition 11.10 (7), we have
              (number of deg 3 atoms) − (number of components) ≥ (#UD − 3#{33} − µ)/2.                        (11.9)
Then, consider the increment of the left hand side of (11.9) in each cutting in Definition 11.10 (8). Using
Definition 8.6, and noting that each such cut can only create one fixed end at one deg 4 atom in each resulting
component, we can verify that this is invariant after cutting one deg 3 atom, and decreases by 1 after each
cutting in Definition 11.10 (4)–(5). Since this value should be come 0 after all atoms of MD have been cut,
we conclude that #{33} ≥ (#UD − 3#{33} − µ)/2, and thus #{33} ≥ 15 · (#UD − µ).                              □

   Finally, we put everything together and present the full algorithm that leads to the proof of Proposition
10.17. As discussed above, this proof features a dichotomy: let the number of ov-components of the deg
3 subgraph of MU (or MD ) after cutting MD (or MU ) as free be λ, then we shall apply 3COMPUP (or
3COMPDN) if λ ≳ ρ, and apply MAINUD if λ ≪ ρ.

Proof of Proposition 10.17. Now we can finally finish the proof of Proposition 10.17. Below we may assume
Remark 11.4; if not, then Proposition 10.17 already follows from Proposition 11.3.
   Start from a molecule MU D as in Proposition 10.17, which satisfies the assumptions in Proposition 10.15
                                                 2
(3)(i)–(3)(iii) and Remark 11.4. Define the set M2conn as in Proposition 11.3. Define good UD connections as
in Proposition 11.11 and define the number of them as #UD . Note that initially #UD ≥ ρ′′′ /4 by Proposition
10.15 (3)(iii). We perform the following steps to MU D :
                                     2
   Step 1. We cut each atom in M2conn as free. If any atom in MU D becomes deg 2, we also cut it as
free, until MU D contains no deg 2 atoms. Note that the contribution of these operations to #{4} is at most
      2
|M2conn | ≤ (C2∗ )−1 · ρ′′′ by Remark 11.4.
                                  2
    Note that by definition of M2conn , cutting any atom in it as free does not create any C-top fixed atom in
MU or any C-bottom fixed atom in MD , and neither does any subsequent operations cutting deg 2 atoms.
Moreover, consider any UD connection e and the maximal ov-segment containing e; cutting each atom in
   2
M2conn breaks at most two such ov-segments (where breaking means cutting an atom in this ov-segment as
free), and any subsequent cutting of deg 2 atoms cannot break any such ov-segment.
    If the ov-segment containing e is not broken, then e will remain a good UD connection if it is initially
so. In fact, consider the component X of MD containing the MD endpoint of e; if X is not affected in the
cutting (i.e. no atom in X is cut and no fixed end is created in X) then e will remain good as it is; if X is
affected then the new component containing the MD endpoint of e must contain a deg 3 atom.
    Therefore, after Step 1, we still have
                                                             2
                                      #UD ≥ ρ′′′ /4 − 2|M2conn | ≥ ρ′′′ /5.                                  (11.10)
                                                                         2
Moreover, after Step 1, MU D is still a canonical UD molecule, and now M2conn = ∅.                The number of full
components of MU and MD satisfy that #fucp (MU ) + #fucp (MD ) ≤ 4(C3∗ )−1 · ρ′′′ .
   Step 2. Now consider all the components of MD that contain at least two deg 3 atoms, and all the deg
3 atoms contained in one such component. Let the set of all such deg 3 atoms be Z 0 , and let Y be the
union of all weakly degenerate atoms and atom pairs in MD (see Proposition 10.16). Let the number of
ov-components of Z 0 in MD be #3ovcp(Z 0 ) as in Propositions 11.7 and 11.9.
   Now, if #3ovcp(Z 0 ) ≥ (10d)4 (C2∗ )−1 · ρ′′′ , then we cut MD as free. For each component of MD containing
at least two deg 3 atoms, we cut it into elementary components using 3COMPDN; for other components
we cut them into elementary components using DOWN. Then cut MU into elementary components using
UP.
   In this case, by Proposition 11.7 we get #{33B} = #{44} = 0, and
                             #{33A} ≥ (100)−1 #3ovcp(Z 0 ) ≥ 100d4 · (C2∗ )−1 · ρ′′′ ,                       (11.11)

                     #{4} ≤ #fucp (MU ) + #fucp (MD ) + (C2∗ )−1 · ρ′′′ ≤ 2 · (C2∗ )−1 · ρ′′′ ,              (11.12)
which already proves (10.21).
           LONG TIME DERIVATION OF THE BOLTZMANN EQUATION FROM HARD SPHERE DYNAMICS                           137


   Step 3. Now suppose #3ovcp(Z 0 ) ≤ (10d)4 (C2∗ )−1 · ρ′′′ . Define Z to be the union of Z0 , together with the
deg 3 atoms in those components of MD that contain only one deg 3 atom and at least one atom in Y . Then
for the set Z we have #3ovcp(Z) ≤ #3ovcp(Z 0 ) + |Y |. Note also |Y | ≤ 10(C2∗ )−1 · ρ′′′ by Remark 11.4.
   We then define S := SELECT(M    e D , Z, Y ) as in Definition 11.8, where M
                                                                             e D contains only those components
of MD that contain an atom in Z ∪ Y (but this does not affect Definition 11.8). We then cut S as free and
subsequently cut it into elementary components using DOWN. If any atom in MU D becomes deg 2, we also
cut it as free, until MU D contains no deg 2 atoms.
                                                                             2
   By Proposition 11.9 (1), we know that MU D is still a UD molecule with M2conn = ∅, and MD becomes
ov-proper after cutting S (those components of MD that do not contain any atom in Y and contain at most
one deg 3 atom are automatically ov-proper). Now MD may contain C-bottom ends, but MU still does
not contain C-top fixed end. Moreover, by Proposition 11.9 (2), we know that the number of ov-segments
corresponding to UD connections e, that are broken in the process of cutting S as free (again subsequent
operations cutting deg 2 atoms will not break any such ov-segments), is at most
                                  10 · (3 · #3ovcp(Z) + |Y |) ≤ C · (C2∗ )−1 · ρ′′′ ,                    (11.13)
therefore we still have
                                   #UD ≥ ρ′′′ /5 − C · (C2∗ )−1 · ρ′′′ ≥ ρ′′′ /6,                        (11.14)
after Step 3. The contribution to #{4} of these cutting operations is also bounded by (11.13). Note that
any component of MD that is not connected to MU after cutting S cannot be full.
    Step 4. Now the molecule MU D satisfies the assumptions (i)–(ii) in Definition 11.10, and has #UD ≥ ρ′′′ /6.
Moreover, since Y has been cut, we know that MD is ov-proper and does not contain any weakly degenerate
atom or atom pair. Define the sets Xj (0 ≤ j ≤ 3) as in Definition 11.10, and consider the value of #ovcp(X2 )
as in Proposition 11.11.
    Now, if #ovcp(X2 ) ≥ (1/106 d2 ) · ρ′′′ , then we cut MD as free and cut it into elementary components
following Definition 11.10 (8) (except that if a component of MD is full, then we first cut any deg 4 atom as
free). This produces {3}-, {4}-, {33A}- and {33B}-components (but no {2}-component), with the number of
{4}-components bounded by #fucp(MD ) ; moreover all {33B}-components are good due to absence of weakly
degenerate atoms and atom pairs.
    Next we work with MU ; if any atom in MU becomes deg 2 then we cut it as free, and repeat until MU
contains no deg 2 atoms. By definitions of Xj in Definition 11.10, it is clear that all the C-atoms in X0 have
been cut, and all the C-atoms in X1 now have deg 3, and all the other C-atoms now have deg 4. Moreover,
an atom n ∈ X1 belongs to X2 , if and only if it belongs to a component of MU that contains at least two
deg 3 atoms, after the above cutting operations.
    Now consider all the components of MU after the above cutting operations, that contain an C-atom in X2
(i.e. ignore the other components, which can always be cut into elementary components using UP, in view of
absence of C-top fixed ends in MU ); denote the union of these components by e    MU . In MU D before cutting,
                                                                 6 2     ′′′
the number of ov-components of X2 is µ := #ovcp(X2 ) ≥ (1/10 d ) · ρ . Now after the cutting operations,
the set of deg 3 atoms in Me U equals X2 ∪ XO , where XO is the set of deg 3 atoms in M   e U that are O-atoms.
Now note that, the number of ov-components of X2 ∪ XO is no less than the number of ov-components of
X2 (which is obviously no less than µ). This is because for each deg 3 O-atom, it must have two edges (free
ends or bonds) that are serial, and one free end that is serial with one fixed end. Therefore, if two atoms in
X2 are ov-connected within X2 ∪ XO , they must also be ov-connected within X2 .
    Then, we can apply 3COMPUP to these components of MU , and use Proposition 11.7 to get
                                       #{33A} ≥ (100)−1 · µ ≥ C −1 · ρ′′′ ,                              (11.15)
                      #{4} ≤ #fucp (MU ) + #fucp (MD ) + C(C2∗ )−1 · ρ′ ≤ C(C2∗ )−1 · ρ′′′ ,             (11.16)
which already proves (10.21).
  Step 5. Now suppose #ovcp(X2 ) ≤ (1/106 d2 ) · ρ′′′ , then we apply MAINUD to cut MU D into elementary

atoms in MD , and MD does not contain weakly degenerate atoms or atom pairs), we get that

which already proves (10.21).
   This completes the proof of Proposition 10.17 in the last scenario, and finishes the proof of Proposition
5.3.                                                                                                       □

                                               12. Proof of Proposition 5.2
   In this section we prove Proposition 5.2. By induction, we may assume that (5.3)–(5.4) hold for ℓ − 1,
and we need to prove them for ℓ, using the expansion (4.37) of f A (ℓτ ) in terms of f A ((ℓ − 1)τ ). This will be
done in the following steps: first, we reformulate the expansion (4.37) of f A (ℓτ ) as a sum over CH molecules.
Second, we show that the leading asymptotics of this sum is given by CH molecules that are trees (i.e. having
no cycles), for which we have an explicit expression for the χT factor appearing in (4.37). Next, we exhibit
a cancellation that further reduces to those CH molecules that are (rooted) binary trees. FInally, we match
these binary tree terms with the iterates of the solution of the Boltzmann equation at time ℓτ with data
given by n((ℓ − 1)τ ).

  We shall delay the proof of Proposition 12.1 until Section 12.4 below, and first present the proof of
Proposition 5.2 assuming Proposition 12.1 holds.

12.2. Cancellation in the f B expansion. To study the main term f1B defined in (12.14), we will first
define an equivalence relation ∼ on leading pairs (T, D), such that we have IN op (M1 ; z) = IN op (M2 ; z)
if (T1 , D1 ) ∼ (T2 , D2 ) and Mi is constructed from Di as in Proposition 7.7. This equivalence relation is
unrelated to the equivalence relations ∼ in Definition 4.26 or ≈ in Section 4.4.3, which will not appear here.
   The use of this equivalence relation will allow us to treat the denominators (|T | − 1)! and mT ! in (12.14),
and rewrite the sum over (T, D) as a sum over the CH molecules M. After this, we will exhibit a cancellation
in this sum which will further reduce it into a sum over all CH molecules M that have an extended binary
tree structure (cf. Definition 8.1).

12.2.1. An equivalence relation. Recall that T ∈ Tℓ,Λ with notations and particle labelings as in Section 4.3;
here, for simplicity, we shall now omit the “layer digit” ℓ as we only have one layer. Then j becomes an
integer j and T = [|T |] with the root jrt labeled by 1, and particles in [mj ]+ are labeled by {j} × [mj ] for
j ∈ T except for the unique root particle. In the rest of this section we will denote particles by p (instead of
vector p as in Section 4.3), and the root particle prt is labeled by (1, 0), so it is the smallest of all particles
under lexicographic ordering. This is different from the convention in Section 4.3 where root particles are
larger than non-root particles, but is more convenient in the current setting.
   For any leading (Ti , Di ) where 1 ≤ i ≤ 2, we define (T1 , D1 ) ∼ (T2 , D2 ) if the following holds:
    (1) We have T1 = T2 = T , and there exists a bijection q : T → T such that q(jrt ) = jrt and mj = mq(j) ,
        and {j, j ′ } ∈ E1 if and only if {q(j), q(j ′ )} ∈ E2 .
    (2) For each j ∈ T , let Pj = {j} × [mj ] be the set of non-root particles in cluster j. Then there exists a
        bijection φ : p(T) → p(T) such that φ(prt ) = prt and φ(Pj ) = Pq(j) .
    (3) Let φ be defined as in (2). Then for any two particles p and p′ , there exists a collision between them
        in some E(Gj ) in D1 , or an overlap oj,j ′ between them in D1 , if and only if there exists a collision
        between φ(p) and φ(p′ ) in some E(Gq(j) ) in D2 , or an overlap oq(j),q(j ′ ) between φ(p) and φ(p′ ) in
        D2 .
    (4) The above φ induces a bijection between collisions and overlaps in D1 and D2 , as in (3). The this
        induced bijection preserves the ordering, where the ordering in Di is given by ≺i which is part of the
        definition of Di (cf. Definition 6.1).
   The above definition can be interpreted as follows: two pairs (T1 , D1 ) and (T2 , D2 ) are equivalent if and
only if one can be obtained from the other by relabeling the |T | − 1 non-root clusters and relabeling the mj
non-root particles in each (root or non-root) cluster.
   It can be shown (see the discussions in Section 12.2.2) that each equivalence class has exactly (|T |−1)!mT !
elements. Moreover, if Mi is the CH molecules constructed from Di with (T1 , D1 ) ∼ (T2 , D2 ), then we have
INop (M1 ; z) = INop (M2 ; z), since since M2 can be obtained from M2 by relabeling the atoms and bonds
that preserves the integral in (12.8). In view of the denominators (|T | − 1)! and mT ! in (12.14), we conclude
that                                         X
                               f1B (x, v) =      (−1)|E| · INop (Mfix ; x − τ v, v),                     (12.17)
                                           (T,D)fix

where the sum is taken over all pairs (T, D)fix , which takes one element from each equivalence class of leading
pairs, and Mfix is the CH molecule constructed from Dfix . The exact choice of (T, D)fix will be discussed in
Section 12.2.2 below.

12.2.2. The choice of (T, D)fix . We now define the representative (T, D)fix of each equivalence class. Given
any (T, D), each pair in the equivalence class [(T, D)] of (T, D) under ∼ is uniquely determined by the
mappings (q, φ) defined in Section 12.2.1, which is equivalent to a permutation (equivalently a liner ordering)
of all particles, such that the particles in each cluster are grouped together, the particles in the root cluster
come before other clusters, and the root particle comes before other particles.
142                                       YU DENG, ZAHER HANI, AND XIAO MA


   Now, for each (T, D) we shall define an intrinsic linear ordering ≺int , similar to the proof of Lemma 6.13.
Note that the linear ordering ≺int is equivalent to a linear ordering ≺cl of all clusters such that the root
                                             (j)
cluster comes first, and a linear ordering ≺par of particles in each cluster [mj ]+ such that the root particle
comes first. We construct this intrinsic linear ordering inductively, as follows:
      (1) Define jrt ≺cl j for any jrt ̸= j ∈ T , where jrt is the root cluster of T0 .
      (2) If dist(jrt , j) < dist(jrt , j ′ ), then define j ≺cl j ′ , where dist(jrt , j) denotes the length of the unique
          shortest path from the root jrt to j in T .
      (3) If dist(jrt , j) = dist(jrt , j ′ ), then let j1 and j1′ be the parents of j and j ′ in T (i.e. the previous term
          in the shortest path from the root jrt to j or j ′ ), and define j ≺cl j ′ if j1 ≺cl j1′ .
      (4) If dist(jrt , j) = dist(jrt , j ′ ) and they have a common parent j1 , then consider the overlaps oj,j1 and
                                                                                                                  (j1 ) ′
          oj ′ ,j1 . Assume oj,j1 |= p and oj ′ ,j1 |= p′ for some p, p′ ∈ [mj1 ]+ , then define j ≺cl j ′ if p ≺par   p , or
                     ′
          if p = p and oj,j1 ≺ oj ′ ,j1 with the partial ordering ≺ defined as part of D.
      (5) For j = jrt , choose the root of the tree Gjrt to be prt . For j > jrt , choose the root of the tree Gj to
          be the unique particle pj ∈ [mj ]+ such that oj,j1 |= pj where j1 is the parent of j in T as defined in
          (3). This makes Gj a rooted tree with root pj .
                                               (j)                                                            (j)
      (6) Define the linear ordering ≺par in each [mj ]+ similar to (1)–(3). Namely, we define p ≺par p′ if p = pj
                                                                         (j)
          is the root, or if dist(p, pj ) < dist(p′ , pj ), or if p1 ≺par p′1 where p1 and p′1 are the parents of p and
            ′                                            ′            ′
          p in Gj as defined in (3), or if p1 = p1 and c ≺ c . In the last case c ∈ E(Gj ) is the collision between
          p and p1 (same for c′ ), and ≺ is again the partial ordering defined as part of D.
   It is clear that this linear ordering ≺int is well defined and uniquely determined by (T, D), and is preserved
under the mappings (q, φ) defined in Section 12.2.1. In particular, if (T1 , D1 ) = (T2 , D2 ) in Section 12.2.1,
then the mappings (q, φ) must be identity, thus each equivalence class indeed has (|T | − 1)!mT ! elements.
Moreover, each equivalence class contains a unique pair (T, D), such that the intrinsic ordering ≺int coincides
with the lexicographic ordering of particles. We define this to be (T, D)fix .
   For any (T, D)fix , let Mfix be the CH molecule constructed from Dfix as in Proposition 7.7. Next we will
show that each Mfix in fact comes from a unique (T, D)fix , so we can write the sum in (12.17) as a sum over
all such molecules.

Proposition 12.2. For n < Λ, define Mn to be the set of full molecules that are trees containing n atoms,
with one particular particle line prt fixed. Fix any (M, prt ) ∈ Mn . Then there exists a unique pair (T, D)fix ,
such that the CH molecule Mfix constructed from Dfix as in Proposition 7.7 equals M, and the root particle
corresponds to prt .

Proof. Given any M and prt , we can recover the (T, D) as follows. The elements of p(T) are particle lines
of M. If two particle lines intersect at a C-atom in M, then draw an edge between the two corresponding
elements of p(T); the components of the resulting graph (with node set p(T)) are just the clusters [mj ]+ ,
and these subgraphs (which are trees themselves) are just the graphs Gj , with prt being the root particle and
the component containing it being the root cluster. Then the overlaps oj,j ′ are determined by particle lines
in M that intersect at an O-atom, and the partial ordering ≺ is determined by the parent-child relation of
atoms in M (cf. Definition 7.2). By suitably relabeling, this provides some leading pair (T, D) such that M
is constructed from D as in Proposition 7.7, and the root particle corresponds to prt .
   Consider now the intrinsic ordering of particles defined in Section 12.2.2 for (T, D), which can be inter-
preted as a linear ordering of all particle lines in M. In fact this linear ordering depends only on M, as
the collisions, overlaps and their partial ordering in D can all be determined using only the structure of M.
As such, there exists a unique (T, D)fix that produces M via the construction in Proposition 7.7, and has
lexicographic ordering coincide with the intrinsic ordering which is determined by M. This completes the
proof.                                                                                                       □

Remark 12.3. The labeling of particle lines in (T, D)fix in Proposition 12.2 can be described as follows. Start
by labeling the particle line prt as (1, 0). At any time, we choose a new particle line p′ that intersects an
           LONG TIME DERIVATION OF THE BOLTZMANN EQUATION FROM HARD SPHERE DYNAMICS                          143


already labeled particle line p at a C- or O-atom, and label p′ by the smallest label available, under the rules
that (i) we prioritize intersections that are C-atoms over those that are O-atoms; (ii) then we prioritize those
p that are colored before other possible p; (iii) then we prioritize those intersections that are lowest along
the same particle line p in M.
Remark 12.4. For each fixed n we have |Mn | ≤ C n . In fact, graphically M is a tree with n atoms and each
atom having degree at most 4; upon fixing one root, this tree can be constructed as an at most ternary tree
(so each non-root node has one parent and at most 3 children), which is well known to have ≤ C n choices.
Setting each atom as C- or O-atom then leads to another factor of 2n , hence the desired upper bound.
  Now, by applying Proposition 12.2, we can rewrite (12.17) as
                                                  X X
                 f1B (x, v) = fℓ−1
                               A
                                   (x − τ v, v) +         (−1)#O · INop (M; x − τ v, v),                (12.18)
                                                 1≤n<Λ M∈Mn

where #O is the number of O-atoms in M, and INop is defined as in (12.8) (where we may also replace the
|T|p\r by n). Note by Remark 12.4 that the number of terms in this summation is ≤ C n for each n.
12.2.3. Cancellation in f1B . Now, starting with (12.18), we shall exhibit a cancellation for certain molecules
M ∈ Mn , which allows us to reduce (12.18) to a sum over a subset M∗n of Mn that contains molecules with
an extended binary tree structure. We start by defining M∗n ⊆ Mn as follows: for (M, prt ) ∈ Mn , let n∗ be
the highest atom on the particle line prt . Note that n∗ has at least one top free end. Now define M ∈ M∗n if
and only if n∗ is the only node with two top free ends.
   Note that since each M ∈ Mn is a full molecule, it must have at least one atom with two top free ends,
namely an atom that is maximal with respect to the partial ordering of atoms of M defined in Definition
7.2. This allows to define the following notion on Mn \M∗n .
Definition 12.5. Let M ∈ Mn \M∗n with the particle line prt fixed. Define the intrinsic linear ordering
of particle lines ≺int as in Section 12.2.2 (see Proposition 12.2) which depends only on M. Then, define
n0 = n0 (M, prt ) to be the unique atom in M that has two top free ends, and doe not equal n∗ , and belongs to
a minimal particle line p under ≺int . Note that p ̸= prt as n0 ̸= n∗ . We then define Mtwi to be the molecule
obtained from M by switching the type of the atom n0 (i.e. from C-atom to O-atom and vice versa), with
the same root particle line prt fixed. See Figure 25.




        Figure 25. The twisting operation in Definition 12.5. Here the two molecules have the
        same root particle line prt (labeled by 1 in the picture) and the same molecule structure
        except at the atom n0 ̸= n∗ . The intrinsic ordering of particle lines are also different in
        the two cases; however the atom n0 is the same in both cases, and the labeling up to the
        minimal particle line containing n0 (the one labeled by 2) is also the same in both cases.
144                                         YU DENG, ZAHER HANI, AND XIAO MA


      The following result then establishes the desired cancellation:

Proposition 12.6. For M ∈ Mn \M∗n we have Mtwi ∈ Mn \M∗n , and n0 (M, prt ) = n0 (Mtwi , prt ). Moreover
(Mtwi )twi = M, and INop (M; z) = INop (Mtwi ; z), and the value of #O changes by 1 from M to Mtwi .

Proof. The existence of n0 in Definition 12.5 is clear. Since going from M to Mtwi we have only changed the
C/O type of one atom n0 , by Remark 12.3 it is also clear that n0 (Mtwi , prt ) = n0 , and hence (Mtwi )twi = M
(as we just switch back the C/O type of n0 ). Also the value of #O either increases or decreases by 1 (so the
sign (−1)#O in (12.18) gets flipped).
   Finally, concerning the integral INop , note that the only difference between the cases of M and Mtwi occurs
at the factor ∆(ze1 , ze2 , ze′1 , ze′2 , tn ) where n = n0 . But since the two top edges (e′1 , e′2 ) at n0 are both free ends,
and n0 ̸= n∗ , so after integrating out the variables (ze′1 , ze′2 , tn0 ) using (7.10) as in the proof of Proposition
7.8 (and noticing that here Q does not depend on ze′1 and ze′2 , as both e′i are free ends), we get exactly the
same expressions for the remaining part of both integrals. This proves INop (M; z) = INop (Mtwi ; z).                         □
      Now, thanks to the cancellation provided by Proposition 12.6, we can rewrite the sum in (12.18) as

           LONG TIME DERIVATION OF THE BOLTZMANN EQUATION FROM HARD SPHERE DYNAMICS                             151


components. By slightly adjusting the definitions in Section 8.2 (for example, the definitions of Sh, Sh∗ and

                 #{33B} = #{4} = #{44} = 0,        and either #good ≥ (R + 1)/2 or X > Λ/2.                (12.40)
    Finally we prove (12.40). If (T, D) is leading, then M is a tree and R = 0, and M  e contains only one cycle.
                                                   ′
It is then easy to see that each component of M cannot be full and cannot contain deg 2 atom or C-bottom
fixed end, and at least one component must contain a cycle or two deg 3 atoms. By applying DOWN to M′ ,
we are guaranteed that #{33B} = #{4} = #{44} = 0, and that there exists at least one {33A}-component
(by Proposition 10.2 (1)). Moreover, the two fixed ends of this {33A}-component cannot both belong to the
ov-segment containing eop (by Remark 8.3), so one of them must come from cutting as free another deg 3
atom. This then leads to at least one good component, by repeating the proof of Proposition 10.2 (2).
    Now assume (T, D) is sub-leading, then R > 0 or X > Λ/2, and M       e = M. In this case we argue similar
to Choice 2 in the proof of Proposition 10.5. Note that M is formed by a collection of sets Mj (j ∈ T )
of C-atoms (corresponding to collisions in each E(Gj )) that are connected by O-atoms (corresponding to
overlaps oj,j ′ for {j, j ′ } ∈ E). Assume the root cluster corresponds to M1 (note that it contains the root
particle prt whose particle line contains eop ). Then after turning eop into fixed end and turning M into M′ ,
we get a C-atom of deg 3 in M1 (unless in the trivial case when M1 contains no C-atoms, which is treated
similarly). This is the only deg 3 atom in the component of M′ containing M1 , and each other component of
M′ also contains exactly one deg 3 atom oj on an ov-segment within some Mj (for convenience we call such
Mj cute).
    We then follow the strategy in Choice 2 in the proof of Proposition 10.5. Namely we cut M1 as free and
cut it into elementary components using DOWN; for any cute Mj , we also cut Mj ∪ {oj } as free and cut it
into elementary components using DOWN. This then creates more cute Mj and then we repeat this process
until all atoms have been cut. Note that overlaps oj,j ′ only occur for {j, j ′ } ∈ E and (T, E) is also a tree, it
is then easy to see that each Mj (or Mj ∪ {oj }) contains exactly one deg 3 atom when it is cut. As such, by
Proposition 10.2, we get a good component for each Mj that comes from a recollisional cluster (because in
this case Mj or Mj ∪ {oj } will contain a cycle after it has been cut as free). By definition of R, this leads
to at least R good components. This proves (12.40) and finishes the proof of Proposition 5.2.

                                       13. Proof of Proposition 5.4
   In this section we prove Proposition 5.4, which provides the needed estimate for the truncation error fserr .
The starting point is (4.22) which bounds ∥fserr ∥L1 in terms of feν ((ℓ − 1)τ ). We then straightforwardly adapt
the arguments in Sections 4 and 6 to expand feν ((ℓ − 1)τ ) (and hence ∥fserr ∥L1 ) first in terms of layered
cluster forests Fe , and then in terms of layered interaction diagrams D. e Here Fe (and similarly D) e involving
layers [1, ℓ] can be viewed as the concatenation of a layered cluster forest F involving layers [0, ℓ − 1] with a
cluster forest Fℓ with a single layer ℓ, which essentially contains one cluster with either ≥ Γ recollisions or
≥ Λℓ particles. By considering the the first time t ∈ [(ℓ − 1)τ, ℓτ ] at which this cluster forms (cf. Lemma
4.8), we may also restrict that the recollision number if ≤ 2Γ and the particle number is ≤ 2Λℓ .
   With the above arguments, we can reduce 5.4 to an estimate for IN ( e          M, H, H ′ ) for the CH molecule
Me corresponding to Fe (and D)  e above. This is then bounded by a cutting algorithm, which combines the
algorithm defined in Sections 10–11 for the part M   e ≤ℓ−1 containing layers ≤ ℓ − 1, with a special algorithm
treating the layer ℓ part e Mℓ ⊂ M  e which is introduced in Proposition 13.1 below. Here the case when Fℓ
has more than Λℓ particles is easy, and the main challenge comes from the case when Fℓ has more than Γ
recollisions, which is discussed in Section 13.3.
13.1. Reduction to molecules. We start from the estimate (4.22), which is satisfied by the expression
                                                                                                      ∗
(3.23). On the right hand side of (4.22), we may fix the values of (ℓ, r, m, γ) at a loss of | log ε|C , and then
                                   ∗
fix λ0 ⊆ [s] at a loss of | log ε|C r .
152                                    YU DENG, ZAHER HANI, AND XIAO MA


   Now λ0 is fixed. We next expand the term feν ((ℓ − 1)τ, z[s+m] ) using Proposition 4.21 (after relabeling the
particles and renaming the variables accordingly). The contribution of the Errℓ−1 term in (4.48) is negligible,
because using (5.6) and iterating the Err2ℓ estimates in Proposition 4.21 yields that ∥Errℓ−1 ∥L1 ≤ τ Λℓ−1 /12 ,

13.3. The cutting algorithm. In this subsection we prove Proposition 13.1. For simplicity we will write
M instead of M e ℓ throughout the proof. The strategy is a modification of the algorithm for the two-layer
molecule MU D in Section 11. To establish the layers within M, we introduce the notion of transversal sets A
in Definition 13.3, which gives a splitting of M into three disjoint parts A+ , A, and A− . Roughly speaking,
the layers MU and MD will be replaced by the pair of sets (A+ , A) (or (A, A− )) for appropriate choices
of A, as we adapt the algorithm in Section 11 to the current setting. The lower bound for the number of
{33}-components comes from a lower bound for number of connections between A+ (or more precisely a
aubset X + (A) ⊂ A+ ) and A (Proposition 13.2), which follows from (the inverse of) the upper bound on the
number of collisions given the number of particles proved in Burago-Ferleger-Kononenko [15].

Proposition 13.2. Suppose M is a full molecule of C-atoms and A ⊆ M is an atom set, such that for any
particle line p and any atoms n1 ≺ n2 ≺ n3 on the particle line p (in the ordering in Definition 7.2) with
           LONG TIME DERIVATION OF THE BOLTZMANN EQUATION FROM HARD SPHERE DYNAMICS                           155


n1 , n3 ∈ A, we must have n2 ∈ A. Let q be the total number of bottom edges at atoms of A that are not
bonds between two atoms of A (i.e. these edges can be bottom ends of M or bonds connecting an atom in A
to an atom not in A), then we must have |A| ≤ G(q), where G : N+ → N+ is a fixed function not depending
on A or M.

Proof. Recall that we have assumed that all the collisions indicated by the molecule M (which corresponds
to some interaction diagram D = Dℓ ) can actually occur for some initial configuration. In this configuration,
we may order all the collisions (represented by atoms in M) in time.
   Consider particle lines p (corresponding to a unique particle in the E-dynamics) that contain at least one
atom in A; define the set of these particle lines to be B. Each such particle line must contain a bottom edge
at an atom of A that is not a bond between two atoms of A, which implies that |B| ≤ q.
   For each particle p ∈ B, consider the collision n−  p immediately before the first collision of the particle p
in A, and the collision n+ p immediately after the last collision of the particle p in A. Note that one or both
of n±p may be absent, but the total number of such collisions is at most 2q. These collisions are ordered in
time as stated above, and we may consider any of the at most 2q + 1 time intervals between two adjacent
collisions n±
            p . For any such interval I, define the set

   B ′ = p ∈ B : n−
         
                    p is either absent, or is on the left of I, or is the left endpoint of I,

                          n+
                           p is either absent, or is on the right of I, or is the right endpoint of I .   (13.11)

We also shrink I a little bit to Ie such that I\Ie contains no collision (other than the ones at the endpoint
of I, which do not belong to A). It is then easy to see that (i) any collision in A that happens within time
interval Ie must be between two particles in B ′ , and (ii) any collision that happens within time interval Ie
and involves an atom in B ′ must belong to A. Let λI be the number of collisions in A that happen within
               e Then, for some configuration, there exists some E-dynamics of the |B ′ | particles in B ′ that
time interval I.
has λI collisions. By Proposition 3.9 (4) we know that λI ≤ G0 (q) for some fixed function G0 : N+ → N
independent of A and M (in fact, using the results of [15] and the proof in Appendix A, we can choose
                         2
G0 (q) = 2q · (32q 3/2 )q ). Summing in I we then get |A| ≤ (2q + 1)G0 (q) := G(q), as desired.

  Next, we define the notion of transversal subsets A of M, and prove some properties.

Definition 13.3. Let M be a full molecule of C-atoms, and A ⊆ M is an atom set. We say A is transversal,
if we can decompose M\A into two disjoint subsets A+ and A− , such that no atom in A− is parent of any
atom in A ∪ A+ , and no atom in A is parent of any atom in A+ .
   For any transversal set A, define the set X0 (A) such that, an atom n ∈ X0 (A) if and only if n ̸∈ A and
n has two bonds connected to two atoms in A. Then define the set X(A) inductively as follows: an atom
n ∈ X(A) if and only if n ̸∈ A and n has two bonds connected to two atoms in X(A) ∪ A.

Proposition 13.4. Let A be a connected transversal subset and M is a connected molecule.
    (1) We can always choose (A+ , A− ) in Definition 13.3 such that each component of A+ has at least one
        bond connected to A.
    (2) Let X0± (A) = X0 (A) ∩ A± and X ± (A) = X(A) ∩ A± , then n ∈ X0± (A) if and only if n ̸∈ A and n
        has two children (or two parents) in A. Similarly, the sets X ± (A) can be defined in the same way
        as X(A), but with the sentence “n has two bonds connected to two atoms in X(A) ∪ A” replaced by
        “n has two children (or two parents) in X ± (A) ∪ A”.
    (3) The set X(A) ∪ A is also connected and transversal. Moreover any atom in M\(X(A) ∪ A) has at
        most one bond with atoms in X(A) ∪ A, so X0 (X(A) ∪ A) = ∅.
    (4) Recall ρ(A) defined in Definition 8.1. Then there exists a connected transversal set B ⊇ A, such
        that ρ(B) = ρ(A), and either B = M or X0 (B) ̸= ∅.

Proof. (1) Suppose A+ has a component U that is not connected to A by a bond, then we replace A+ by
A+ \U and A− by A− ∪ U , which still satisfies the requirements for (A+ , A− ) in Definition 13.3, but the
156                                   YU DENG, ZAHER HANI, AND XIAO MA


number of components of A+ decreases by 1. Repeat until component of A+ is connected to A by a bond
(or A+ becomes empty).
   (2) If an atom n ∈ X0 (A) belongs to A± , then n must have two children (or two parents) in A. Now
suppose n ∈ X(A) has two adjacent atoms n1 and n2 in X0 (A) ∪ A. If n1 belongs to A± , then n must be a
parent (or child) of n1 , and the same holds for n2 . This means that either both n1 , n2 ∈ A+ ∪ A and both
are children of n (so n ∈ A+ ), or both n1 , n2 ∈ A− ∪ A and both are parents of n. Repeating this discussion,
we get the desired splitting X(A) = X + (A) ∪ X − (A).
   (3) Connectedness is obvious by definition. To prove transversality, we simply decompose M\(A∪X(A)) =
B + ∪ B − , where B = A+ \X + (A) and B − = A− \X − (A). This then satisfies the requirements (using the
fact that any child (or parent) of any atom n ∈ X ± (A) must belong to X ± (A) ∪ A). The second statement
follows from the definition of X(A) in Definition 13.3.
   (4) Suppose A is connected and transversal; we may assume A ̸= M and X0 (A) = ∅ (otherwise choose
B = A). Since M is connected, there must exist an atom n ∈ M\A that is either a parent or child of an atom
in A. We may assume it is a parent, and then choose a lowest atom n ∈ M\A among these parents. Since
X0 (A) = ∅, we know that n has only one bond with atoms in A; let A1 = A ∪ {n}, then A1 is connected
and ρ(A1 ) = ρ(A).
   Now we claim that A1 is transversal. To see this, define C to be the set of atoms m ∈ A+ \{n} that are
                                                       −                                   −
descendants of n, and decompose M\A1 = A+                         +      +                        −
                                                1 ∪ A1 , where A1 = A \(C ∪ {n}) and A1 = A ∪ C. Clearly
no atom in A1 can be parent of any atom in A1 , and no atom in A can be parent of any atom in A1 ∪ A+
                                                  +                     −
                                                                                                               1.
If an atom m ∈ C is parent of some atom p ∈ A+     1 , then p ∈ A +
                                                                    \C, but m ∈ C and p is child of m, so we also
have p ∈ C, contradiction. Finally if m ∈ C is parent of p ∈ A1 = A ∪ {n}, clearly p ∈ A, but then m is also
parent of an atom in A, contradicting the lowest assumption of n.
   Now we know that A1 is also connected transversal; replacing A by A1 and repeating the above discussion,
we eventually will reach some B such that either B = M or X0 (B) ̸= ∅, as desired.                             □
Definition 13.5 (Algorithm TRANSUP). Suppose M is connected full molecule and A is a connected
transversal subset. We may choose (A+ , A− ) as in Proposition 13.4 (1). Define the following algorithm:
      (1) If A contains any deg 2 atom n, then cut it as free, and repeat until there is no deg 2 atom left.
      (2) Choose a lowest atom n in the set of all deg 3 atoms in A (or a lowest atom in A, if A only contains
          deg 4 atoms). Let Sn be the set of descendants of n in A.
      (3) Starting from n, choose a highest atom m in Sn that has not been cut. If m has deg 3, and is adjacent
          to an atom p ∈ X0+ (A) that also has deg 3, then cut {m, p} as free; otherwise cut m as free. Repeat
          until all atoms in Sn have been cut. Then go to (1).
      (4) Repeat (1)–(3) until all atoms in A have been cut. Then cut the remaining part of A+ as free and cut
          it into elementary components using UP, then cut A− into elementary components using DOWN.
We also define the dual algorithm TRANSDN by reversing the notions of parent/child etc. Note that we
replace A+ and X0+ (A) by A− and X0− (A), and also replace A+ by A− in Proposition 13.4 (1).
Proposition 13.6. For the algorithm TRANSUP (and same for TRANSDN), we have #{33B} = #{44} =
0 and #{4} = 1, and #{33A} ≥ |X0+ (A)|/2 − ρ(A) − 1.
Proof. It is clear, in the same way as in the proof of Proposition 10.2, that during the process, there is
no top fixed end in (A\Sn ) ∪ {n} or in A+ , and there is no bottom fixed end in Sn \{n} or in A− , so
#{33B} = #{44} = 0. Moreover #{4} = 1 because no component of the remaining part of A+ after Definition
13.5 (3) can be full when it is first cut in Definition 13.5 (4) (thanks to Proposition 13.4 (1)), and no
component of A− can be full when it is first cut in Definition 13.5 (4) (thanks to M being connected).
   To prove the lower bound for #{33A} , note that only one atom in A belongs to a {4}-component (as A
is connected). Let the number of atoms in A that belongs to a {2}-component be q, then by using the
invariance of |E∗ | − 3|M| during cutting operations on A (where E∗ is the set of all free ends and bonds at
atoms in A, including in all the elementary components), we deduce that q ≤ ρ(A). To see this, just note
that initially |E∗ | − 3|M| is −ρ(A) + 1 and is finally −q + 1. Then, for each n ∈ X0+ (A), which is connected
          LONG TIME DERIVATION OF THE BOLTZMANN EQUATION FROM HARD SPHERE DYNAMICS                          157


to two atoms n1 , n2 ∈ A by two bonds, assume say n2 is cut after n1 . The total number of such n2 is at least
|X0+ (A)|/2 as each n2 can be obtained from at most two n. For each such n2 , the corresponding n must have
deg 3 when it is cut, so by Definition 13.5 (3), it must belong to either {4}-, or {2}-, or {33A}-component.
Using the upper bound for the number of {4}- and {2}-components, this completes the proof.                  □
Definition 13.7 (The function SELECT2). Let A be a connected molecule with only C-atoms, no bottom
fixed end, and no deg 2 atoms. Let Z be the set of deg 3 atoms in A, and let Y be a subset of atoms
in A such that A becomes a forest after removing the atoms in Y . We define the function SELECT2 =
SELECT2(A, Z, Y ) as follows.
    (1) Consider all the components of Z ∪ Y in A, which is a finite collection of disjoint subsets of A.
    (2) Since A only has C-atoms, the shortest ov-distance between sets (Definition 8.2) coincides with the
        standard definition of distance on a graph. If any two of the subsets in (1), say U and V , have the
        shortest distance (within A) which is at most 4, then we choose one shortest path between an atom
        in U and an atom in V , and let the atoms on this path be nj . Then replace the two sets U and V
        by a single set which is U ∪ V ∪ {nj }.
    (3) Repeat (2) until this can no longer be done. Next, if a single subset U contains two atoms (which
        may be the same) that are connected by a path of length at most 4 with none of the intermediate
        atoms belonging to U or any other subset, then add the atoms on this path to U . If this causes the
        shortest distance between two subsets to be ≤ 4, then proceed to (2) and repeat it as above.
    (4) When no scenario in (2) or (3) occurs, we output S := SELECT2(A, Z, Y ) as the union of all the
        current sets.
Proposition 13.8. The molecule A becomes a proper forest after cutting S as free, and we also have
|S| ≤ 10(|Y | + |Z| + ρ(A)).
Proof. The proof is essentially the same as Proposition 11.9. First A becomes a forest after cutting S as
free, because Y ⊆ S. Also A becomes proper after cutting S as free, due to the same reason in the proof of
Proposition 11.9; in the language of that proof, the absence of the scenario in Definition 13.7 (2) prevents
the case when m and m′ belong to two different components U and V , and the absence of the scenario in
Definition 13.7 (3) prevents the case when m and m′ belong to the same components U .
   As for the upper bound for |S|, simply note that each single step in Definition 13.7 (2)–(3) adds at most
3 atoms to S. Moreover each single step in Definition 13.7 (2) decreases the number of subsets by 1 and
does not decrease ρ(S), while each single step in Definition 13.7 (3) does not change the number of subsets,
but adds one more cycle to S. In particular adding this cycle increases the value of ρ(S) by at least 1, while
we always have ρ(S) ≤ ρ(A) because S ⊆ A, so the number of steps in Definition 13.7 (3) is bounded by a
constant multiple of ρ(A), hence the result.                                                                □
Definition 13.9 (Algorithm MAINTRUP). Suppose M is a connected full molecule and A is a connected
transversal subset. We may choose (A+ , A− ) as in Proposition 13.4 (1). Let the number of bonds connecting
an atom in X + (A) to an atom in A be #+   conn . It is easy to prove that there exists a set Y0 ⊆ A of at most
ρ(A) atoms, such that A becomes a forest after cutting atoms in Y0 as free. Moreover, by decomposing 1
into at most C |A|+ρ(A) indicator functions, we can identify a set of weakly degenerate atoms and atom pairs
in A in the sense of Proposition 10.16; let Y1 ⊆ A be the atoms involved in these weak degeneracies. We
define the following algorithm, which contains two Options that we can choose at the beginning.
   In Option 1 we do the followings:
    (1) Cut A as free, then cut it into elementary components using UP.
    (2) Then cut A+ as free and cut it into elementary components using UP, then cut A− into elementary
        components using DOWN.
  In Option 2 we do the followings:
    (1) Cut all atoms in X0+ (A) as free. If any atom in A becomes deg 2, also cut it as free until A has no
        deg 2 atom.
158                                     YU DENG, ZAHER HANI, AND XIAO MA


       (2) If A remains connected after the above step, let Z be the set of deg 3 atoms in A, and Y be those
           atoms in Y0 ∪ Y1 that have not been cut. Define S = SELECT2(A, Z, Y ) as in Definition 13.7, then
           cut S as free and cut it into elementary components using DOWN. If A is not connected, apply
           this step to each connected component of A.
       (3) If not all atoms in X + (A) have been cut, then choose a lowest atom n in X + (A) that has not been
           cut. If n is adjacent to an atom p ∈ A that has deg 3, then cut {n, p} as free; otherwise cut n as free.
           If A becomes non-proper (Definition 8.2, but without O-atoms) then repeat the steps in Definition
           11.10 (4)–(5) until A becomes proper again.
       (4) Repeat (3) until all atoms in X + (A) have been cut. Then repeat the step in Definition 11.10 (8)
           until all atoms in A have been cut.
       (5) Finally, cut (the remaining parts of) A+ as free and cut it into elementary components using UP,
           and then cut A− into elementary components using DOWN.
                                                                                      −
      We define the dual algorithm MAINTRDN in the same way (so #+
                                                                 conn is replaced by #conn etc.).

and moreover
                                       1                                  +
