Proof. The case of Option 1 is easy; #{33B} = #{44} = 0 is obvious by definition, and #{4} = 1 because
A is connected and A+ satisfies the assumption in Proposition 13.4 (1), in the same way as in the proof
of Proposition 13.6. Moreover, each atom in A belongs to a {4}- or{3}- or {2}- or {33A}-component, and
the total number of {2}- and {33A}-components is equal to ρ(A). For any atom n that belongs to a {3}-
component, if it is a weakly degenerate atom, or if it is part of a weakly degenerate pair and is cut after
the other atom n′ of the pair, then {n} must be a good component by Definition 9.5 (5). This gives at least
|Y1 |/10 − ρ(A) − 1 good components, as desired.
   In the case of Option 2, note that in the whole process, there is no top fixed end in A+ and no bottom
fixed end in A− ; this is because for the lowest atom n in X + (A) chosen in Definition 13.9 (3), any child of n
must either belong to A or belong to X + (A) (and thus will have already been cut). Moreover, any n chosen
in this step either has deg 2 and no bond connecting to A or has deg 3 and exactly one bond connecting
to A (because all atoms in X0+ (A) have been cut in Definition 13.9 (1)). Also any {33}-component {n, p}
cut in this way must be {33A}-component, and no full component can be cut (hence no {4}-component
created) in Definition 13.9 (5). Therefore, all {33B}-component must have both atoms in A, and the only
{4}-components created are those created in Definition 13.9 (1) (contributing at most |X0+ (A)| many {4}-
components) and Definition 13.9 (2) (contributing at most |Y1 | + ρ(A) many {4}-components), hence the
upper bound #{4} ≤ |Y1 | + ρ(A) + |X0+ (A)|.
   Now we prove the lower bound on #{33} . Note that after Definition 13.9 (1), there is no bottom fixed end
in A (and hence none in S), so we can cut S into elementary components using DOWN as in Definition
13.9 (2). By definition of Y0 and Y1 , and by Proposition 13.8, we know that after Definition 13.9 (2) is
finished, A will become a forest which is proper, and contains no weakly degenerate atoms or atom pairs.
Next we prove an upper bound on |S|; let Z0 be the set of atoms in A cut in Definition 13.9 (1), then
|Z| ≤ 4(|Z0 | + |X0+ (A)|) and |S| ≤ 10(4|Z0 | + 4|X0+ (A)| + |Y1 | + 2ρ(A)) by Proposition 13.8. By Definition
13.9 (1), we know that each parent of each atom in Z0 must be in either Z0 or X0+ (A). This then leads to
ρ(Z0 ) ≥ |Z0 | − 2|X0+ (A)|, but also ρ(Z0 ) ≤ ρ(A), so |Z0 | ≤ ρ(A) + 2|X0+ (A)|, thus

                                       |S| ≤ 2 · 102 (ρ(A) + |Y1 | + |X0+ (A)|).

  New we can prove Proposition 13.1.

Proof of Proposition 13.1. First assume M contains at least one pair of strongly degenerate and primitive
atoms (m, n), then we can cut {m, n} as free and cut the rest of M into elementary components using either
UP or DOWN (cf. proof of Proposition 10.5, Choice 1 ). This creates one good {44}-component and no
{4}-component (as M is connected), which meets the requirement of Proposition 13.1 (1). Now assume M
contains no pair of strongly degenerate and primitive atoms, then by Proposition 10.4 we know that each
{33A}-component is either good or can be cut into one {2}- and one good {3}-atom. Therefore, from now
on, we will treat {33A}-components as good.
   Start by choosing a connected transversal subset A1 of M which is a tree (i.e. ρ(A1 ) = 0) and either
A1 = M or X0 (A1 ) ̸= ∅; the existence of such A1 follows in the same way as in Proposition 13.4 (4) starting
from a single atom with no children. If A1 ̸= M, let X1 = X(A1 ), then A1 ∪ X1 is connected transversal
by Proposition 13.4 (3), so we can find A2 ⊇ A1 ∪ X1 starting from A1 ∪ X1 , using Proposition 13.4 (4). If
A2 ̸= M, then let X2 = X(A2 ) and find A3 by Proposition 13.4 (4), then define X3 = X(A3 ) and so on.
                                                         2
   Recall the function G(q) = (2q + 1) · 2q · (32q 3/2 )q as in Proposition 13.2. Define D = K1 := (60d)60d

and Kj+1 = (60d · G((60dKj )60d ))60d for j ≥ 1. We will assume Γ > (60d)60d KD below, and consider several

we can list the elements of Bj := Aj \(Aj−1 ∪ Xj−1 ) as nj1 , · · · , nj|Bj | such that nj1 has only one bond with
Aj−1 ∪ Xj−1 and nji has only one bond with Aj−1 ∪ Xj−1 ∪ {nj1 , · · · , nji−1 }. Similarly we can list the atoms
in B1 := A1 . Choose also an element mj ∈ X0 (Aj ) for each j, then this mj is adjacent to two atoms in Aj ,
with at least one being in Bj . We then perform the following cutting sequence:
    Starting from j = 1, for each j, cut the atoms (nji : i ≤ |Bj |) as free in the increasing order of i. However,
if nji is adjacent to mj and mj has deg 3 when we cut nji , then we cut {nji , mj } instead of nji . After all atoms
in Bj have been cut, the atom mj will also have been cut; we then cut the remaining atoms in Xj as free
(starting from the lowest ones; each will have deg 2 when we cut it), and proceed with Bj+1 = Aj+1 \(Aj ∪Xj )
and so on.
    In this way, it is clear that each mj must belong to a {33}-component, which also has to be {33A}-
component because mj will have two top (or two bottom) free ends when it is cut, assuming mj ∈ X0+ (Aj )
(or mj ∈ X0− (Aj )). This produces at least D many {33A}-components (which are treated as good), while
#{4} = 1, which meets the requirement of Proposition 13.1 (2).
    (2) Suppose Aj = M for some j < D, then ρ(Aj ) ≥ Γ for this j. Therefore we may choose the smallest j
such that ρ(Aj+1 ) ≥ Kj+1 . Let A = Aj , then we have

                                    Appendix A. Some auxiliary results
   In this appendix, we first discuss the proof of basic properties of the original (O-), extended (E-) and
truncated (T- or (Λ, Γ)-) dynamics, namely Propositions 1.2 and 3.9.

Proof of Propositions 1.2 and 3.9. We need to prove the following statements (1)–(6). Note that the flow

      (1) For any of the O-, E- or T-dynamics, the initial configurations where some two collisions happen at
          the same time, or an infinite number of collisions happen in a finite time interval, or some collision
          happens at a fixed time, form a Lebesgue zero set.

          depends only on d and N .
   First, the proof of (1) for the O-dynamics is already done in [1, 27]. The cases of E- and T-dynamics (or
any modified dynamics) follow from the same proof. The fact that HN (t) defined by the O-dynamics are
diffeomorphisms and satisfy the flow property, also follow from time reversibility. The measure preserving
property of HN (t) follows from similar proof as in Lemma 3.13, or see [1, 27]. This proves (2).
   Next, for (3), notice that by Definition 3.5, the motion of the particles starting from any time t depends
only on the configuration of the system at time t but not on the collision history before time t. The flow
property in (3) then follows. Moreover, by Definition 3.5, we know that if the distance between any two
particles i and j ever becomes ≥ ε along the E-dynamics, then their distance will remain ≥ ε for the rest of
time; therefore, if the distance between any two particles is > ε initially, then all these distances will remain
≥ ε forever, and any occasion where |xE           E                          E        E         E        E
                                         i (t) − xj (t)| = ε must satisfy (xi (t) − xj (t)) · (vi (t) − vj (t)) ≤ 0,
so the E-dynamics coincide with the O-dynamics, which proves (4).
   Next, (5) follows by the definition of (Λ, Γ)-truncated dynamics (Definition 3.7). By the requirements
(a) and (b) in Definition 3.7 (3), we know that: if counting an occasion where |xΛ,Γ                Λ,Γ

λ, then this occasion will not be counted as a collision by Definition 3.7. The result of (5) then follows.
   Finally we prove (6). For the O-dynamics this follows from [15]; in fact the upper bound is explicit

just notice that, by (1.13) we have
                                       (∂t + v · ∇x )(∇x n) = Q(n, ∇x n) + Q(∇x n, n),
the same result holds by repeating the proof above.                                                                         □
